Fundamentals / Mechanical engineering / Machine elements and contact mechanics

Machine elements and contact mechanics

Follow loads through shafts, fasteners, bearings and drives, then use fatigue, lubrication, contact and tolerance models to assess an assembly.

Chapters, problem sheets and the applied study in reading order. For a paper copy, use your browser’s print command. Open the worked solutions you want to include before printing.

Download course PDF with solutions

Advanced study across the subject

Each sequence connects the course to detailed chapters in neighbouring subjects. The complete course PDF includes their derivations, examples and exercises.

  1. Account for compliance, contact and failure

    Determine what the support actually imposes before sizing a shaft, flexure or connection. Check its static deformation, cyclic response and sensitivity to temperature.

  2. Actuation, bearings and measurement

    Follow the coupled force, motion and dissipation in a lubricating film, a compliant positioning system and a motion sensor.

How the course fits together

  1. Chapters 1–4

    Translate service loads into component stresses, then follow them through shafts, bolted joints and rolling contacts. The stiffness of the parts decides how a load is shared and where a local pressure develops.

  2. Chapters 5–8

    Gear geometry transfers torque, fits transmit pressure, springs store energy, and belts use friction to transmit tension. Distinguish prescribed displacement from prescribed force when reading a component rating.

  3. Chapters 9–12

    Brakes dissipate energy, fluid films carry loads through motion, and weld groups distribute loads in a joint. Finish by propagating variable dimensions and strengths into a probability of meeting the design requirement.

Chapter 1 · From machine duty to a design load

Translate a machine’s operating cycle into member forces, stress amplitudes and a life estimate for a repeated duty block.

From first principles

Convert a repeated force history into stress and accumulated damage

Begin by isolating the moving part and balancing its forces. For a mass \(m\) accelerated upwards at \(a\), an ideal vertical lifting member supplies \(F=m(g+a)\) if its own inertia is negligible. In a straight prismatic member of area \(A\) loaded along its centroid, the nominal axial stress is \(F/A\). Record the largest and smallest stress during one repeated operating cycle. Their mean and half-range are

\[\sigma_m=\frac{F_{\max}+F_{\min}}{2A},\]
\[\sigma_a=\frac{F_{\max}-F_{\min}}{2A}.\]

Fatigue life is empirical: tests on the appropriate detail relate a specified stress amplitude and mean stress to a characteristic failure count \(N_i\). Under the linear damage-summation approximation, \(n_i\) repetitions of that level consume a fraction \(n_i/N_i\) of life. For one recurring duty block,

\[D_{\rm block}=\sum_i\frac{n_i}{N_i},\]
\[B_{\rm predicted}=\frac1{D_{\rm block}} .\]

The second relation uses a nominal failure threshold of one. It requires the same load block to repeat and assumes damage fractions add regardless of sequence. Peak stress and cumulative fatigue damage answer different questions about that operating history.

Derivation and applications

Convert a variable-amplitude block to a damage-equivalent stress

Two repeated machine duties can have different peak stresses yet accumulate the same fatigue damage in a specified model. Consider a fixed detail, a common stress definition and constant mean-stress conditions. Use an experimentally fitted single-slope life relation \(N(S)=N_r(S_r/S)^m\), where \(S\) is alternating stress amplitude, \(m>0\) is the fitted slope and the reference point is \((S_r,N_r)\). Assume the same environment and material condition as the fit. Palmgren–Miner summation gives the predicted damage of one load block with \(n_i\) cycles at each amplitude \(S_i\).

\[D_b=\sum_i\frac{n_i}{N(S_i)}=\frac{1}{N_rS_r^m}\sum_i n_i S_i^m .\]

Define a constant amplitude \(S_{\rm eq}\) acting for the same total number of cycles \(n=\sum_i n_i\). Equate its predicted damage to the variable-amplitude damage. The reference point cancels from this comparison because both predictions use the same life curve.

\[n S_{\rm eq}^m=\sum_i n_i S_i^m,\]
\[S_{\rm eq}=\left(\sum_i\frac{n_i}{n}S_i^m\right)^{1/m}.\]

A rare high stress can dominate this power average. For two levels the proportion of total damage due to the higher one follows by dividing its contribution by their sum. This fraction is independent of how often the entire block is repeated.

\[f_H=\frac{n_H S_H^m}{n_H S_H^m+n_L S_L^m}.\]

Changing sequence, residual stress, mean stress or a transition between slopes can invalidate the reduction; the equality here is an exact statement about the stated damage model.

Worked example

Identify which cycles dominate a conveyor duty

A block contains 900 cycles at 40.0 MPa and 100 at 80.0 MPa. For \(m=4\), the equivalent amplitude is \(40[0.9+0.1(2^4)]^{1/4}=50.3\) MPa. The high cycles provide \(1.6/(0.9+1.6)=0.640\) of damage, though they occupy one tenth of the block. Simply taking the arithmetic mean would give 44.0 MPa and understate predicted damage.

Second application

Determine a permissible high-cycle fraction

Another duty must stay below a damage-equivalent amplitude of 60.0 MPa. Let the available levels be 50.0 and 75.0 MPa with slope \(m=3\). Solving \(60^3=(1-f)50^3+f75^3\) gives \(f=(60^3-50^3)/(75^3-50^3)=0.307\). At most about 30.7 percent of cycles can occupy the higher level under these assumptions. The result concerns cycle count; unequal shaft speeds would require converting operating durations first.

Follow the force through the machine

A machine specification may give lifting capacity in kilograms or motor power in kilowatts. Neither quantity by itself gives the tension in an individual link. Begin with the moving assembly and its connections. Isolate one body, mark every contact and decide which reactions the connection can transmit. A pin carries two force components in a planar model. A flexible cable carries tension along its length. A pair of guides may transmit a couple even though each individual contact carries only a normal force. These decisions determine which part experiences each load.

Consider a carriage raised by a vertical cable. Its cable attachment transfers tension through a flat link of net area \(A\). If upward is positive, the carriage balance reads \(F-mg=ma\), hence \(F=m(g+a)\). This also applies while the carriage is moving downwards and slowing down. Its upward acceleration increases tension. A speed record alone therefore conceals an important load case. Draw both the velocity and its slope before assigning signs. A cable solution with negative tension means that the cable would slacken and the assumed constraint has failed.

Separate a steady bias from an alternating load

Cut the link across its smallest section. With the load centred and bending negligible, integration of an approximately uniform axial stress gives \(F=\sigma A\). The stress at the two turning points defines the mean stress and alternating amplitude for that cycle. An amplitude is measured from the mean, so a recorded peak-to-peak range must be divided by two.

\[\sigma_{\max,\min}=F_{\max,\min}/A,\qquad \sigma_m=(\sigma_{\max}+\sigma_{\min})/2,\qquad \sigma_a=(\sigma_{\max}-\sigma_{\min})/2.\]

A part loaded from zero to a maximum tensile stress has equal positive mean and alternating components. A fully reversed load with the same peak tensile stress has zero mean and twice that alternating amplitude. To assess fatigue, a material curve must describe the applicable mean stress, surface condition, stress concentration and environment. For yielding, first compare the largest local stress with the yield strength. Those two calculations use different features of the same history.

A lift with repeated starts

A carriage and its payload have a combined mass of 800 kg. In each trip the drive accelerates upwards at 2.0 m/s² and later decelerates while travelling upwards at 3.0 m/s². The cable passes without mechanical advantage to a link with net section 80 mm². Gravity is 9.81 m/s². Assume the cable remains taut, and treat the measured accelerations as representing the load on the carriage.

The maximum tension is \(800(9.81+2.0)=9448\,\mathrm N\) and the minimum during the stated loaded cycle is \(800(9.81-3.0)=5448\,\mathrm N\). Dividing by area gives 118.1 and 68.1 MPa. Their mean is 93.1 MPa and their amplitude is 25.0 MPa. A stopped, suspended carriage still loads the link at 98.1 MPa. Unloading the carriage fully between trips would introduce another turning point. The original two-level description would no longer define every cycle experienced by the link.

Convert a repeated block into a fatigue estimate

Suppose tests at the applicable stress ratios give lives of \(8.0\times10^5\) cycles for ordinary loaded trips and \(1.2\times10^5\) for heavier trips. One representative block contains 200 ordinary and 30 heavy cycles. Palmgren–Miner averaging assigns the used fraction \(n_i/N_i\) to each level. If a block is repeated \(B\) times, its predicted accumulated damage is

\[D=B\sum_i n_i/N_i=B(200/800000+30/120000).\]

The conventional estimate \(D=1\) gives 2000 blocks. The smaller count of heavy cycles contributes half of the estimated damage. This calculation can help decide which trips to instrument. It assumes that sequence effects and interactions between levels can be neglected. Changing the order of the same cycles would leave this prediction unchanged. An overload that yields the link, changes its residual stress or damages a connection calls for evidence beyond this linear rule. Record the peak event separately when preparing the input for a component calculation.

Combine alternating stress with a tensile mean stress

A duty histogram can contain cycles with the same amplitude but different tensile mean stresses. Applying a fully reversed fatigue curve to every cycle ignores this distinction. A common preliminary engineering construction at a selected life is the modified Goodman line. Let \(S_f(N)\) be a component’s fully reversed fatigue strength at that life and \(S_u\) its tensile ultimate strength. Draw the line joining \((\sigma_m=0,\sigma_a=S_f)\) and \((\sigma_m=S_u,\sigma_a=0)\) on a mean–alternating stress diagram. Points on the line satisfy

\[\frac{\sigma_a}{S_f(N)}+\frac{\sigma_m}{S_u}=1,\qquad \sigma_{a,\rm eq}=\frac{\sigma_a}{1-\sigma_m/S_u}.\]

The second expression gives the fully reversed amplitude assigned to the measured cycle by this interpolation. This is an empirical mean-stress correction, rather than a derivation of material behaviour from elasticity. Use component or specimen data appropriate to surface, size, loading mode, environment and stress concentration. Compressive mean stresses and regions near yield require their own treatment. If a local cycle produces plastic strain, an elastic stress-life calculation can be inappropriate.

If all stresses scale in proportion to an external load multiplier \(n_L\), the intersection of that load ray with the Goodman line gives \(n_L=1/(\sigma_a/S_f+\sigma_m/S_u)\). A fixed installation preload is different: it contributes to the mean but will not scale with a later service-load multiplier. Adding preload to a stress diagram and multiplying the whole state by a single factor would then misstate the available service-load increase.

Compare a raised preload with an increased cycle range

Consider a smooth tensile link with illustrative life-\(10^6\)-cycle strength \(S_f=180\,\mathrm{MPa}\), ultimate strength \(S_u=600\,\mathrm{MPa}\) and yield strength 360 MPa. Its measured stress cycles from 40 to 200 MPa. Mean and amplitude are 120 and 80 MPa; the Goodman sum is \(80/180+120/600=0.644\). The model’s equivalent reversed amplitude is \(80/(1-120/600)=100\,\mathrm{MPa}\). The peak of 200 MPa is below yield. If the full cycle scales with service load, the fatigue-ray multiplier is 1.55, while first yield at the nominal peak gives \(360/200=1.80\).

Suppose instead that a 60 MPa assembly preload remains fixed and only the remainder scales. At the original operating point the service component then has a mean of 60 MPa and an amplitude of 80 MPa. Its fatigue boundary is \(n_L(80/180)+(60+60n_L)/600=1\), giving \(n_L=1.65\). The peak yield condition becomes \(60+140n_L=360\), or \(n_L=2.14\). Both multipliers differ from scaling everything because the preload has a different physical source. A further rise of fixed preload by 30 MPa at the original service range changes the equivalent reversed amplitude to \(80/(1-150/600)=106.7\,\mathrm{MPa}\). This comparison shows which load measurement must change if the aim is reducing fatigue demand rather than merely reducing the largest force in a test record.

The same diagram can represent a measured range with uncertainty. A sensor offset changes the reconstructed mean while leaving the alternating component unchanged. A gain calibration error generally changes both. Reporting only the highest gauge reading leaves these two uncertainty sources indistinguishable when estimating fatigue demand.

Allocate fluctuating and mean stress in a fatigue screen

Use a linear Goodman estimate \(\sigma_a/S_e+\sigma_m/S_u=1\) with \(S_u=600\) MPa. Also require the peak tensile stress \(\sigma_m+\sigma_a\) to remain below the 380 MPa yield scale. At each mean stress the smaller ordinate is the controlling screen.

Chapter 2 · Shaft reactions, twist and rotating bending

Trace power and belt forces into shaft reactions, then check the shaft section for yielding, twist and cyclic bending.

From first principles

Derive bending and torsional stress at a round shaft surface

A rotating shaft transmits torque through circumferential shear and can carry transverse loads through bending. Consider a slender, solid, circular shaft of diameter \(d\) under small elastic deformation. In pure bending, plane sections stay plane so longitudinal strain varies linearly with distance \(y\) from the neutral axis. In circular torsion, shear strain varies linearly with radius \(r\). Choosing stress gradients to reproduce the applied moment and torque gives

\[M=\int_A y\sigma\,dA,\]
\[\sigma=My/I,\]
\[T=\int_A r\tau\,dA,\]
\[\tau=Tr/J .\]

Here \(I=\int_A y^2dA=\pi d^4/64\) and \(J=\int_A r^2dA=\pi d^4/32\). Taking the surface distance as \(d/2\) gives the nominal maximum components.

\[|\sigma_b|_{\max}=\frac{32|M|}{\pi d^3},\]
\[|\tau_t|_{\max}=\frac{16|T|}{\pi d^3}.\]

Torque can be inferred from mechanical power using \(T=P/\omega\), while bending moment requires transverse force and support equilibrium. A stationary transverse load produces alternating bending at a material point as the shaft rotates. The formulas assume a smooth section; shoulders and keyways alter local stress.

Derivation and applications

Distribute a fixed-end shaft torque by twist compatibility

A shaft supported against rotation at both ends has an extra reaction torque that cannot be found from overall moment equilibrium alone. Consider two coaxial circular segments meeting at a collar. Apply a static torque \(T\) at the collar. The left and right outer ends are held fixed. Assume linear elastic shear, small twist, fully bonded interfaces and torque transfer without slip. Segment \(j\) has length \(L_j\), shear modulus \(G_j\) and polar second moment \(J_j\). Common collar angle \(\theta\) twists each segment through the same magnitude.

\[\theta=\frac{T_L L_L}{G_LJ_L}=\frac{T_RL_R}{G_RJ_R},\]
\[T_L+T_R=T .\]

The symbols \(T_L,T_R\) are positive magnitudes of the moments resisting the applied torque. Define torsional stiffness \(k_j=G_jJ_j/L_j\) and substitute into equilibrium. The two segments behave as parallel torsional springs when observed from the collar.

\[\theta=\frac{T}{k_L+k_R},\]
\[T_L=\frac{k_L}{k_L+k_R}T,\]
\[T_R=\frac{k_R}{k_L+k_R}T .\]

For a solid round shaft \(J_j=\pi d_j^4/32\). Local surface shear is proportional to torque times outer radius divided by \(J_j\). The increase of torque carried by a stiffer segment therefore does not translate directly into the same factor of stress.

\[\tau_{j,\max}=\frac{T_jd_j}{2J_j}=\frac{16T_j}{\pi d_j^3}.\]

If one support becomes torsionally soft, its torque approaches zero and the stiffer side carries nearly all of \(T\). The fixed-end assumption is especially important in using rotation measurements to identify a support reaction.

Worked example

Split torque across two lengths of shaft

Two segments have the same 20.0 mm diameter and shear modulus 80.0 GPa, with lengths 0.200 and 0.400 m. The shorter segment is twice as stiff. A 90.0 N m collar torque splits into 60.0 and 30.0 N m. The shorter segment reaches \(16(60)/[\pi(0.0200)^3]=38.2\) MPa surface shear. Its twist is \(60(0.200)/(80.0\times10^9 J)=0.00955\) rad, shared by the collar.

Second application

Select a diameter for equal torque share

Another shaft uses identical materials, with the right segment twice as long as the left. To carry equal torques require \(d_R^4/L_R=d_L^4/L_L\). If \(d_L=25.0\) mm, \(d_R=25.0(2)^{1/4}=29.7\) mm. Each carries half the applied torque, but the right surface stress is only \((25.0/29.7)^3=0.595\) of the left because its larger diameter provides greater torsional section modulus.

Rotation determines the torque, contacts determine the bending

A shaft transmits a given power through torque at a specified speed. Mechanical work in a small rotation is \(dW=T\,d\theta\), so power is \(T\omega\), with angular speed in radians per second. Belt tensions act at opposite sides of a pulley. Their difference gives the net driving torque. For two nearly parallel belt spans pulling in the same transverse direction their sum loads the shaft sideways. A belt with greater pretension can increase bearing loads although the transmitted power remains constant.

\[\omega=2\pi n/60,\qquad T=P/\omega=(F_1-F_2)r,\qquad F_\perp\simeq F_1+F_2 .\]

Choose bearing centres as ideal simple supports and identify where each torque enters or leaves the shaft. A section between an input coupling and an output pulley carries the transmitted torque. Beyond the output pulley it may carry transverse shear while carrying virtually zero torque. Mark the bending and torque diagrams separately so the combination at a critical cross-section represents loads that actually coexist there.

Determine the stress components from section geometry

For a solid circular shaft the bending second moment is \(I=\pi d^4/64\), and its polar moment is \(J=2I\). The elastic bending stress at the outer fibre is \(M(d/2)/I\); the torsional shear stress is \(T(d/2)/J\). Under proportional elastic loading, the plane-stress von Mises expression then gives a static yield check for a ductile material.

\[\sigma_b=32M/(\pi d^3),\qquad \tau_t=16T/(\pi d^3),\qquad \sigma_v=\sqrt{\sigma_b^2+3\tau_t^2}.\]

The cubic diameter dependence makes a shoulder important even before its local stress concentration is considered. These equations describe a smooth prismatic section and nominal stresses. At a fillet, the maximum stresses may be estimated from geometric concentration factors appropriate to bending and torsion. For cyclic design a fatigue concentration factor may differ from the elastic geometric factor because sensitivity to the notch depends on material and radius.

Size the loading at the pulley seat

A 120 mm radius pulley sits at the middle of a 0.40 m span and draws 6.0 kW at 600 rpm. The two belt forces are parallel and their sum is 1800 N. The driving coupling lies immediately outside the left bearing. The shaft is 32 mm in diameter. From \(\omega=62.83\,\mathrm{rad/s}\), the transmitted torque is 95.5 N m and the tension difference is \(95.5/0.120=795.8\,\mathrm N\). Combining difference and sum gives 1298 and 502 N.

Each bearing carries 900 N. Taking moments about a cut just to the left of the pulley gives \(M=900(0.20)=180\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). There the shaft still transmits full torque. Substitution gives 56.0 MPa bending and 14.8 MPa torsional shear. Thus the nominal von Mises stress is 61.6 MPa, giving a nominal yield safety factor of 4.06 for steel with 250 MPa yield strength. This calculation has not yet accounted for a keyway or the pulley seat fillet.

Follow one material point through a revolution

A fixed vertical belt pull produces a steady bending moment in the laboratory. Mark a point on the rotating shaft surface. It passes through the tensile and compressive bending fibres once each per turn, so that its bending stress is fully reversed with amplitude 56.0 MPa. Steady driving torque supplies mean shear. A fatigue test at zero mean axial load describes only one part of this combined loading history. A shaft that spends a long time at constant speed can accumulate many stress cycles while the torque meter shows a level trace.

The twist angle is a further service requirement. Integrate \(d\theta/dx=T/(GJ)\) along the torque-carrying length. With \(G=80\,\mathrm{GPa}\), \(J=1.029\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm{m^4}\), and a 0.20 m torque-carrying span, the relative twist is 0.00232 rad, or 0.133°. Bending stress varies as the inverse cube of diameter and twist at fixed torque as the inverse fourth power. A diameter selected from a strength limit can still give an unacceptable positioning error at the driven component.

Use the static deflection to estimate a shaft critical speed

The shaft diameter required to transmit torque need not satisfy the dynamic constraints of a rotating machine. For a first screening, model a relatively light simply supported shaft carrying a heavy disc at midspan. Treat the disc as a concentrated translating mass and neglect shaft mass, gyroscopic coupling, bearing flexibility and damping at first. Under a static transverse force \(F\), bending theory gives midpoint deflection \(\delta=FL^3/(48EI)\), hence lateral stiffness \(k_s=48EI/L^3\). With gravity loading only from the disc, \(\delta_g=mg/k_s\). The ideal bending natural frequency then follows without explicitly measuring disc mass:

\[\omega_n=\sqrt{k_s/m}=\sqrt{g/\delta_g}.\]

A small rotating mass eccentricity \(e\) produces lateral forcing \(me\Omega^2\) synchronous with speed. The undamped critical speed occurs when \(\Omega\) equals \(\omega_n\). Gravity sag itself is a static offset in vertical operation of this horizontal shaft; its magnitude is useful because it reveals the ratio \(m/k_s\). For a shaft with distributed mass or several mounted elements, one measured sag at a disc need not represent the relevant modal inertia. A compliant test stand also contributes to the measured deflection and can lower the assembled critical speed.

Adding simple viscous damping gives synchronous orbit-amplitude ratio \(X/e=r^2/\sqrt{(1-r^2)^2+(2\zeta r)^2}\), \(r=\Omega/\omega_n\), for the ideal isotropic single-disc model. It approaches unity far above critical speed but can be much larger near it.

Check a shaft after increasing disc mass

A machine shaft carrying a balanced test disc deflects 0.40 mm at its centre when the disc’s weight is applied. The fixture was tared for its own sag. The estimated natural frequency is \(\sqrt{9.81/0.00040}=156.6\,\mathrm{rad/s}\), corresponding to 1496 rpm. A production target of 1200 rpm gives \(r=0.802\). With small residual eccentricity \(e=0.050\,\mathrm{mm}\) and damping ratio 0.04, the predicted orbit amplitude is \(0.050(0.802)^2/\sqrt{(1-0.802^2)^2+(0.08\times0.802)^2}=0.089\,\mathrm{mm}\).

A new disc doubles the dominant mass at the same shaft stiffness. Sag doubles to 0.80 mm and the estimated critical speed falls to 1057 rpm. The machine must now pass through that speed during startup and operates above it, despite the torque capability of the shaft being essentially unchanged. To restore its original critical speed under the doubled mass while retaining the span and material, stiffness must double. For a smooth solid circular shaft \(I\propto d^4\), so \(d_{\rm new}/d_{\rm old}=2^{1/4}=1.189\). A 30 mm shaft would need about 35.7 mm under this model. Restoring the natural frequency alone does not fix a bearing or coupling alignment issue, and a run-up through resonance requires transient analysis if the dwell time is significant relative to the decay time.

The static stiffness estimate assumes the midpoint force and the displacement gauge describe the same work-conjugate location and direction. An overhung disc or a gauge near a support can give a very different apparent stiffness. Recording the shaft layout alongside the sag value keeps the screening calculation reproducible when the assembly changes.

Why a single universal joint produces a twice-per-revolution speed fluctuation

A Hooke joint lets two intersecting shafts transmit rotation at a fixed misalignment angle \(\beta\). Its cross carries two perpendicular trunnions. Choose the input axis as \(z\), tilt the output axis about \(x\), and measure input rotation \(\theta\) and output rotation \(\phi\) from aligned angular references. A unit vector along the first trunnion is \((\cos\theta,\sin\theta,0)\). The second rotates in the tilted output plane and can be written \((-\sin\phi,\cos\beta\cos\phi,\sin\beta\cos\phi)\). Their dot product vanishes, giving \(\tan\phi=\cos\beta\tan\theta\). Follow a continuous angular branch over a full turn.

Differentiate this constraint before averaging. Using the tangent relation to remove \(\phi\) from the derivative gives

\[\frac{\omega_\mathrm{out}}{\omega_\mathrm{in}}=\frac{d\phi}{d\theta}=\frac{\cos\beta}{1-\sin^2\beta\sin^2\theta}.\]

The extremes are \(\cos\beta\) and \(1/\cos\beta\). Both shafts complete one revolution in the same time, yet the instantaneous ratio changes twice per revolution. For small \(\beta\) in radians it is \(1-(\beta^2/2)\cos2\theta+O(\beta^4)\). This identifies a second-order dependence on small misalignment and a forcing frequency twice the shaft frequency.

A test rig drives a single joint at a constant 600 rpm with \(\beta=20^\circ\). The driven shaft ranges from \(600\cos20^\circ=563.8\,\mathrm{rpm}\) to \(600/\cos20^\circ=638.5\,\mathrm{rpm}\). Its speed ripple repeats at \(2(600/60)=20.0\,\mathrm{Hz}\). A large downstream inertia will therefore demand an alternating accelerating torque even if the external load is steady. A double-joint arrangement can cancel the kinematic ripple at the final shaft: with parallel outer shafts, equal joint angles and correctly phased forks, the second joint implements the inverse angular mapping of the first. The intermediate shaft still fluctuates, so bearing and torsional loads within the assembly merit separate treatment.

Practise on sheet 1 · Set a joint-angle limit from the driven-shaft speed tolerance. Further reading · Jönköping University · Applied Mechanics · §10.20 Shaft couplings, “The Hooke joint” and “Two Hooke joints”.

Will shaft strength or angular stiffness set the diameter?

A solid shaft transmits 120 N·m over 0.80 m with shear modulus 80 GPa. Limit nominal shear to 60 MPa and end rotation to one degree. Compare \(\tau=TR/J\) and \(\phi=TL/(GJ)\) only after dividing each quantity by its own allowable.

Problem sheet 1 · Duty blocks and shaft sections

Resolve a duty record into stresses and compare shaft bending, torsion and twist using the actual load paths.

An accelerating counterweight

A 550 kg counterweight hangs from a single vertical link of area 60 mm². Its upward acceleration ranges from −1.8 to +2.2 m/s² during each loaded cycle. Find the force extremes and the stress mean and amplitude. During commissioning the same link is fully unloaded once per trip instead. Which stress quantity changes for a simple zero-to-peak cycle?

Resolve the accelerations

The link force is \(m(g+a)\), giving 4406 and 6606 N for the stated endpoints. The corresponding stresses are 73.43 and 110.09 MPa. The mean is 91.76 MPa and the amplitude 18.33 MPa. For a single excursion from zero to the same peak, mean and amplitude are both 55.05 MPa. The peak is unchanged although the amplitude triples. Multiple turning points in an actual trip require separate cycle counting.

Use a measured duty block

A pump link sees 800 cycles per shift at a stress level with test life 2.0 million cycles, and 60 cycles at a level with test life 150,000 cycles. Find the expected shift count at unit damage under linear accumulation. What is the effect of halving the number of heavy events only?

Weight each level by its tested life

The two contributions are \(800/2000000=0.0004\) and \(60/150000=0.0004\) per shift. Their sum gives 1250 shifts. With only 30 heavy events per shift, the total becomes 0.0006 and the estimate rises to 1667 shifts. The predicted improvement is one third, despite the heavy-event count falling by half.

Bending at an off-centre pulley

A smooth 30 mm shaft spans 0.45 m between bearings. A pulley 0.15 m from the left support imposes a 1200 N downward load. The shaft receives 60 N m torque at its left end and delivers it at the pulley. Find both reactions, the nominal stresses just left of the pulley and the twist between left bearing and pulley for \(G=80\) GPa.

Follow the section cut

Moment balance about the left gives \(R_B=1200(0.15)/0.45=400\,\mathrm N\), hence \(R_A=800\,\mathrm N\) and \(M=120\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). Using millimetres and N mm, \(\sigma_b=32(120000)/(\pi30^3)=45.3\,\mathrm{MPa}\) and \(\tau=16(60000)/(\pi30^3)=11.3\,\mathrm{MPa}\). With \(J=\pi(0.030)^4/32\), \(\theta=60(0.15)/(80\times10^9J)=0.00141\,\mathrm{rad}\), or 0.081°. The section to the right carries bending but no drive torque.

Set a joint-angle limit from the driven-shaft speed tolerance

A single joint drives a lightly loaded inspection spindle at a mean 900 rpm. Its instantaneous speed may be at most 2.0% above and no more than 2.0% below the mean. Determine the maximum shaft angle, the minimum speed at that angle and the frequency of the speed fluctuation. Would increasing the input speed remove the fractional error?

Show the worked solution

The upper bound needs \(1/\cos\beta\le1.02\), hence \(\cos\beta\ge1/1.02=0.980392\). The lower bound only requires \(\cos\beta\ge0.98\), so the upper bound controls: \(\beta_\mathrm{max}=\arccos(1/1.02)=11.36^\circ\). At this angle the minimum is \(900/1.02=882.35\,\mathrm{rpm}\) and the maximum is \(918.0\,\mathrm{rpm}\). The fluctuation frequency is \(2(900/60)=30.0\,\mathrm{Hz}\). Increasing input rpm raises both the fluctuation frequency and its absolute amplitude in rpm at fixed angle. The fractional speed ratio follows joint geometry.

Revisit chapter 2 · Why a single universal joint produces a twice-per-revolution speed fluctuation.

Chapter 3 · Preload, joint stiffness and separation

Compute how a tensile service load changes bolt force and clamp force, and determine when a closed-joint calculation ceases to apply.

From first principles

Derive bolt and clamp increments from compatible deformation

A tightened bolt stretches while the clamped members compress. Before external loading their force magnitudes equal the preload \(F_i\). Apply a separating load \(P\) and assume both components respond elastically and the interfaces stay closed. A small opening displacement \(\delta\) stretches the bolt additionally and relieves the members by the same displacement. With stiffnesses \(k_b,k_m\) the increments are

\[\Delta F_b=k_b\delta,\]
\[-\Delta F_m=k_m\delta,\]
\[P=\Delta F_b-\Delta F_m=(k_b+k_m)\delta .\]

Here member force \(F_m\) is positive in compression. Eliminate the displacement to find both service forces and the fraction \(C\) of external load reaching the bolt.

\[C=\frac{k_b}{k_b+k_m},\]
\[F_b=F_i+CP,\]
\[F_m=F_i-(1-C)P .\]

Setting \(F_m=0\) predicts incipient separation at \(P=F_i/(1-C)\). Once the interface can no longer supply compressive reaction, the closed-joint stiffness partition ceases to describe the load path. The derivation explains why service bolt tension grows by only a fraction of applied load before separation.

Derivation and applications

Predict preload loss caused by settlement of a bolted joint

A tightened joint may lose clamp load before it sees any external service force. Model the bolt by axial stiffness \(k_b\) and the compressed members by stiffness \(k_m\). Assume elastic incremental behaviour, closed contact, fixed temperature and a nut that does not rotate after tightening. Initial preload \(F_i\) stretches the bolt and compresses the members. A small permanent settlement \(s\) at asperities or interfaces consumes part of the initial imposed interference.

\[\Delta_{\rm installed}=F_i\left(\frac1{k_b}+\frac1{k_m}\right),\]
\[\Delta_{\rm remaining}=\Delta_{\rm installed}-s .\]

The same preload passes through the bolt and compressed stack before an external tensile load is applied. Recovering their combined elastic displacement therefore reduces the preload according to their series stiffness. The result applies as long as positive clamp load remains.

\[F_r=F_i-k_{\rm eq}s,\]
\[k_{\rm eq}=\left(\frac1{k_b}+\frac1{k_m}\right)^{-1}.\]

Now apply an external separating force \(P\). In a closed joint the bolt takes fraction \(C=k_b/(k_b+k_m)\) of that force and the clamp decreases by \((1-C)P\). Setting the residual clamp to zero gives the revised separation threshold.

\[F_{\rm clamp}=F_r-(1-C)P,\]
\[P_{\rm sep}=\frac{F_i-k_{\rm eq}s}{1-C}.\]

Settlement is a displacement, so a more compliant bolt can reduce the preload lost per micrometre. Other design constraints, including stiffness, load introduction and fatigue, remain relevant when altering bolt geometry.

Worked example

Audit a joint after a measured bedding-in displacement

Let \(k_b=200\) kN/mm, \(k_m=800\) kN/mm and \(F_i=24.0\) kN. The series stiffness is 160 kN/mm. A settlement of 15.0 µm reduces preload by \(160(0.0150)=2.40\) kN, leaving 21.6 kN. Since \(C=0.200\), the new separation load is 27.0 kN. Before settlement it would have been 30.0 kN.

Second application

Find an allowable settlement from a service condition

A separate joint has \(k_b=120\) kN/mm, \(k_m=360\) kN/mm and initial preload 18.0 kN. At maximum separating force 12.0 kN it must retain 5.00 kN clamp. The required residual preload is \(5.00+0.750(12.0)=14.0\) kN. The allowable preload loss is 4.00 kN and \(k_{\rm eq}=90.0\) kN/mm, yielding \(s_{\max}=0.0444\) mm. The stated requirement is more restrictive than merely keeping contact closed.

Establish the state before the external load arrives

A tensioned bolt in an assembled flange has already elongated. The material clamped between head and nut has already shortened. In the ideal concentric joint these initial forces have equal magnitude \(F_i\), as a free-body diagram of either flange shows. Tightening stores elastic energy in both parts. Calling a joint unloaded usually means that the external service load is zero. The thread root can still carry a significant tensile stress.

Represent the effective bolt grip by a tensile spring \(k_b\) and the compressed members by a spring \(k_m\). For a uniform segment the axial stiffness is \(EA/L\); threaded and plain segments in series have added compliances. The member stiffness depends on the path through which compressive force spreads from the head and washer. A thin flexible flange may also bend. For this derivation consider one bolt, an axial load introduced at the outside faces and no eccentricity or prying. Let clamp force denote a positive magnitude in compression.

Apply compatibility to the load increment

An external separating force \(P\) allows the bolt to extend a little while the clamped members recover some of their initial compression. As long as the faces remain in contact, the extension and recovery have the same magnitude \(x\). The bolt-force increase is \(k_bx\) and the clamp-force decrease is \(k_mx\). Force equilibrium across the joint gives \(P=F_b-F_m=(k_b+k_m)x\). Eliminate \(x\) and introduce the load fraction \(C\).

\[C=\frac{k_b}{k_b+k_m},\quad F_b=F_i+CP,\quad F_m=F_i-(1-C)P.\]

The slopes of bolt force and clamp-force magnitude have opposite signs. The joint opens in this ideal model when \(F_m\) reaches zero, at \(P_{\rm sep}=F_i/(1-C)\). Continuing the same line beyond that point would predict tension between unbonded faces. Once open, the bolt must carry the full external tensile force and the load slope changes to unity. Both expressions give the same bolt force at the instant of separation.

Check a service cycle and a separate overload

Take \(k_b=180\,\mathrm{kN/mm}\), \(k_m=720\,\mathrm{kN/mm}\), \(F_i=25\,\mathrm{kN}\) and bolt tensile stress area \(A_t=58.0\,\mathrm{mm^2}\). A pressure pulsation creates a separating force cycling between 4 and 16 kN. Here \(C=0.20\), hence bolt force ranges from 25.8 to 28.2 kN. Clamp force falls from 21.8 to 12.2 kN and remains positive. The bolt’s alternating tensile stress is the force range divided by twice its tensile stress area.

\[\sigma_a=(28.2-25.8)10^3/(2\times58.0)=20.7\,\mathrm{MPa}.\]

At the maximum ordinary load the calculated tensile stress is 486 MPa. If the specified proof stress is 600 MPa, the axial proof-load benchmark is \(600(58.0)=34.8\,\mathrm{kN}\). The estimated separation threshold is \(25/0.8=31.25\,\mathrm{kN}\). A singular tensile overload of 34 kN opens the interface and brings the bolt to 34 kN, just below the supplied axial proof benchmark. It can nevertheless break the seal at the flange face. Different requirements can control the same event.

Use attainable preload, including its scatter

Increasing preload by 3 kN raises both initial forces and delays separation by \(3/(1-C)=3.75\,\mathrm{kN}\). It also raises the peak bolt stress by \(3000/58=51.7\,\mathrm{MPa}\) throughout the closed regime. Reducing bolt stiffness relative to the members can lower the alternating force. For example a longer effective grip can increase bolt compliance. Recheck the residual clamp and separation conditions whenever changing stiffness.

If tightening produces a range of preloads, assess separation with the low end and proof stress with the high end. An assembly tolerance of ±3 kN around the nominal value gives a minimum separation threshold of \(22/0.8=27.5\,\mathrm{kN}\). The largest ordinary bolt force would be \(28+0.2(16)=31.2\,\mathrm{kN}\). The preload scatter enters these checks even though the external service force is perfectly known. In a real flange, surface settling, differential thermal expansion and off-axis loading can change the available clamp force after assembly.

Resolve tightening torque into thread lift and friction

Joint preload depends on the tightening method as well as bolt and flange stiffness. For an elementary torque estimate, unwrap one turn of the thread at pitch diameter \(d_2\). Its lead \(l\) over the circumferential distance \(\pi d_2\) defines helix angle \(\lambda=\arctan[l/(\pi d_2)]\). For a square thread lifting axial load \(F_i\), resolving an applied tangential force and Coulomb friction on the incline gives \(F_{\rm tan}=F_i\tan(\lambda+\phi)\), where \(\tan\phi=\mu_t\). For a symmetric V-thread of half-angle \(\alpha\), a common small-helix approximation increases the effective friction to \(\mu_t/\cos\alpha\) because the flanks carry a larger normal force.

Under the head or nut, sliding friction at effective radius \(r_b\) adds torque \(\mu_b F_i r_b\). A simple total tightening estimate is

\[T\simeq F_i[(d_2/2)\tan(\lambda+\arctan(\mu_t/\cos\alpha))+\mu_b r_b].\]

The first term includes ideal screw lift and thread friction; the second is bearing-face friction. In the limit of zero friction the thread term reduces to \(F_i l/(2\pi)\), as verified by equating work over one revolution to axial load times lead. This lower bound also shows why most measured tightening torque can disappear into friction rather than useful bolt elongation. Effective bearing radius depends on contact geometry and pressure distribution, and the coefficients may differ between production lots.

Determine preload sensitivity to lubrication at fixed torque

Use an illustrative single-start thread with \(d_2=9.0\) mm, \(l=1.50\) mm, flank half-angle 30° and effective bearing radius 7.0 mm. The helix angle is \(\arctan[1.5/(\pi9)]=3.04^\circ\). With \(\mu_t=\mu_b=0.15\), the effective thread friction angle is 9.83°. The thread torque arm is \(4.5\tan(12.87^\circ)=1.03\,\mathrm{mm}\), and bearing friction contributes another 1.05 mm. A tightening torque of 40 N m therefore suggests \(F_i=40000/(1.03+1.05)=19.2\,\mathrm{kN}\). The frictionless lift portion would be only \(19.2(1.50)/(2\pi)=4.59\,\mathrm{N\,m}\).

If lubrication reduces both coefficients to 0.08 at the same applied torque, the effective thread friction angle becomes 5.28°. The torque arms are now \(4.5\tan(8.32^\circ)=0.658\) mm and 0.560 mm. Implied preload rises to \(40000/1.218=32.8\,\mathrm{kN}\). A tensile area of 58 mm² would then carry about 566 MPa before the service force arrived, compared with 331 MPa originally. An old torque setting may therefore raise preload substantially after a change of coating or lubricant. A direct elongation measurement can help distinguish this friction change from loss of clamp due to settling, although the effective bolt grip and modulus must also be known.

The axial stretch provides an independent expected signal. Suppose the effective uniform tensile length is 50 mm with \(E=210\,\mathrm{GPa}\) and area 58 mm². The two preload estimates imply elastic elongations \(F_iL/(EA)=0.079\) and 0.135 mm. An instrument resolving about 0.01 mm could distinguish the two states in this example if its gauge length includes the correctly modelled bolt segments. Thread and shank regions of different areas contribute as elements in series. A clamp-force reading taken after surfaces have settled can also differ from a tightening-time elongation record without a change in friction coefficient.

Chapter 4 · Bearing arrangements and rating life

Select the reactions carried by each bearing and convert a changing bearing load into an equivalent load and a basic rating life.

From first principles

Derive an equivalent bearing load from damage per revolution

A bearing's basic dynamic rating \(C\) calibrates an empirical relationship between constant equivalent load \(P\) and 90 percent survival life. In the usual power-law model, \(p\) is the bearing-type exponent and \(L_{10}\) is measured in revolutions. At constant shaft speed \(n\) in revolutions per minute, division by revolutions per hour converts to operating hours.

\[L_{10}=10^6(C/P)^p,\]
\[L_{10h}=\frac{10^6}{60n}(C/P)^p .\]

For variable duty let segment \(j\) cover \(N_j\) revolutions at load \(P_j\). Assuming damage fractions per revolution add linearly, define the constant load \(P_{\rm eq}\) producing the same predicted damage over \(N=\sum_jN_j\) revolutions. Substitution of the rating law cancels its common factors.

\[\frac{N}{L_{10}(P_{\rm eq})}=\sum_j\frac{N_j}{L_{10}(P_j)},\]
\[P_{\rm eq}=\left(\sum_j\frac{N_j}{N}P_j^p\right)^{1/p}.\]

The load fractions must be weighted by revolutions. Equal durations at different speeds produce different numbers of contact cycles. At standstill or very low speed, permanent indentation can control an overload, requiring a separate static assessment.

Derivation and applications

Convert two bearing life distributions into a shaft survival curve

A shaft supported by two bearings generally requires both to work until the next service interval. Suppose life at a fixed speed and load follows a two-parameter Weibull distribution with shape \(\beta\) common to both bearings. Let \(L_j\) denote the life at which bearing \(j\) has survival 0.90. Assume independent fatigue failures and no wear-out mechanism shared by the pair. Write the individual survival curve with a constant \(a_j\) to be chosen from the rated survival condition.

\[S_j(t)=\exp(-a_jt^\beta),\]
\[a_j=\frac{-\ln0.90}{L_j^\beta}.\]

The shaft survives only if both bearings survive. Independence turns that event into a product of probabilities and hence a sum of the exponents. This sum has the same Weibull shape, allowing the shaft's own 90 percent survival life to be identified.

\[S_{\rm shaft}(t)=\exp[-(a_1+a_2)t^\beta],\]
\[L_{\rm shaft}=(L_1^{-\beta}+L_2^{-\beta})^{-1/\beta}.\]

For equal bearings this becomes \(L_{\rm shaft}=L\,2^{-1/\beta}\). To evaluate any mission time one can use the equivalent pair life in the same survival expression. Alternatively, solving for mission time at a requested survival probability \(S_*\) gives

\[t_* =L_{\rm shaft}\left(\frac{\ln S_*}{\ln0.90}\right)^{1/\beta}.\]

A rating life is a population quantile. The distribution shape used here is an additional modelling input, and contamination affecting both bearings would undermine independence even if the individual life curves remained suitable.

Worked example

Size identical bearings for a service interval

With \(\beta=2.00\) and individual \(L=20{,}000\) hours, the pair has \(L_{\rm shaft}=20{,}000/\sqrt2=14{,}142\) hours. At 20,000 hours each bearing survives with probability 0.90, giving pair survival \(0.90^2=0.810\). If the shaft itself must reach 20,000 hours at 90 percent survival, each individual life must instead be at least 28,284 hours under these assumptions.

Second application

Combine two unequal ratings

Another shaft uses bearings with 90 percent lives of 12,000 and 24,000 hours, again with \(\beta=2.00\). Pair life is \(12{,}000/\sqrt{1+1/4}=10{,}733\) hours. At a 6000 hour mission the pair survival is \(0.90^{(6000/12000)^2+(6000/24000)^2}=0.968\). The shorter-life bearing contributes four times as much to the cumulative failure exponent as the longer-life bearing.

Start from this chapter. Recover the Hertz exponents for load and contact radius under an imposed approach. Halve the approach, then compare both ratios with the finite-block pressure field.

OneSimulation laboratory · Test the contact law under imposed approach

Constrain motion while allowing expansion

A radial bearing supports the shaft perpendicular to its axis. Some bearing types can also transmit an axial reaction in one or both directions. A common two-bearing shaft arrangement uses one locating bearing or bearing pair to establish axial position. A second support permits axial expansion while still providing radial support. Depending on construction, that relative movement occurs within the bearing or between a ring and its seat. Drawing a free-body diagram with two fully fixed axial supports can hide an unwanted thermal force.

For example, a 0.60 m steel shaft with thermal expansion coefficient \(12\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}\) lengthens by 0.288 mm when it warms by 40 K relative to a housing of unchanged length. If the support arrangement can accommodate that travel, it need not create a significant thermal axial reaction. A locating pair set with insufficient operating clearance can behave very differently. First identify radial reactions in each transverse plane and how the axial thrust returns to the housing. Only then assign loads to individual bearings.

Express a survival criterion in revolutions

A loaded rolling contact subjects the ring material to repeated subsurface stresses. The basic dynamic load rating \(C\) is associated with a basic rating life of one million revolutions at a constant equivalent dynamic load equal to \(C\). The basic rating life \(L_{10}\) describes a population under the rating assumptions. Ninety percent are expected to reach or exceed this life with respect to rolling-contact fatigue. For ball bearings use exponent \(p=3\); for roller bearings use \(p=10/3\). The rating model is empirical.

\[L_{10}=(C/P)^p \ \text{million revolutions},\qquad L_{10h}=\frac{10^6}{60n}(C/P)^p .\]

Here \(P\) is the equivalent dynamic bearing load and \(n\) the speed in revolutions per minute. A purely radial load on a suitable radial ball bearing can be used directly. When radial and axial components both act, obtain the appropriate equivalent-load coefficients and applicability thresholds for that bearing. Their directions relative to the shaft matter. The hypotenuse of axial and radial loads generally fails to represent the internal rolling contact load distribution.

Weight changing loads by revolutions

Suppose interval \(i\) occupies fraction \(f_i\) of the revolutions. Under the usual linear accumulation approximation, the life fraction consumed per revolution scales with \(P_i^p/C^p\). Equating the weighted consumption to that of a constant load gives

\[P_{\rm eq}^p=\sum_i f_iP_i^p,\qquad f_i=\frac{n_it_i}{\sum_jn_jt_j}.\]

Consider a ball bearing rated at \(C=18\,\mathrm{kN}\). It operates for 30 minutes at 1000 rpm under 2 kN of pure radial load, followed by 10 minutes at 1500 rpm under 4 kN. The intervals contain 30,000 and 15,000 revolutions. Thus \(P_{\rm eq}^3=(2/3)2^3+(1/3)4^3=26.667\,\mathrm{kN^3}\), giving 2.988 kN. The rating life is \(18^3/26.667=218.7\) million revolutions. The cycle mean speed is \(45000/40=1125\) rpm, giving about 3240 operating hours. The heavier interval contributes four fifths of the calculated damage even though it occupies only one quarter of the operating time.

Treat slow overloads as a separate question

While the shaft is stationary there are no accumulated revolutions in the preceding fatigue sum. A large stationary force can still leave permanent indentations at the rolling contacts. A static check compares the bearing-specific static equivalent load \(P_0\) with its basic static rating \(C_0\). For an example catalogue entry \(C_0=10\,\mathrm{kN}\) and a stationary equivalent load of 6 kN, the ratio \(s_0=C_0/P_0=1.67\). Compare this value with the application requirement, taking account of shock and required smoothness.

A selected rolling bearing also requires sufficient lubrication and compatible fits and operating clearances. The basic life calculation quantifies one fatigue mechanism. It is especially useful for comparing changes to a known duty cycle because of the strongly nonlinear dependence on load. When a measured bearing fails well before its estimated rating life, inspect mounting marks, lubrication and contamination evidence before changing the equivalent load to fit the failure time.

The manufacturer explains the bearing ratings and exponents in its SKF rolling bearing catalogue.

Recover the size of a contact from its pressure field

A catalogue load rating describes an entire bearing. To estimate the local pressure beneath one rolling element, specify the load on that contact. Start with two smooth isotropic elastic spheres in frictionless normal contact. Their radii \(R_1,R_2\) define \(1/R^*=1/R_1+1/R_2\) for external contact. A plane has zero curvature. The half-space compliances add, giving \(1/E^*=(1-\nu_1^2)/E_1+(1-\nu_2^2)/E_2\). A conforming raceway generally has two different principal curvatures, which would require an elliptical contact calculation.

The Hertz elastic solution gives an axisymmetric pressure \(p(r)=p_0\sqrt{1-r^2/a^2}\) within a contact radius \(a\), with \(p_0=2E^*a/(\pi R^*)\). These two statements use the half-space solution. The normal force follows by integrating pressure over rings of area \(2\pi r\,dr\). The integral of \(r\sqrt{1-r^2/a^2}\) from zero to \(a\) is \(a^2/3\). Substituting the peak pressure then gives

\[F=\tfrac{2}{3}\pi a^2p_0=\frac{4E^*a^3}{3R^*},\qquad a=\left(\frac{3FR^*}{4E^*}\right)^{1/3},\qquad p_0=\frac{3F}{2\pi a^2}.\]

For a steel sphere of radius 10 mm pressing on a steel flat with 25 N, \(E=210\) GPa and \(\nu=0.30\) for each body give \(E^*=115.4\) GPa. The radius is \(0.118\) mm, the centre pressure \(0.864\) GPa and the mean pressure \(0.576\) GPa. The elastic approach is \(a^2/R^*=1.38\) μm. The small ratio \(a/R^*\simeq0.012\) supports the local geometry approximation. Elasticity must still be assessed using material properties and the subsurface stress state. The compressive surface pressure alone is insufficient as a uniaxial yield check.

An eightfold increase in load doubles the radius. Area rises fourfold, and the peak pressure only doubles. How can a pressure field on a discrete mesh be compared with this circular solution when the edge cuts across elements? Use the second pressure moment \(M_2=\int r^2p\,dA\). Under Hertz loading the ratio \(M_2/F\) is the ratio of two integrals. Substitution \(s=r/a\) gives \(\int_0^1s^3\sqrt{1-s^2}\,ds=2/15\) in the numerator and \(\int_0^1s\sqrt{1-s^2}\,ds=1/3\) in the denominator. Therefore \(M_2/F=2a^2/5\). Multiplying a discrete ratio by \(5/2\) before taking its square root gives a Hertz-equivalent radius. Compute both sums with the surface integration weights. A quarter-model symmetry factor cancels in the ratio, but it must be restored for the full force.

On its initial mesh the OneSimulation contact experiment returns 2060.2 N and a moment-based radius of 0.5033 mm. For that force, 10 mm curvature radius and the same steel pair, the half-space formula predicts 0.5116 mm. The radius difference is about −1.6%. A non-Hertz pressure field can have the same second moment. Agreement of the radius is one test alongside force balance, mesh refinement and inspection of the profile.

Practise on sheet 2 · Infer a contact load from an optical footprint. Further reading · Heidelberg University · Continuum Mechanics · §5.8 · Contact of two elastic spheres (Hertz solution).

Distinguish displacement control from a rating test

Contact between a rolling element and a race concentrates elastic load over a small area. An ideal sphere on an elastic half-space gives \(a\propto\sqrt{R\delta}\) and \(F\propto E^*\sqrt{R}\,\delta^{3/2}\) for imposed approach \(\delta\). Predict the radius and force ratios when approach falls from 25 to 12.5 micrometres. In a second test halve both Young’s moduli while keeping the original approach. Which output should remain almost constant? Compare both predictions on the same mesh. These static contact outputs describe the stress-producing contact mechanism; the bearing rating life also requires repeated loading and bearing-specific rating data. Inspect the sphere-contact experiment.

Derive preload redistribution in opposing contact sets

A locating arrangement can use two opposing angular-contact bearings. An axial displacement loads one contact set more heavily while unloading the other. For a simplified comparison, represent each complete bearing by the same measured axial force law \(F=k\delta^{3/2}\) over the working range. The exponent is motivated by elastic Hertzian point contacts; actual bearing geometry, contact angle and load sharing are incorporated here into an illustrative equivalent coefficient. Assume rigid mounting seats, no clearance after installation, symmetric preload and no radial load in this calculation.

If the installed preload per bearing is \(F_0=k\delta_0^{3/2}\), the unloaded equilibrium has two equal opposing internal forces. An external axial load displaces the shaft by \(x\), giving

\[P=k[(\delta_0+x)^{3/2}-(\delta_0-x)^{3/2}],\qquad |x|\le\delta_0 .\]

Linearising about zero displacement gives pair stiffness \(dP/dx=3k\sqrt{\delta_0}=3F_0/\delta_0\). At \(x=\delta_0\) the relieving bearing just loses its force, while the loaded one carries \(2^{3/2}F_0\). Beyond that point its companion cannot develop tensile rolling contact and must be removed from the active force balance. A constant stiffness extrapolated from the preloaded point misses this contact transition. A preloaded arrangement can provide useful stiffness at small loads while still putting appreciable internal force on both contacts when the external thrust is zero.

Check stiffness and unloading against a thrust cycle

Suppose a measured arrangement gives \(F_0=2.0\,\mathrm{kN}\) per bearing at \(\delta_0=20\,\mathrm{\mu m}\). Initial pair stiffness is \(3(2000)/(20)=300\,\mathrm{N/\mu m}\). A 600 N thrust thus moves the shaft about 2 µm according to the small-displacement estimate. Substituting \(x/\delta_0=0.10\) into the full relation gives \(P=2000[(1.10)^{3/2}-(0.90)^{3/2}]=600\,\mathrm N\) to the shown precision. Individual bearing forces are approximately 2307 and 1708 N, so replacing each by half the applied thrust would seriously misrepresent both rolling loads.

At \(x=10\,\mathrm{\mu m}\), the full model gives \(P=2000[1.5^{3/2}-0.5^{3/2}]=2967\,\mathrm N\). Unloading starts at \(P=2^{3/2}(2000)=5657\,\mathrm N\). A peak external thrust of 6.0 kN exceeds that value. The loaded bearing alone then carries approximately 6.0 kN, with \(\delta_0+x=\delta_0(6000/2000)^{2/3}=41.6\,\mathrm{\mu m}\), hence \(x=21.6\,\mathrm{\mu m}\). A thermal adjustment that increases initial compression raises both stiffness and the unloading threshold, but it also raises bearing load during low-thrust operation. Any catalogue life calculation must use the appropriate equivalent load for each bearing over the duty cycle; external thrust by itself does not describe its internal preload history.

The relation gives a particularly simple load-range comparison at the onset of unloading. Immediately before it, the lightly loaded bearing’s tangent stiffness approaches zero as its contact deflection vanishes. The loaded bearing still has tangent stiffness proportional to \(\sqrt{2\delta_0}\), so shaft displacement retains a finite slope through the transition. A visible sudden jump in measured shaft position would point instead to clearance elsewhere, seat movement or an inadequately captured connection. Quasistatic loading and unloading measurements can also reveal hysteresis due to friction, which the purely elastic contact law used here cannot reproduce.

Quantify how a load underestimate changes bearing life

Keep dynamic load rating and speed fixed. The basic rating life varies as \((C/P)^p\), so a multiplicative error in equivalent load enters with the full life exponent. Take the ratio to the original prediction before converting revolutions to hours.
