Problem sheet 2 · Clamped interfaces and rolling contact

Evaluate the least favourable preload and bearing duty, then distinguish thermal expansion from externally applied loading.

A tolerance band on preload

A joint has stiffnesses \(k_b=200\) and \(k_m=600\) kN/mm and is preloaded to \(18\pm2\) kN. The external separating force cycles from 2 to 12 kN. Find the minimum residual clamp, maximum bolt force and alternating stress for a stress area of 36 mm². Find the smallest separation load.

Use the appropriate end of the tolerance interval

The bolt load fraction is 0.25. The least residual clamp is \(16-0.75(12)=7\,\mathrm{kN}\). The largest bolt force is \(20+0.25(12)=23\,\mathrm{kN}\). For any fixed preload within the band, the stress amplitude is \(0.25(12-2)1000/(2\times36)=34.7\,\mathrm{MPa}\). The lowest preload separates first, at \(16/0.75=21.33\,\mathrm{kN}\), above the ordinary maximum.

Convert a load block into bearing hours

A ball bearing turns for 15 min at 800 rpm under radial load 3 kN, then 10 min at 1200 rpm under 5 kN. Find the equivalent radial load, the basic life in hours for \(C=22\,\mathrm{kN}\), and the static ratio for a separate stationary load \(P_0=8\,\mathrm{kN}\) and \(C_0=12\,\mathrm{kN}\).

Count revolutions before averaging

Each interval contains 12,000 revolutions. Thus \(P_{\rm eq}^3=(3^3+5^3)/2=76\,\mathrm{kN^3}\), hence \(P_{\rm eq}=4.236\,\mathrm{kN}\). Life is \(22^3/76=140.1\) million revolutions at cycle-average speed \(24000/25=960\,\mathrm{rpm}\), or about 2432 h. For the separate stationary event, \(C_0/P_0=1.50\). A stationary overload adds no revolutions but can indent the races.

Two bearing seats warm by different amounts

A 0.50 m steel shaft warms by 55 K while its aluminium housing between the support planes warms by 20 K. Use linear expansions 12 and 23 micrometres per metre per kelvin. Determine relative growth. Does 0.08 mm axial travel accommodate it? Estimate the force after that travel is exhausted if the equivalent axial stiffness is 110 kN/mm.

Compare travel with differential growth

The shaft lengthens by 0.330 mm and the housing by 0.230 mm. Thus one end must accommodate 0.100 mm relative to the locating end. The available travel leaves 0.020 mm incompatible. In the stated linear idealisation the extra axial force magnitude is \(110(0.020)=2.2\,\mathrm{kN}\). Changing the locating scheme or the operating clearances changes this reaction.

Infer a contact load from an optical footprint

An optical instrument resolves a circular contact radius of \(0.150\) mm, with a radius uncertainty of ±2%. A 10 mm radius sphere and a flat have effective modulus \(115\) GPa. Estimate load and peak pressure from the footprint. How do the two relative uncertainties differ if the elastic model and other inputs are exact? A numerical pressure map gives \(M_2/F=9.00\times10^{-9}\) m². Is that result consistent with the optical radius?

Worked solution

Invert the Hertz law to obtain \(F=4E^*a^3/(3R^*)=51.75\) N. The peak pressure is \(2E^*a/(\pi R^*)=1.098\) GPa. The ratio of peak to mean pressure is \(3/2\), which also follows by integrating the assumed profile.

Since the inferred load scales as \(a^3\), its first-order relative uncertainty is about 6%. The exact upper factor is \(1.02^3=1.061208\). Peak pressure scales linearly with \(a\), giving 2%. Treating the reconstructed force and measured area as independent error sources would count the same radius uncertainty twice. The numerical moment implies a radius of √(2.5 × 9.00 × 10⁻⁹) = 0.150 mm. Agreement of these independent radius estimates tests the shape assumption involved in converting a pressure moment into an edge position.

Revisit chapter 4 · Recover the size of a contact from its pressure field.

Measure contact-law exponents from two solutions

Fit power laws \(F\propto d^p\) and \(a\propto d^q\) locally from the two runs at 210 GPa. At \(d=25\) µm the normal force is 2060.2 N and the inferred contact radius 0.5033 mm. At 12.5 µm they are 714.5 N and 0.3481 mm. Derive both exponents, compare them with Hertz theory, and name an independent numerical check.

Work through the solution

Divide the two power laws to eliminate their unknown prefactors. Taking logarithms gives \(p=\ln(714.5/2060.2)/\ln(1/2)=1.528\) and \(q=\ln(0.3481/0.5033)/\ln(1/2)=0.532\). The elastic half-space predicts \(3/2\) and \(1/2\). Two observations fit two power laws but cannot tell whether the exponents vary with approach. Repeat with a finer contact mesh and a third approach. Check the force integral and the radius from the pressure distribution separately.

Use the OneSimulation laboratory to check your answer

Chapter 5 · Spur gear kinematics and tooth loads

Use pitch-line compatibility and power balance to find gear speed, tooth forces and the bending demand at a tooth root.

From first principles

Connect gear speed and torque through the pitch point

For two externally meshing ideal spur gears of pitch radii \(r_1,r_2\), the pitch circles roll together at their contact point. Their tangential speeds there have equal magnitude and their rotations are opposite. With a common angular sign convention the speed relation and torque-producing tangential tooth force \(F_t\) follow from geometry and moment equilibrium.

\[\omega_1r_1=-\omega_2r_2,\]
\[|T_1|=F_t r_1,\]
\[|T_2|=F_t r_2 .\]

The two pitch circles have the same module, so radius scales with tooth count \(z\). Combining the relations gives both the signed speed ratio and the ideal ratio of delivered to applied torque magnitudes.

\[\frac{\omega_2}{\omega_1}=-\frac{z_1}{z_2},\]
\[\frac{|T_2|}{|T_1|}=\frac{z_2}{z_1}.\]

For frictionless involute teeth the contact force acts along their common normal, at pressure angle \(\alpha\) to the pitch tangent. Resolving it gives \(F_r=F_t\tan\alpha\) and \(F_n=F_t/\cos\alpha\). The radial separating load affects shaft and bearing reactions even though only the tangential component transmits shaft power.

Derivation and applications

Resolve the axial bearing load of a helical gear

Helical teeth maintain an axial component of contact force while transmitting torque. Consider a parallel-axis external helical mesh with helix angle \(\beta\) at the pitch cylinder and normal pressure angle \(\alpha_n\). Assume a representative quasi-static contact force, ideal geometry and negligible friction. Define the tangential force \(F_t\) from pitch diameter \(d\) and applied shaft torque \(T\). To resolve the remaining components, first project the normal contact force \(F_n\) onto the tangent plane of the pitch cylinder.

\[F_t=\frac{2T}{d},\]
\[F_{\rm plane}=F_n\cos\alpha_n,\]
\[F_t=F_{\rm plane}\cos\beta .\]

The projection in the tangent plane is perpendicular to the tooth trace and thus makes angle \(\beta\) to the circumferential direction. Its other component acts axially. The radial component comes directly from the normal pressure angle. Eliminating \(F_{\rm plane}\) and \(F_n\) gives the magnitudes

\[F_a=F_t\tan|\beta|,\]
\[F_r=\frac{F_t\tan\alpha_n}{\cos\beta},\]
\[F_n=\frac{F_t}{\cos\alpha_n\cos\beta}.\]

Work transmission can be checked with circumferential velocity \(v=\omega d/2\). Ideal axial and radial components do no shaft work because the gear has no velocity along those axes. They still enter bearing and housing loads.

\[P=T\omega=F_t v,\]
\[F_n^2=F_t^2+F_a^2+F_r^2.\]

The limiting spur geometry \(\beta=0\) removes axial thrust and recovers \(F_r=F_t\tan\alpha_n\). Thrust direction depends on handedness, rotation and whether the gear drives or is driven, so components calculated here are magnitudes.

Worked example

Determine the bearing thrust at a conveyor mesh

Take \(d=100\) mm, \(T=50.0\) N m, \(\beta=25.0^\circ\) and \(\alpha_n=20.0^\circ\). Pitch force is 1000 N. The axial thrust is \(1000\tan25^\circ=466\) N and radial separating force is \(1000\tan20^\circ/\cos25^\circ=402\) N. The vector resultant is 1.17 kN. Its axial component must be reacted by a locating bearing or an opposing mesh.

Second application

Limit helix angle using a thrust allowance

A different gear transmits 80.0 N m at \(d=160\) mm, also giving \(F_t=1000\) N. Its bearing arrangement permits 300 N of steady axial load from the mesh. Thus \(|\beta|\leq\arctan(300/1000)=16.7^\circ\). With normal pressure angle 20.0° the radial force at that limit is 380 N. Altering helix angle changes thrust and radial loading while the tangential force remains fixed by torque.

Replace the mesh by two rolling pitch circles

Two external spur gears have straight teeth parallel to their axes. The common normal to a pair of involute flanks passes through a fixed pitch point on the line joining the centres. At that point the pitch circles roll together at equal tangential speed. The actual contacting flanks slide relative to one another away from the pitch point. Distinguishing pitch-circle motion from local sliding explains why the simple velocity ratio can hold while the tooth surfaces still require lubrication.

For unshifted standard gears of the same module \(m\), pitch diameter equals module times tooth count, and the design centre distance equals half the sum of the pitch diameters. With a common angular sign convention on both parallel axes, the two external gears turn in opposite directions. Writing equality of pitch speeds in magnitude and then inserting direction gives

\[d_i=mz_i,\qquad a=(d_1+d_2)/2,\qquad \frac{\omega_2}{\omega_1}=-\frac{d_1}{d_2}=-\frac{z_1}{z_2}.\]

An idler changes the direction of the last gear in a simple train without changing the magnitude of the first-to-last ratio. A compound shaft carrying two distinct gears is different. Those two gears share angular speed, so the ratios of successive meshes multiply. Draw the shafts before multiplying any tooth counts.

Resolve the force along the common normal

Neglect tooth friction in the force construction. The resultant \(F_n\) acts normal to the contacting flanks. Its projection along the pitch tangent, \(F_t=F_n\cos\alpha\), produces torque on each shaft. Its radial projection, \(F_r=F_t\tan\alpha\), pushes the two gear centres apart. A straight spur mesh with aligned shafts produces no ideal axial component. The bearings must return both transverse force components to the housing.

\[T_1=F_t d_1/2,\qquad T_2=F_t d_2/2,\qquad P=F_t v,\quad v=\pi d_1 n_1/60 .\]

The transmitted tooth forces have equal magnitudes on both gears. The larger gear develops more torque because that force acts through a larger radius. Multiplying output torque by the correspondingly lower output speed recovers the input power in an ideal mesh. When estimating useful output power for a real stage, multiply input power by the specified efficiency and state which shaft receives that output.

A low-speed conveyor stage

A motor drives a 20-tooth pinion at 1200 rpm. It meshes with a 60-tooth gear of module 3 mm and pressure angle 20°. The input power is 4.0 kW. Pitch diameters are 60 and 180 mm, the centre distance is 120 mm, and the output turns at 400 rpm in the opposite direction. Angular input speed is 125.66 rad/s, giving pinion torque 31.83 N m. The pitch-line speed is 3.770 m/s.

Tangential force follows from \(4000/3.770=1061\,\mathrm N\). The radial separating force is \(1061\tan20^\circ=386\,\mathrm N\). The normal force is \(1061/\cos20^\circ=1129\,\mathrm N\). The ideal output torque is 95.5 N m. With a measured stage efficiency of 0.96, useful output is 3.84 kW and output torque is 91.7 N m. A design based on shaft torque alone omits the transverse force that bends the shaft and loads the bearings.

Build a first tooth-root estimate

For a preliminary bending model treat the loaded tooth as a short cantilever of face width \(b\), effective thickness \(s\) and moment arm \(h\). The rectangular section modulus is \(bs^2/6\); the bending moment is approximately \(F_t h\). Hence root stress scales as \(6F_t h/(bs^2)\). For geometrically similar teeth write \(Y=s^2/(6hm)\), a dimensionless form factor in the module convention used here. Then \(\sigma_b\simeq F_t/(bmY)\).

Taking \(b=24\,\mathrm{mm}\), \(m=3\,\mathrm{mm}\) and an illustrative \(Y=0.32\) gives \(1061/(24\times3\times0.32)=46.1\,\mathrm{MPa}\). This estimates nominal bending in the simplified geometry. The real tooth also sees local root concentration and surface contact stress. Uneven load across the face, dynamic tooth loads and changing load sharing during mesh can change the result. The shaft experiences a transverse force throughout steady running; an individual tooth is loaded only as it enters each mesh. That distinction changes its number of fatigue cycles.

Count how many tooth pairs can carry load simultaneously

The pitch circles determine the transmission ratio but do not establish whether tooth engagement is continuous. In an ideal external spur-gear pair the point of contact moves along the common line of action between intersections with the two addendum circles. A pair engages before the pitch point and disengages after it. The transverse contact ratio is that total active path length divided by base pitch, the spacing of adjacent involutes measured along the same line. If contact ratio lies between one and two, one pair is in mesh during part of a pitch interval and two during the remainder.

For standard unshifted pitch radii \(r_1,r_2\), base radii \(r_{bi}=r_i\cos\alpha\), addendum radii \(r_{ai}\) and operating centre distance \(a_w\), the distance from the base tangent point to the addendum intersection is \(\sqrt{r_{ai}^2-r_{bi}^2}\). Subtract the distance between the two base tangent points, \(a_w\sin\alpha_w\). The operating angle satisfies \(\cos\alpha_w=(r_{b1}+r_{b2})/a_w\). For module \(m\) and reference pressure angle \(\alpha\), base pitch is \(p_b=\pi m\cos\alpha\). Therefore

\[\varepsilon_\alpha=\frac{\sqrt{r_{a1}^2-r_{b1}^2}+\sqrt{r_{a2}^2-r_{b2}^2}-a_w\sin\alpha_w}{p_b}.\]

This geometric expression presumes sufficient usable involute profile at both roots and no interference, profile modification or edge contact that shortens the path. It yields geometric overlap, not equal division of tooth load; stiffness and deflection evolve as a tooth crosses the mesh.

Evaluate a centre-distance change in a reducer

A 20-tooth pinion meshes with a 40-tooth gear, both module 3 mm with 20° reference pressure angle and an addendum of one module. Reference pitch radii are 30 and 60 mm, base radii 28.19 and 56.38 mm, and addendum radii 33 and 63 mm. At the reference centre distance 90 mm, the first two square-root terms are 17.155 and 28.109 mm. The tangent-point spacing is \(90\sin20^\circ=30.782\) mm, giving active path length 14.482 mm. Base pitch is 8.856 mm and transverse contact ratio is 1.635.

In this ideal mesh, about 63.5% of each base-pitch interval has two pairs engaged and 36.5% has one. Increase the centre distance to 90.5 mm while retaining exactly the same gears. The operating pressure angle increases to \(\arccos[(28.19+56.38)/90.5]=20.85^\circ\). The base pitch and addendum intersections remain fixed, but the longer tangent-point spacing lowers the contact ratio to 1.473. The single-pair portion expands to 52.7%. The tooth-count ratio remains exactly two, so a speed measurement alone cannot detect this reduction in load-sharing opportunity. Measuring backlash, centre distance or mesh vibration supplies different evidence. A target for tooth root stress should account for the time spent in single-pair mesh as well as nominal torque.

At 1200 pinion rpm a 20-tooth pinion gives a mesh frequency of \(20(1200)/60=400\) Hz. The altered centre distance leaves this frequency unchanged while changing the fraction of each 2.50 ms mesh interval spent in single-pair contact. Dynamic excitation amplitude can therefore change without a shift in the measured mesh frequency.

Find the three speeds and housing reaction of a planetary gearset

A simple epicyclic train has a sun with \(N_s\) teeth, an internal ring with \(N_r\) teeth, and planets whose centres are carried by a rotating arm. One fixed-axis gear ratio cannot relate all three members. In a frame turning with the carrier, the planet centres are stationary and each mesh is an ordinary pitch-circle rolling constraint. The sun–planet mesh reverses the relative spin direction. The internal planet–ring mesh preserves it. Eliminating the planet speed gives the Willis relation

\[N_s(\omega_s-\omega_c)+N_r(\omega_r-\omega_c)=0,\qquad \omega_c=\frac{N_s\omega_s+N_r\omega_r}{N_s+N_r}.\]

All speeds use the same positive direction in the stationary frame. With ring locked, the carrier turns with the sun at the reduction ratio \(1+N_r/N_s\). If both input members turn together, all parts rotate as a rigid train and the planets have no spin relative to the carrier. These two limits help detect a sign error at an internal mesh.

Next define all torques as torques applied to the train. In ideal steady operation the external powers sum to zero. For independent admissible sun and ring speeds, substitute the carrier relation into \(T_s\omega_s+T_r\omega_r+T_c\omega_c=0\). The two coefficients must each vanish, giving \(T_s=-T_c N_s/(N_s+N_r)\) and \(T_r=-T_c N_r/(N_s+N_r)\). This also yields \(T_s+T_r+T_c=0\), as required by angular momentum balance at constant speed.

For a bench mixer choose \(N_s=24,\ N_r=72\). Same-module gears then need \(N_p=(72-24)/2=24\) teeth per planet. With the ring bolted to the frame and the sun driven at \(1200\,\mathrm{rpm}\), the carrier turns at \(300\,\mathrm{rpm}\). At \(T_s=+12.0\,\mathrm{N\,m}\), the load applies \(T_c=-48.0\,\mathrm{N\,m}\) and the frame applies \(T_r=+36.0\,\mathrm{N\,m}\) to the gearset. Sun input and carrier output each have magnitude \(1.51\,\mathrm{kW}\). The stationary ring transmits no power but its mounts still carry a reaction torque of 36.0 N m.

Practise on sheet 3 · Trace the power when both the sun and ring move. Further reading · Jönköping University · Applied Mechanics · §10.8 Planetary gears, “Seen from the carrier: the Willis equation” and “Torques and the forces on the teeth”.

Chapter 6 · Tolerances, interference and torque transmission

Turn shaft and hub limits into a range of contact pressures and check whether a cylindrical interference fit can transmit torque.

From first principles

Relate diametral interference to frictional holding torque

An unassembled shaft can have a larger free diameter than its mating hub bore. Define positive diametral interference \(I=d_s-d_h\). Under elastic assembly the shaft surface moves inwards and the bore outwards until their radii agree. Assume axisymmetry, uniform pressure \(p\), nominal diameter \(d\), contact length \(L\), and combined radial compliance \(c_r\) giving total radial displacement per unit pressure. Compatibility gives

\[I/2=|u_s|+u_h=c_rp,\]
\[p=\frac{I}{2c_r}.\]

Under ideal Coulomb friction, the local shear traction at impending uniform circumferential slip is \(\mu p\). Every area element of the cylindrical contact has moment arm \(d/2\). Integrating the limiting traction yields

\[T_{\rm lim}=\int_A\frac d2\mu p\,dA=\frac{\pi}{2}\mu pLd^2 .\]

Uniform axial sliding instead has limiting force \(F_{\rm lim}=\mu p\pi dL\). These rely on the same available friction traction, so simultaneous axial and torsional loading must share that capacity. The factor of two in compatibility matters because manufacturing limits usually specify diameters, while surface displacements are radial.

Derivation and applications

Predict how differential thermal expansion changes an interference fit

A shaft and hub assembled with a press fit can lose contact pressure when their temperatures change. Let \(d\) be their common nominal diameter at reference temperature and \(I_0>0\) the initial diametral interference, defined as free shaft diameter minus free hub-bore diameter. Assume small strains, uniform temperature in each part, free axial expansion and elastic response while contact is closed. Let \(\alpha_s,\alpha_h\) be the linear expansion coefficients and \(\Delta T_s,\Delta T_h\) the temperature changes. To first order in strain and interference-to-diameter ratio,

\[I(T)=I_0+d(\alpha_s\Delta T_s-\alpha_h\Delta T_h).\]

If \(c_r\) is the combined radial compliance per unit pressure, the radial mismatch is \(I/2\). Positive pressure requires positive interference. When the calculated interference becomes negative the unloaded circular parts have clearance; the elastic closure formula then ceases to apply.

\[p(T)=\frac{\max[0,I(T)]}{2c_r}.\]

For uniform axial contact length \(L\) and constant friction coefficient \(\mu\), the maximum tangential traction is \(\mu p\). Integrating that traction over the cylindrical surface, with moment arm \(d/2\), translates a temperature difference into a torque capacity.

\[T_{\rm lim}=\frac{\pi}{2}\mu pLd^2,\]
\[\frac{T_{\rm lim}(T)}{T_{\rm lim}(0)}=\frac{\max[0,I(T)]}{I_0}.\]

This ratio assumes the elastic moduli, friction coefficient and dimensions appearing outside the interference stay effectively constant. Thermal gradients that distort the circular geometry can invalidate the uniform-pressure assumption before mean interference reaches zero.

Worked example

Estimate capacity loss after uniform warming

A 50.0 mm steel shaft in an aluminium hub has \(I_0=40.0\) µm. Their expansion coefficients are 12.0 and 23.0 µm/(m K). A common 40.0 K temperature rise reduces interference by \(0.0500(11\times10^{-6})(40.0)=22.0\) µm. Remaining interference is 18.0 µm. A measured reference capacity of 120 N m scales to \(120(18/40)=54.0\) N m in this model.

Second application

Choose a temporary assembly temperature

A separate 80.0 mm bore with expansion coefficient \(18.0\times10^{-6}\) K⁻¹ has 50.0 µm reference interference. With the shaft kept at reference temperature, a desired assembly clearance of 20.0 µm requires bore growth of 70.0 µm. The uniform hub temperature rise is \(70.0\times10^{-6}/[0.0800(18.0\times10^{-6})]=48.6\) K. The nominal interference returns after both parts settle back to the reference temperature, provided the material response and surfaces remain elastic.

Start from this chapter. In a displacement-controlled elastic contact, scaling both elastic moduli changes reaction force. Use the sphere and block to test the scaling, then consider which geometric assumptions change for a cylindrical interference fit.

OneSimulation laboratory · Test the contact law under imposed approach

Convert manufacturing limits into assembly conditions

A shaft and a hub can share a nominal diameter while their manufactured diameters differ by several tens of micrometres. Name the diameters explicitly before subtracting them. For the present joint use positive diametral interference \(I=d_s-d_h\), where \(d_s\) is the free shaft diameter and \(d_h\) the free bore. Assemble the smallest shaft with the largest hole to obtain the minimum interference. The opposite pair gives the maximum. Worst-case limits do not require a probability distribution for the production process.

Consider a 40 mm connection with shaft limits 40.030 to 40.045 mm and bore limits 40.000 to 40.015 mm. The minimum difference is 0.015 mm and the maximum 0.045 mm. Every permitted pair interferes. The radial mismatch is half the diametral interference because the radius changes on both sides of a diameter. Confusing these conventions introduces a factor of two in the inferred pressure and torque.

Relate geometric mismatch to elastic deformation

When the parts return to their common assembly temperature, contact pressure compresses the shaft and expands the bore. Assume cylindrical surfaces, a short open-ended elastic hub with negligible axial stress, a solid elastic shaft and approximately uniform pressure away from the ends. Define the radial compliance \(c_r\) as the sum of the inward shaft and outward hub displacements per unit interface pressure. Compatibility across the common radius then reads

\[\delta_r=I/2=|u_s|+u_h=p\,c_r,\qquad p=\frac{I}{2c_r}.\]

The combined compliance can be found from an elastic cylinder calculation or a verified test of geometrically identical parts. Providing it separately permits a torque calculation without assuming an infinitely stiff hub. A thinner hub generally expands further at the same pressure. Its larger compliance reduces the pressure developed by a given interference. Surface roughness that flattens during assembly can also reduce the effective interference relative to a measurement over the original asperities.

Integrate friction over the contact surface

A narrow surface patch of area \(dA\) can support a tangential friction force up to \(\mu p\,dA\). All such forces act at radius \(r=d/2\) and oppose impending rotation. Under uniform contact pressure and fully mobilised Coulomb friction, integrating around the circumference and along the overlap length \(L\) gives the limiting torque. For a purely axial pull the same friction forces act along the axis and their direct sum gives the limiting pull-out force.

\[T_{\lim}=\int r\mu p\,dA=\frac{\pi}{2}\mu pLd^2,\qquad F_{\lim}=\mu p\pi dL .\]

Take \(L=35\,\mathrm{mm}\), \(\mu=0.12\), and \(c_r=0.50\,\mathrm{\mu m/MPa}\) in the 40 mm joint. The radial mismatches range from 7.5 to 22.5 micrometres, so calculated pressures range from 15 to 45 MPa. At the lower limit the tangential force capacity is \(0.12(15)\pi(40)(35)=7917\,\mathrm N\), and the torque capacity is \(7917(0.020)=158\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). At the upper limit pressure and capacity are three times larger. Use the lowest pressure to test transmission and the highest to evaluate hub stress.

Provide thermal clearance and account for combined service loads

Suppose only the hub is heated, so its bore grows approximately by \(\alpha d\Delta T\). To slip the worst-case pair together with a further 0.020 mm of diametral assembly clearance, the bore must grow by \(0.045+0.020=0.065\,\mathrm{mm}\). With \(\alpha=12\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}\) a uniform temperature rise of \(0.065/[40(12\times10^{-6})]=135\,\mathrm K\) is required in this ideal expansion calculation. Heat loss during transfer changes the actual temperature at insertion.

A shaft may also carry a sustained axial pull while delivering torque. Under the uniform-traction model the axial and circumferential components of the surface friction force share one bound. At the lower interference with a 4.0 kN pull the remaining circumferential capacity is \(\sqrt{7917^2-4000^2}=6832\,\mathrm N\), or about 137 N m. Checking the separate pure-load capacities would miss this interaction. If repeated microslip, an uncertain friction coefficient or stiffness near the hub edges is important, measurements of the actual assembly can determine which assumptions need closer investigation.

Derive interference pressure from thick-cylinder compliance

The radial compliance used above can be related to actual dimensions and materials. Consider a long enough cylindrical fit for a uniform central contact region, with free axial expansion so a plane-stress approximation is used for each cross-section. Let the common nominal radius be \(a\), outer hub radius \(b\), and positive pressure \(p\). The solid shaft experiences uniform in-plane compression \(\sigma_r=\sigma_\theta=-p\). Consequently its surface circumferential strain is \(-p(1-\nu_s)/E_s\), giving radial shrinkage magnitude \(pa(1-\nu_s)/E_s\).

For an elastic hub with free outside, radial equilibrium \(d\sigma_r/dr+(\sigma_r-\sigma_\theta)/r=0\) and axisymmetric compatibility give Lamé fields \(\sigma_r=A-B/r^2,\ \sigma_\theta=A+B/r^2\). Setting \(\sigma_r(a)=-p,\ \sigma_r(b)=0\) gives bore hoop stress \(\sigma_\theta(a)=p(b^2+a^2)/(b^2-a^2)\). Use \(\epsilon_\theta(a)=[\sigma_\theta(a)-\nu_h\sigma_r(a)]/E_h=u_h(a)/a\). Adding hub expansion and shaft contraction leads to

\[I/2=pa\left[\frac{1-\nu_s}{E_s}+\frac{(b^2+a^2)/(b^2-a^2)+\nu_h}{E_h}\right],\]

where \(I\) is the free diametral interference. The factor of two and the sign of radial pressure both affect the result. Significant axial restraint, plastic deformation, a hollow shaft or edge-pressure peaks require a different compliance model.

Compare thick and thin hubs at the same interference

A steel shaft and hub both have \(E=210\,\mathrm{GPa}\), \(\nu=0.30\). Their contact radius is 20 mm, hub outer radius 40 mm and measured diametral interference 30 micrometres. In mm–MPa units the combined radial compliance is \(20[0.70+(40^2+20^2)/(40^2-20^2)+0.30]/210000=0.000254\,\mathrm{mm/MPa}\). Pressure is \(0.015/0.000254=59.1\,\mathrm{MPa}\). The tensile hoop stress at the hub bore is \(59.1(5/3)=98.5\,\mathrm{MPa}\). For engagement length 25 mm and \(\mu=0.12\), torque capacity is \(2\pi\mu p a^2L=446\,\mathrm{N\,m}\) approximately.

Now reduce the outer radius to 26 mm with the same parts at the fit surface. The hub geometry factor becomes \((26^2+20^2)/(26^2-20^2)=3.90\), giving combined radial compliance \(20(1+3.90)/210000=0.000467\,\mathrm{mm/MPa}\). Pressure falls to 32.2 MPa and torque capacity falls to about 243 N m. Yet bore hoop stress rises to \(32.2(3.90)=126\,\mathrm{MPa}\) because the thinner hub amplifies hoop demand more strongly. Reducing contact pressure alone is not evidence that the hub becomes safer. The same dimensional change can reduce torque capacity and increase peak elastic stress, so both checks should use the geometry of the actual mating parts.

For ductile elastic parts, the hub bore should also be assessed in its multiaxial state. In the thicker example its principal stresses are approximately \(+98.5,0,-59.1\) MPa under plane stress. The von Mises value is \(\sqrt{98.5^2+59.1^2+98.5(59.1)}=138\,\mathrm{MPa}\), above the tensile hoop stress by itself. For the thinner hub, using \(+126,0,-32.2\) MPa gives approximately 145 MPa. A comparison with an allowable uniaxial stress must therefore state the criterion applied. Once significant yielding occurs the linear compliance used to determine pressure is itself affected, and enlarging the nominal interference will no longer guarantee a proportional increase in transmitted torque. A pull-out or torque-to-slip test on representative surfaces checks the independent assumption about friction.

For an interference test, measuring the outside hub diameter change offers an additional observable. At the outer surface radial stress vanishes, so its hoop strain depends directly on the local tensile hoop stress.

In fact the hub’s outside hoop stress from the same Lamé field is \(2pa^2/(b^2-a^2)\). For the 40 mm outer radius it is \(2(59.1)(400)/(1600-400)=39.4\,\mathrm{MPa}\), so the predicted 80 mm outside diameter increases by \(80(39.4)/210000=0.0150\,\mathrm{mm}\). For the thinner hub it is \(2(32.2)(400)/(676-400)=93.3\,\mathrm{MPa}\), giving an outside diameter increase \(52(93.3)/210000=0.0231\,\mathrm{mm}\). The thinner outer wall moves more under a smaller interface pressure. Observing this change together with transmitted torque can separate an incorrect elastic pressure estimate from an incorrect friction coefficient.

For example, if the thick hub expands by the predicted 15 micrometres but slips at 330 N m under otherwise matching conditions, the implied mean friction coefficient is \(0.12(330/446)=0.089\). If the measured expansion were instead much smaller, an undersized effective interference, partial contact or a nonuniform fit would deserve investigation before calibrating friction. The micrometer reading is itself temperature sensitive: a uniform temperature difference of 10 K changes an 80 mm steel diameter by about 9.6 micrometres at \(12\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}\). Stabilise the temperatures before comparing the calculated elastic expansion with metrology.

Size the working flanks and shear section of a parallel key

A shaft can drive a removable hub through the flanks of a parallel key. The load path runs from the shaft keyway into one side of the key, across its shear section, and out of the opposite side into the hub. Let \(d\) be the shaft diameter. Represent the distributed tangential contact by the resultant \(F=2T/d\) at the nominal shaft radius. This model assumes a single fitted key carries the specified torque without credit for friction from a separate clamp.

There are two different projected areas. If \(b\) is the key width and \(L_e\) the effective engaged length, the shear plane has area \(bL_e\). If \(h_e\) is the actual load-bearing radial height on the hub side, the projected bearing area on that flank is \(h_eL_e\). The full key height includes material inside the shaft. Chamfers, end relief and clearance can further reduce the effective dimensions. Under a uniform-load sizing approximation,

\[\tau_\mathrm{key}=\frac{2T}{dbL_e},\qquad p_\mathrm{hub}=\frac{2T}{dh_eL_e},\qquad L_{e,\min}=\max\left(\frac{2T}{db\tau_\mathrm{allow}},\,\frac{2T}{dh_ep_\mathrm{allow}}\right).\]

For a conveyor pulley, \(T=180\,\mathrm{N\,m}\), \(d=40.0\,\mathrm{mm}\), \(b=12.0\,\mathrm{mm}\), \(h_e=3.30\,\mathrm{mm}\) and \(L_e=36.0\,\mathrm{mm}\). Work in N and mm, giving \(F=2(180000)/40=9000\,\mathrm N\). Mean shear is \(9000/(12\times36)=20.8\,\mathrm{MPa}\), whereas hub bearing pressure is \(9000/(3.30\times36)=75.8\,\mathrm{MPa}\). For design allowables of 45 and 90 MPa respectively, required engaged lengths are 16.7 and 30.3 mm. The proposed engagement passes these two static checks, with hub bearing pressure setting the longer minimum. A softer hub can therefore govern even when a much stronger key material is chosen. This calculation does not establish the shaft's fatigue strength at its keyway root. That requires the local shaft stress, particularly for rotating bending.

Practise on sheet 3 · Can the available hub length carry the specified steady torque?. Further reading · Jönköping University · Applied Mechanics · §10.18 Shaft–hub joints, “The parallel key”.

Problem sheet 3 · Gear meshes and assembled fits

Link tooth forces to shaft loads and use manufacturing limits to check a transmitting interface.

Resolve gear forces

An 18-tooth pinion of module 4 mm drives a 54-tooth external gear. Input torque is 90 N m at 900 rpm. Use a pressure angle of 20°. Find output speed, ideal output torque, both pitch diameters and the radial force on either shaft. What part of the bearing load would a torque-only analysis omit?

Draw the pitch circles and the force normal

Pitch diameters are 72 and 216 mm. The output turns at 300 rpm in the opposing direction, with ideal torque 270 N m. The tangential force is \(90/0.036=2500\,\mathrm N\). The radial force is \(2500\tan20^\circ=910\,\mathrm N\), and the normal resultant is 2660 N. A drawing showing only torque misses both transverse forces feeding shaft bending and bearing reactions.

Find the least favourable fit

A shaft spans 50.028–50.040 mm, and its bore spans 50.005–50.016 mm. The combined radial compliance is 0.40 micrometres/MPa. The overlap is 30 mm and friction coefficient 0.10. Find pressure limits and minimum torque capacity. Is a demand of 160 N m supported by every allowed pair in this model?

Keep track of radius and diameter

Diametral interference ranges from \(28-16=12\) to \(40-5=35\) micrometres. Radial mismatches are 6 and 17.5 micrometres and pressures 15 and 43.75 MPa. With N and mm the limiting torque at minimum pressure is \(\pi(0.10)(15)(30)(50^2)/2=176715\,\mathrm{N\,mm}=176.7\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). The smallest nominal capacity exceeds the demand by about 10%. This margin is sensitive to error in the friction coefficient.

Connect a gear stage to a friction joint

A compound reducer uses tooth pairs 20:50 and 24:48, with both meshes external, each 97% efficient. Its input runs at 1500 rpm and supplies 3 kW. Find output speed, direction and useful output torque. If a fit of radius 20 mm supports at most 6000 N resultant surface friction and also transmits a 4000 N axial force, can it transmit that torque?

Compare drive output with remaining surface capacity

The total reduction is \(2.5\times2=5\); the output turns at 300 rpm in the input direction. Useful output power is \(3000(0.97)^2=2823\,\mathrm W\), hence \(T=2823/[2\pi(300)/60]=89.85\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). Remaining tangential force is \(\sqrt{6000^2-4000^2}=4472\,\mathrm N\), giving 89.44 N m. The joint falls just short even before an allowance for manufacturing and friction scatter.

Trace the power when both the sun and ring move

A lossless test gearbox has \(N_s=30,\ N_r=90\). The sun runs at \(+900\,\mathrm{rpm}\) with \(T_s=+8.00\,\mathrm{N\,m}\), and a speed-controlled ring runs at \(-120\,\mathrm{rpm}\). Determine the carrier speed, the other two torques and which ports supply or receive power.

Show the worked solution

The carrier speed is \([30(900)+90(-120)]/120=135\,\mathrm{rpm}\). The torque proportions are \(T_c=-8(120/30)=-32.0\,\mathrm{N\,m}\) and \(T_r=+24.0\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). Multiplying each torque by its signed speed in rad/s gives \(P_s=+754.0\,\mathrm W,\ P_r=-301.6\,\mathrm W,\ P_c=-452.4\,\mathrm W\). The sun supplies all the power, and both the ring drive and carrier load receive it. A positive torque on a member rotating negatively represents negative power to the gearset.

Revisit chapter 5 · Find the three speeds and housing reaction of a planetary gearset.

Can the available hub length carry the specified steady torque?

A pump hub must transmit 240 N m on a 50.0 mm shaft. Its parallel key has width 14.0 mm and usable hub-side flank height 3.80 mm. The mean shear allowable is 45 MPa and the bearing allowable 80 MPa. Find the minimum effective length and the maximum torque if only 30.0 mm of effective engagement fits.

Show the worked solution

The tangential force is \(2(240000)/50=9600\,\mathrm N\). The shear requirement is \(9600/(14\times45)=15.24\,\mathrm{mm}\). The bearing requirement is \(9600/(3.80\times80)=31.58\,\mathrm{mm}\). The available 30.0 mm falls short. Inverting both checks at that length gives shear capacity \(45(14)(30)(25)=472500\,\mathrm{N\,mm}\) and bearing capacity \(80(3.80)(30)(25)=228000\,\mathrm{N\,mm}\). The limiting torque is 228 N m. A longer nominal key only helps if it increases effective flank engagement.

Chapter 7 · Helical springs, wire stress and stored energy

Derive the rate of a close-coiled compression spring and check its working stress and available travel.

From first principles

Find the spring rate from torsional strain energy in the wire

A close-coiled spring of mean coil diameter \(D\) and round wire diameter \(d\) converts an axial force \(F\) into wire torque about \(FD/2\). Assume a small helix angle, slender wire, \(N_a\) active turns and linear elastic shear modulus \(G\). The active wire length is approximately \(\pi D N_a\) and its polar section moment is \(J=\pi d^4/32\). The torsional energy of a straight wire element of length \(ds\) under torque \(T\) is \(T^2ds/(2GJ)\).

\[U=\int_{\rm active}\frac{T^2}{2GJ}\,ds\simeq\frac{(FD/2)^2\pi D N_a}{2GJ}=\frac{4F^2D^3N_a}{Gd^4}.\]

For a linear spring loaded gradually from zero, energy also equals \(F\delta/2\). Equating the two expressions gives axial deflection and rate.

\[\delta=\frac{8FD^3N_a}{Gd^4},\]
\[k=\frac{F}{\delta}=\frac{Gd^4}{8D^3N_a}.\]

More active wire stores more energy at fixed force and therefore gives greater travel. Curvature corrections to local wire stress, inactive end turns and solid-height limits require separate checks before applying this compliance across the full working stroke.

Derivation and applications

Derive the stiffness change as one compression spring reaches a stop

Two springs in series can provide a soft initial rate and a stiffer response later in the stroke. Consider linear spring rates \(k_1,k_2\) and a rigid stop limiting the deflection of spring one to \(s>0\). Initially both springs are unloaded, their masses are negligible and contact remains axial. Loading is quasistatic. Before stop engagement the same force passes through both springs, so their elastic displacements add. Write total travel as \(x\).

\[x=\frac{F}{k_1}+\frac{F}{k_2},\]
\[k_{\rm series}=\frac{k_1k_2}{k_1+k_2}.\]

The stop first engages when spring one has shortened by \(s\). Its force then is \(F_*=k_1s\) and spring two has already shortened by \(F_*/k_2\). Beyond this position the stop carries any extra force passing around spring one, whose deflection stays fixed. All further travel goes into spring two.

\[x_*=s+\frac{F_*}{k_2},\]
\[F(x)=\begin{cases}k_{\rm series}x,&0\leq x\leq x_*,\\ F_*+k_2(x-x_*),&x>x_*.\end{cases}\]

Force is continuous at engagement while tangent stiffness changes. Integration of the force over the total travel gives stored elastic energy. Past engagement it contains the energy already stored, the work against the existing force and the quadratic incremental contribution.

\[U(x)=\tfrac12F_*x_*+F_*(x-x_*)+\tfrac12k_2(x-x_*)^2,\]
\[x\geq x_* .\]

A real stop has finite compliance and impact can excite spring mass. This static law describes the slowly measured transition and does not determine the transient peak force at engagement.

Worked example

Predict an end-of-stroke force

A return assembly has \(k_1=30.0\) N/mm, \(k_2=60.0\) N/mm and \(s=4.00\) mm. Its initial rate is 20.0 N/mm; engagement occurs at 120 N and 6.00 mm total travel. At 8.00 mm the force is \(120+60(2)=240\) N. Stored energy is \(360+240+120=720\) N mm or 0.720 J. Spring one still supports 120 N and the stop supports the extra 120 N.

Second application

Recover the hidden spring rate from a force trace

A different mechanism has measured slopes of 15.0 N/mm before contact and 40.0 N/mm afterwards. The second slope identifies \(k_2\). From \(1/k_1=1/15-1/40\) one finds \(k_1=24.0\) N/mm. If the kink occurs at 3.20 mm total travel, its force is 48.0 N and the internal stop gap is \(48.0/24.0=2.00\) mm. The other spring accounts for the remaining 1.20 mm at engagement.

An axial spring force twists the wire

A helical spring appears to shorten along its axis under a compressive force. Follow one turn of its wire, however, and the main elastic deformation is torsion. For a close-coiled spring the helix angle is small and the force line along the spring axis lies a mean radius \(D/2\) from the wire centre. A cut through a wire turn therefore transmits a torque \(T=FD/2\) as well as direct shear. Use mean coil diameter \(D\), wire diameter \(d\) and \(N_a\) active turns. End turns that rest against a seat may add solid height without contributing equally to the spring rate.

Approximate wire length as \(\pi D N_a\) and wire polar moment as \(\pi d^4/32\). Under small elastic deformation the torsional strain energy is \(U=T^2\ell/(2GJ)\). Differentiating this energy with respect to axial load gives axial deflection, since an incremental load is work-conjugate to that displacement.

\[U=\frac{4F^2D^3N_a}{Gd^4},\qquad \delta=\frac{\partial U}{\partial F}=\frac{8FD^3N_a}{Gd^4},\qquad k=F/\delta=\frac{Gd^4}{8D^3N_a}.\]

Doubling the number of active turns halves the rate at fixed wire and coil diameter. Increasing wire diameter has a much stronger fourth-power effect. Changing wire diameter at fixed outside diameter also changes the mean coil diameter, so both terms must be recomputed.

Account for the curvature of the wire

Nominal torsional surface stress in straight wire is \(16T/(\pi d^3)=8FD/(\pi d^3)\). Curvature and direct shear raise the maximum stress on the inside of a real coil. One common elastic estimate for round wire is the Wahl factor \(K_w=(4C-1)/(4C-4)+0.615/C\), with spring index \(C=D/d\). Here it multiplies the nominal torsional expression to estimate peak shear. A small spring index makes the curvature contribution more important. Use a consistent stress convention with the material or fatigue data used for assessment.

Compare working travel with available space

A fixture spring uses 4 mm steel wire, 28 mm mean diameter, six active turns and \(G=79\,\mathrm{GPa}\). Its rate is \(79000(4^4)/[8(28^3)(6)]=19.2\,\mathrm{N/mm}\). At 120 N it compresses 6.25 mm and stores \(F\delta/2=0.375\,\mathrm J\). The spring index is seven, giving \(K_w=1.213\) and corrected shear \(1.213[8(120)(28)/(\pi4^3)]=162\,\mathrm{MPa}\).

With eight total turns and an ideal closed and ground construction, an approximate solid height is \(8(4)=32\,\mathrm{mm}\). A 44 mm free length leaves about 12 mm before full contact. At the calculated working deflection the remaining travel is 5.75 mm. Travel to solid height sets a geometric limit even if a stress calculation permits a larger load. When coils start contacting sequentially, the active turn count falls and the spring stiffens; the preceding constant-rate relation then no longer describes its full travel.

Check the active turn count

Two measured loads of 40 and 100 N at compressions of 2 and 5 mm give an incremental rate of \(60/3=20\,\mathrm{N/mm}\). Using a difference between readings removes a constant force offset. A change of slope near full compression can indicate that additional coils have made contact.

Derive the contribution of a spring’s own mass

A spring designed from static force and travel also has moving material. Near an operating resonance the extra inertia changes response and can produce dynamic coil loading. As a first approximation take a uniform active helical spring of total moving mass \(m_s\), lower end fixed, upper end attached to a translating mass \(M\). Let \(s\) be a dimensionless material coordinate running from zero to one along active turns. For slow axial deformation the extension and axial velocity of a point grow nearly linearly with position: \(u(s)=sx,\ \dot u=s\dot x\). Direct support movement, coil contact and lateral buckling are excluded.

Integrating the spring’s translational kinetic energy gives \(T_s=\tfrac12 m_s\dot x^2\int_0^1s^2ds=\tfrac12(m_s/3)\dot x^2\). With spring potential \(kx^2/2\), the lowest frequency estimate is \(\sqrt{k/(M+m_s/3)}\). The one-third factor arises from the assumed distribution of speed; treating every turn as moving at the end velocity would overestimate inertia. Dead-end turns attached rigidly at the fixed seat have essentially zero axial speed and should not all be included at this weight.

One can refine the axial continuum idealisation by letting displacement be \(u(s,t)\) and using elastic energy \((k/2)\int_0^1u_s^2ds\). The field equation becomes \(m_su_{tt}=ku_{ss}\), with \(u(0)=0\) and \(M u_{tt}(1)=-k u_s(1)\). A mode \(\sin(\beta s)\) gives \(\omega=\beta\sqrt{k/m_s}\) and \(\beta\tan\beta=m_s/M\). This also predicts internal surge modes, beyond the single effective-mass approximation.

Estimate the frequency reserve of a return mechanism

An actuator return spring has measured rate 4000 N/m, active spring mass 0.20 kg and an attached moving assembly of 0.50 kg. Ignoring spring mass gives \(f_n=\sqrt{4000/0.50}/(2\pi)=14.24\,\mathrm{Hz}\). Including one third of the active spring mass gives \(f_n=\sqrt{4000/(0.50+0.20/3)}/(2\pi)=13.37\,\mathrm{Hz}\). The distributed relation has \(\beta\tan\beta=0.40\), giving \(\beta=0.5932\) and \(f_n=13.35\,\mathrm{Hz}\). The quick effective-mass estimate happens to be close for this mass ratio since the first mode shape is nearly linear.

If drive motion has a strong third harmonic at 13.5 Hz, the massless estimate places resonance 0.74 Hz above it while the distributed estimate places it only 0.15 Hz below. A static travel check would miss this change completely. Reducing attached mass shifts the frequency upwards but also makes the spring’s distributed character more important. In the limiting case of no attached mass, the first root tends to \(\pi/2\); the exact continuum frequency then differs from the \(m_s/3\) estimate. Real helical springs also exhibit rotational motion of the wire, damping and nonuniform end conditions. Measuring one low-frequency mode can validate the effective-mass estimate while leaving higher-frequency coil surge unverified.

A frequency test with an added known tip mass can help estimate the effective spring mass empirically. Within the low-mode approximation plot \(1/\omega^2\) against attached mass \(M\): the slope is \(1/k\), and the intercept is \(m_s/(3k)\). Curvature as the tip mass becomes small reveals the limits of the linear assumed mode shape.

Separate wire curvature from nominal spring stress

A close-coiled helical spring carries axial load \(F\). Nominal wire torsion gives \(8FD/(\pi d^3)\). Compare a direct-shear correction with the Wahl factor, which also accounts for curvature of the wound wire.

Read values from the plot
Shear-stress multiplier at selected values of Spring index D/d.

Chapter 8 · Belt traction, wrap and power transmission

Derive the tension-ratio bound for a flat belt and determine the pretension and bearing load needed to transmit a specified power.

From first principles

Derive the capstan bound from a small belt arc

A flexible flat belt transmits force by friction as it wraps around a pulley. Assume a thin belt, small bending stiffness, steady low-speed operation and constant coefficient of static friction \(\mu\). Consider an arc element subtending \(d\theta\) radians with tension \(T\) at one end and \(T+dT\) at the other. The inward components of the adjoining tensions produce a normal contact force approximately \(dN=T\,d\theta\). Tangential equilibrium requires friction to carry the tension change.

\[|dT|\leq\mu\,dN=\mu T\,d\theta .\]

Integrate from the slack-side tension \(T_2\) to tight-side tension \(T_1\) over total contact angle \(\beta\). Equality describes impending gross slip in this ideal limit.

\[\ln(T_1/T_2)\leq\mu\beta,\]
\[T_1/T_2\leq e^{\mu\beta}.\]

The net tangential force is the tension difference. At belt speed \(v\) the transmitted mechanical power is \(P=(T_1-T_2)v\). Raising both tensions together can increase the available difference but also raises shaft and bearing loads. At high belt speed the inward acceleration of each moving element changes the normal-force balance.

Derivation and applications

Calculate belt wrap and length from pulley geometry

A small pulley loses contact angle when the two pulley radii differ. Consider an open, crossed-free belt around circular pitch radii \(R>r\) separated by centre distance \(C>R-r\). Treat the belt as thin, taut and inextensible and ignore sag. The radii to the ends of one straight span are perpendicular to its tangent. Translating that tangent parallel to itself produces a right triangle with hypotenuse \(C\) and short side \(R-r\). The resulting angle \(\alpha\) and span length \(\ell\) obey

\[\sin\alpha=\frac{R-r}{C},\]
\[\ell=\sqrt{C^2-(R-r)^2}=C\cos\alpha .\]

The small pulley loses angle \(\alpha\) at each side relative to a semicircle, while the larger one gains the same amount. Summing the two straight spans and the two arc lengths gives the exact pitch length in the ideal geometry.

\[\beta_s=\pi-2\alpha,\]
\[\beta_L=\pi+2\alpha,\]
\[L=2\ell+\pi(R+r)+2(R-r)\alpha .\]

For a long centre distance expand the square root and inverse sine in \((R-r)/C\). The leading correction to the simple sum of two centre distances and two semicircles is positive. The same geometry sets the small pulley's friction-limited tension ratio for a stationary or low-speed flat belt.

\[L\simeq2C+\pi(R+r)+\frac{(R-r)^2}{C},\]
\[T_1/T_2\leq e^{\mu\beta_s}.\]

Angle in the exponential must be in radians. Increasing centre distance brings the two wraps closer to half a circle but also changes required belt length. A tensioning device must accommodate both geometry and elastic stretch.

Worked example

Determine a small pulley's available traction

For \(R=100\) mm, \(r=40.0\) mm and \(C=300\) mm, \(\alpha=\arcsin0.200=0.2014\) rad. Small-pulley wrap is 2.739 rad or 156.9°. Exact pitch length is \(2(293.94)+\pi(140)+2(60)(0.2014)=1052\) mm. With \(\mu=0.300\) the limiting tension ratio is \(e^{0.300(2.739)}=2.27\), lower than the 2.57 obtained from a half-circle wrap.

Second application

Find the minimum shaft spacing for a specified wrap

Another drive uses pitch radii of 75.0 and 25.0 mm and requires at least 160° contact on the smaller pulley. This limits \(\alpha\) to 10.0°, so \(C\geq50.0/\sin10.0^\circ=288\) mm. At this spacing the straight span is \(C\cos10.0^\circ=284\) mm. A proposed spacing of 250 mm would give only \(180^\circ-2\arcsin(50/250)=156.9^\circ\).

Resolve the forces on a small arc

A flat belt transmits torque through friction distributed over the part of the pulley it touches. Cut out a short belt element subtending \(d\theta\). Let tension entering the element be \(T\) and tension leaving be \(T+dT\). For a flexible belt in quasistatic normal equilibrium the two tension vectors have a small inward radial resultant \(T\,d\theta\). The pulley supplies an outward normal force of the same magnitude. Neglect belt bending stiffness and centrifugal tension in the following low-speed model.

At impending gross slip, available tangential friction is \(\mu\,dN\). Tangential equilibrium gives \(dT=\mu T\,d\theta\) along the direction of increasing belt tension. Integrate from the slack to the tight end of a contact arc of angle \(\beta\) in radians. Below impending slip the resulting ratio is an inequality.

\[\ln(T_1/T_2)\le\mu\beta,\qquad T_1/T_2\le e^{\mu\beta},\qquad P=(T_1-T_2)v.\]

The final power expression comes from transmitted torque \((T_1-T_2)r\) times angular velocity \(v/r\). It is the tension difference that drives the machine. Both tensions contribute to radial loading on the shafts. In an open belt drive the smaller pulley often has the smaller contact angle and controls traction first.

Compute the least operating tensions

A flat belt must transmit 2.0 kW at 8.0 m/s. It wraps through 160° on the limiting pulley, with effective friction coefficient 0.30. The required tension difference is 250 N. Converting 160° to 2.793 rad gives a maximum ratio \(r_T=e^{0.30(2.793)}=2.31\). At the limiting ratio \(T_2=250/(r_T-1)=191\,\mathrm N\) and \(T_1=441\,\mathrm N\). Lower slack-side tension would require a ratio beyond the assumed friction limit.

For negligible centrifugal effect and approximately equal span compliances, an initial pretension estimate is \(T_0=(T_1+T_2)/2=316\,\mathrm N\) per span. If the two straight spans pull nearly parallel, the resultant transverse shaft force is approximately \(T_1+T_2=632\,\mathrm N\). If their directions differ appreciably, add the two tension vectors rather than their magnitudes. A higher pretension improves the available traction margin while increasing load on bearings and belt cords.

Decide what a loss of friction does

If oil contamination reduces the friction coefficient to 0.20 at the same wrap, the limiting tension ratio falls to \(e^{0.20(2.793)}=1.75\). If the tension sum remains 632 N, the largest transferable difference is \(632(r_T-1)/(r_T+1)\simeq172\,\mathrm N\), giving at most 1.38 kW at 8 m/s. The original power demand now exceeds available traction. A larger contact angle, different belt design or altered surface condition may address the shortfall; increasing motor power alone does not change the traction bound. A toothed timing belt has a different transmission mechanism and requires its tooth engagement and cord loading to be evaluated separately.

Interpret belt speed

The pitch-line speed in the no-slip approximation is \(\pi d n/60\). A 200 mm pulley carrying the original 8 m/s belt turns at approximately 764 rpm. Speed should be measured on both shafts if slipping contact is suspected.

Include the tension needed to turn a moving belt

The quasistatic capstan law assumes the belt has negligible inertia as it changes direction round a pulley. At higher speed, each arc element of linear mass density \(\lambda\) needs inward centripetal force. Consider a segment subtending \(d\theta\) at radius \(r\). Its mass is \(\lambda r\,d\theta\) and required inward force is \(\lambda v^2 d\theta\). Radial equilibrium of the segment gives \(T\,d\theta-dN=\lambda v^2 d\theta\), where \(dN\) is the outward reaction from the pulley. Available traction thus depends on the effective tension \(T-T_c\), with \(T_c=\lambda v^2\).

At incipient sliding \(dT=\mu(T-T_c)d\theta\) for constant belt speed. Integrating gives \((T_1-T_c)/(T_2-T_c)=e^{\mu\beta}\). If the allowable total tight-side tension is \(T_{\max}\) and wrap and friction are fixed, the maximum tension difference is \((T_{\max}-\lambda v^2)(1-e^{-\mu\beta})\). Multiplying by \(v\) yields a cubic in speed for ideal power capacity:

\[P_{\max}(v)=(1-e^{-\mu\beta})(T_{\max}v-\lambda v^3).\]

The derivative vanishes at \(v_*=\sqrt{T_{\max}/(3\lambda)}\), where centrifugal tension consumes one third of the allowed total tight-side tension. This optimum presumes adequate pretension, fixed allowable tension independent of speed, and acceptable bending and thermal losses. It should not be applied without checking the belt and pulley operating limits.

Choose between increasing shaft speed and increasing wrap

For an illustrative belt \(T_{\max}=700\,\mathrm N,\ \lambda=0.25\,\mathrm{kg/m},\ \mu=0.30,\ \beta=\pi\) rad. The traction factor is \(1-e^{-0.30\pi}=0.610\). At 20 m/s centrifugal tension is \(0.25(20)^2=100\,\mathrm N\), giving limiting tension difference \(0.610(700-100)=366\,\mathrm N\) and deliverable power 7.32 kW. At the ideal optimum \(v_*=\sqrt{700/0.75}=30.55\,\mathrm{m/s}\), centrifugal tension is 233.3 N, difference 284.7 N and power 8.70 kW. Extra speed initially helps even as the available tension difference falls.

At 40 m/s centrifugal tension becomes 400 N and capacity falls back to \(0.610(300)(40)=7.32\,\mathrm{kW}\). Thus two pulley speeds can deliver the same calculated limiting power with very different running tensions and bending-cycle rates. Increasing wrap from 180° to 210° at 20 m/s instead changes the traction factor to \(1-e^{-0.30(3.665)}=0.667\), raising calculated capacity to 8.00 kW without higher speed. An added idler can supply this wrap but also introduces another bend and bearing load. The choice should follow the limiting failure mechanism: speed-dependent belt fatigue, inadequate traction margin and shaft overloading respond differently to the two modifications.

The effect of an allowable tension change is also nonlinear when speed is chosen optimally. Substituting \(v_*\) gives optimum ideal power proportional to \(T_{\max}^{3/2}/\sqrt\lambda\) at fixed wrap and friction. A reinforcement that increases both allowable tension and mass density must be assessed using both factors. For example, raising \(T_{\max}\) by 20% while increasing \(\lambda\) by 44% gives only \(1.2^{3/2}/\sqrt{1.44}=1.095\), or about 9.5% more ideal peak power. A heavier belt can also impose greater centrifugal tension at an existing pulley speed, so the actual duty point may benefit less than this re-optimised maximum.

The characteristic speed should consequently be recalculated for the proposed belt construction at its actual working radius.

Find the slack-side tension needed to transmit a force

A flat belt must transmit a 200 N difference between tight- and slack-side tensions. At impending slip \(T_t/T_s=e^{\mu\theta}\). Eliminate \(T_t\) to find the minimum positive slack-side tension for the required difference.

Read values from the plot

Wrap angle (degrees)Friction coefficient 0.18Friction coefficient 0.28Friction coefficient 0.38
