Problem sheet 4 · Elastic springs and belt traction

Estimate stiffness, stress and belt tensions and identify the geometric assumptions controlling each calculation.

A return spring at its working point

A close-coiled steel spring has wire diameter 3 mm, mean coil diameter 21 mm, seven active turns and \(G=79\,\mathrm{GPa}\). Find its rate, deflection and stored energy at 50 N. Calculate peak shear using the Wahl factor.

Reveal the worked solution

The rate is \(79000(3^4)/[8(21^3)(7)]=12.34\,\mathrm{N/mm}\), giving 4.05 mm deflection and \(50(0.00405)/2=0.101\,\mathrm J\). With \(C=7\), \(K_w=1.213\). Corrected shear is \(1.213[8(50)(21)/(\pi3^3)]=120\,\mathrm{MPa}\).

Combine two different springs

Springs with rates 18 and 30 N/mm are installed with negligible preload. Determine equivalent rate and travel of each at total force 90 N when in series. Then put them in parallel and find force carried by each at a 3 mm common compression.

Reveal the worked solution

In series the same force acts in both. Their compressions are \(90/18=5.0\,\mathrm{mm}\) and \(90/30=3.0\,\mathrm{mm}\); effective rate is \(90/8=11.25\,\mathrm{N/mm}\). In parallel the common deflection gives 54 and 90 N, a total 144 N and equivalent rate 48 N/mm. The active constraint is equal force in the first layout and equal travel in the second.

A reduction in wrap

A flat belt operates at 6 m/s with total span tension 600 N and friction coefficient 0.28. Estimate maximum power at a 180° wrap and again at a 120° wrap. Assume fixed sum of span tensions and negligible centrifugal tension.

Reveal the worked solution

At limiting ratio \(r=e^{\mu\beta}\), the tension difference is \(600(r-1)/(r+1)\). The respective ratios are 2.410 and 1.798, giving differences 248.1 and 171.1 N. The corresponding maximum powers are 1.49 and 1.03 kW. The force pressing the shaft can remain similar even as the available power limit falls.

Chapter 9 · Friction clutches, braking torque and heat

Integrate annular contact traction and connect the torque needed to synchronise rotating bodies with their frictional heat release.

From first principles

Obtain friction torque by integrating the pressure over an annulus

A dry clutch or disc brake transmits torque through normal pressure and frictional traction over its contact faces. For one axisymmetric annular face spanning radii \(r_i\) to \(r_o\), a ring of radial width \(dr\) has area \(2\pi r\,dr\). Let \(p(r)\) be its pressure and take constant friction coefficient \(\mu\) during slip. The ring carries normal force \(dW=p\,dA\) and contributes moment \(\mu r\,dW\). For \(z\) equally loaded active faces the total is

\[W=2\pi\int_{r_i}^{r_o}p(r)r\,dr,\]
\[T=2\pi z\mu\int_{r_i}^{r_o}p(r)r^2\,dr=z\mu W r_e .\]

The effective radius is a pressure-weighted mean lever arm. For uniform pressure, integrate the powers of \(r\) and cancel the pressure between numerator and denominator.

\[r_e=\frac{2}{3}\frac{r_o^3-r_i^3}{r_o^2-r_i^2}.\]

For a uniform-wear model, local sliding speed scales with radius and the pressure model is \(p(r)r=\text{constant}\); integration gives \(r_e=(r_o+r_i)/2\). During relative rotation the frictional heating rate is \(T|\omega_1-\omega_2|\). It changes as the two faces synchronise.

Derivation and applications

Predict stopping angle when a brake also sees a driving torque

A brake on a driven indexing shaft may oppose both stored rotational energy and continued motor work. Let the combined inertia referred to the braking shaft be \(J\) and its initial angular speed \(\omega_0>0\). During the stopping interval assume constant brake torque magnitude \(T_b\), constant driving torque \(T_d\) in the original direction, and negligible other losses. For a stop to occur the opposing torque must exceed the drive. Angular momentum balance gives the deceleration and stopping time.

\[J\dot\omega=T_d-T_b,\]
\[t_s=\frac{J\omega_0}{T_b-T_d}.\]

During uniform deceleration the average speed is \(\omega_0/2\). Integrating speed over the stop yields angular travel. Multiply the brake torque by that angle to obtain the heat generated at the brake interface, assuming the brake member attached to ground is stationary.

\[\theta_s=\frac{J\omega_0^2}{2(T_b-T_d)},\]
\[Q_b=T_b\theta_s.\]

The energy balance exposes the extra term hidden when heat is identified with the initial rotor energy alone. Positive work from the still-active drive increases both the angle and brake heat as the torque margin shrinks.

\[Q_b=\frac12J\omega_0^2+T_d\theta_s=\frac12J\omega_0^2\frac{T_b}{T_b-T_d}.\]

These results apply only until speed reaches zero. Whether the shaft remains stationary afterwards depends on the brake's static torque capacity and the drive command. Temperature-dependent friction during the stop would also alter the assumed constant torque.

Worked example

Quantify the heat from a conveyor stop

Take \(J=0.800\) kg m², \(\omega_0=15.0\) rad/s, \(T_b=18.0\) N m and \(T_d=6.00\) N m. Net deceleration is 15.0 rad/s², stopping time 1.00 s and angle 7.50 rad. The brake absorbs \(18.0(7.50)=135\) J, consisting of 90.0 J initial kinetic energy and 45.0 J additional drive work. Cutting the drive before braking would avoid that extra energy.

Second application

Size a brake from allowable travel

In another indexer \(J=0.300\) kg m², \(\omega_0=20.0\) rad/s and residual drive torque is 4.00 N m. To stop within 2.50 rad requires \(T_b\geq T_d+J\omega_0^2/(2\theta_s)=28.0\) N m. At this threshold the stop takes 0.250 s and generates 70.0 J of heat. The motor contributes 10.0 J of that amount while the shaft turns through the permitted angle.

Each ring acts through a different lever arm

A single-plate clutch uses an axial force to transmit torque across annular friction interfaces. Assume axisymmetric pressure \(p(r)\) with inner radius \(r_i\), outer radius \(r_o\) and friction coefficient \(\mu\). A narrow ring has normal force \(dN=p(r)2\pi r\,dr\). When slipping with fully mobilised friction, it transmits torque \(dT=\mu r\,dN\). The normal force on each active interface is the axial clamp force \(W\) in an ordinary axial stack; the total torque capacity adds over the number \(z\) of active interfaces.

For uniform pressure integrate the normal force and torque separately, then eliminate pressure. For the idealised uniform-wear law the wear rate scales with \(pv\). Since sliding speed is proportional to \(r\), equal wear implies \(p(r)r\) constant. Integration gives two different effective radii.

\[T=z\mu W r_e,\qquad r_{e,p}=\frac{2}{3}\frac{r_o^3-r_i^3}{r_o^2-r_i^2},\qquad r_{e,w}=(r_o+r_i)/2 .\]

Uniform pressure is a useful model for a new ideal face; uniform wear models a different radial distribution. Both depend on alignment and the ability of the backing plates to distribute load. The distinction is greater for a wide annulus than for a narrow ring.

Determine the relative speed decay

During engagement two rotors initially have angular speeds \(\omega_1,\omega_2\) and inertias \(I_1,I_2\). If external torques are negligible during a short engagement, angular momentum conservation gives the common final speed. Friction does equal and opposite angular work on the two rotors, but at different instantaneous speeds while they slip. Their net mechanical energy decreases. Eliminating the final speed from the energy difference gives

\[\omega_f=\frac{I_1\omega_1+I_2\omega_2}{I_1+I_2},\qquad E_{\rm heat}=\tfrac12 I_{\rm red}(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2,\quad I_{\rm red}=\frac{I_1I_2}{I_1+I_2}.\]

For a constant slipping torque \(T\), relative speed decreases at \(T/I_{\rm red}\) until synchronisation. Increasing clamp force shortens the engagement but does not reduce this energy at the same initial speeds in the isolated model. It increases the heat generation rate and can alter peak surface temperature.

Check one start and repeated operation

Let \(I_1=0.20,\ I_2=0.30\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\) and starting speeds be 100 and 0 rad/s. Then \(\omega_f=40\,\mathrm{rad/s}\), \(I_{\rm red}=0.12\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\) and heat release is 600 J. For \(r_i=40,\ r_o=80\,\mathrm{mm}\), \(z=2\), \(\mu=0.30\), \(W=1000\,\mathrm N\) and uniform wear, the torque is \(2(0.30)(1000)(0.060)=36\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). Synchronisation takes \(0.12(100)/36=0.333\,\mathrm s\). The mean heat generation over that interval is 1.80 kW. Six identical starts per minute give an average of 60 W over a complete operating cycle. These two rates govern different aspects of cooling and surface temperature; an application with an actively driven motor during engagement requires its external work to be included.

Check the pressure at the inner radius

Under uniform wear \(pr=C\) and \(W=2\pi C(r_o-r_i)\). At the stated load and radii the maximum pressure occurs at the inner edge and equals \(1000/[2\pi(0.080-0.040)(0.040)]=99.5\,\mathrm{kPa}\). An average face pressure alone would hide this radial concentration.

Derive the peak temperature under repeated braking

A mean heat load sets the long-time cooling requirement, while peak friction temperature may control a brake lining. A simple intermediate model treats the thermally active rotor and lining as a uniform heat capacity \(C\) connected to ambient by conductance \(H\). Let \(\theta=T-T_\infty\). For the heat rate \(P(t)\) actually entering this lump, \(C\dot\theta=P-H\theta\) with thermal time \(\tau=C/H\). The generated friction heat may be shared between two bodies; use the fraction entering the modelled part or include both bodies in the same effective lump only when their temperatures justify that choice.

For a repeat duty with constant heat rate \(P\) over braking time \(t_b\), then zero heat input over cooling time \(t_c\), integrate each constant-input segment. Writing \(a=e^{-t_b/\tau}\), \(b=e^{-t_c/\tau}\) gives \(\theta_{\rm peak}=a\theta_{\rm start}+(P/H)(1-a)\) and \(\theta_{\rm next}=b\theta_{\rm peak}\). At cyclic equilibrium next equals start, so

\[\theta_{\rm peak}=\frac{P}{H}\frac{1-a}{1-ab},\qquad \theta_{\min}=b\theta_{\rm peak}.\]

Integrating the balance over one full cycle independently gives mean rise \(\bar\theta=(P/H)t_b/(t_b+t_c)\), because stored energy returns to its starting value. The peak depends on capacity \(C\), although the mean is fixed by \(H\) and time-averaged power. A high-conductivity rotor with ample time for diffusion may fit a lumped model reasonably; very short contact events can create local flash temperatures far above the bulk estimate.

Distinguish larger thermal mass from better cooling

A test rotor receives 6000 J every minute, distributed approximately uniformly over a 10 s braking interval. Use effective rotor heat capacity \(C=800\,\mathrm{J/K}\), conductance \(H=4.0\,\mathrm{W/K}\) and ambient temperature 25 °C. During braking \(P=600\,\mathrm W\); during the next 50 s it is zero. The time constant is 200 s. For periodic operation \(a=e^{-0.05}=0.9512,\ b=e^{-0.25}=0.7788\), giving peak rise \(150(1-0.9512)/(1-e^{-0.30})=28.23\) K and minimum rise \(0.7788(28.23)=21.98\) K. The rotor cycles between approximately 47.0 and 53.2 °C, while the mean is 50 °C.

The first stop from ambient raises the rotor by only \(150(1-e^{-0.05})=7.32\) K. A single-stop test is therefore poor evidence for repeated-duty thermal margin. Doubling rotor heat capacity while keeping cooling unchanged changes \(\tau\) to 400 s. The periodic peak rise becomes \(150(1-e^{-0.025})/(1-e^{-0.15})=26.59\) K, but the mean rise stays 25 K. By contrast, doubling conductance would halve the mean rise under the same duty. Added thermal mass mainly reduces the ripple and slows warmup; stronger heat removal lowers the long-term mean as well. Actual fans and convection coefficients can vary with rotor speed during a stop and should be represented if the measured cooldown deviates systematically from one exponential.

Thermal approach to periodic operation follows directly from the update. The difference from the steady-cycle temperature at the same point of each cycle is multiplied by \(ab=e^{-60/200}=0.741\) every minute. Reaching within 5% of that periodic offset therefore takes about ten cycles. A test of only two or three stops will still be dominated by warmup.

Chapter 10 · Hydrodynamic lubrication and sliding bearings

Derive the flow balance in a converging lubricant film and determine why the sliding surface can support a load without solid contact.

From first principles

Derive the flux through a moving lubricant film

Consider two broad bearing surfaces separated by a thin gap \(h\). Let the lower surface move at speed \(U\) and the upper one remain still. Assume incompressible Newtonian lubricant, no slip, negligible inertia and a gap changing only slowly in the streamwise direction. Pressure is nearly constant through the thickness. Locally, the streamwise momentum balance reduces to a pressure gradient balanced by viscous shear curvature.

\[\mu\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial y^2}=\frac{dp}{dx},\]
\[u(0)=U,\]
\[u(h)=0 .\]

Integrating twice gives a linear velocity contribution from sliding and a parabolic contribution from the pressure gradient. Integrating that velocity across the gap gives the discharge per unit width.

\[u(y)=U(1-y/h)-\frac{1}{2\mu}\frac{dp}{dx}y(h-y),\]
\[q=\frac{Uh}{2}-\frac{h^3}{12\mu}\frac{dp}{dx}.\]

In a steady film without leakage or normal surface motion the same \(q\) crosses every section. A varying gap changes the sliding contribution, forcing a pressure gradient to preserve that flow. The integral of gauge pressure over the bearing pad gives its normal load capacity.

Derivation and applications

Relate recess pressure to restrictor resistance in a hydrostatic pad

A hydrostatic bearing can sustain load at zero sliding speed because an external supply drives lubricant through a restriction. Consider a flat circular thrust pad with a central recess of radius \(a\) and outer land radius \(b\). Let the parallel land clearance \(h\) be uniform and much smaller than its radial extent. Use incompressible Newtonian oil of viscosity \(\mu\), steady laminar radial outflow, no squeeze motion, ambient gauge pressure at \(b\), and negligible pressure drop inside the recess. The pressure-driven flow per circumferential width follows the parallel-plate law.

\[Q=-2\pi r\frac{h^3}{12\mu}\frac{dp}{dr},\]
\[p(r)=\frac{6\mu Q}{\pi h^3}\ln\frac br .\]

At the recess edge \(p(a)=p_r\). The land therefore behaves as a flow resistance \(R_L\) proportional to the inverse cube of clearance. A linear feed restrictor \(R_f\) drops supply pressure \(p_s\) to \(p_r\). Equal flow passes through the feed and land, giving a hydraulic pressure divider.

\[R_L=\frac{6\mu}{\pi h^3}\ln(b/a),\]
\[p_r=Q R_L=p_s\frac{R_L}{R_f+R_L}.\]

Integrate pressure over the uniform recess and logarithmic land. The logarithm integral contains an inner-boundary term that cancels the associated part of the recess load, leaving a simple effective area.

\[W=\pi a^2p_r+2\pi\int_a^b r p(r)\,dr=\frac{\pi(b^2-a^2)}{2\ln(b/a)}p_r .\]

Closing the pad increases land resistance, raises recess pressure and increases force opposing closure. When the land resistance is already very large, recess pressure approaches supply pressure and the available load saturates.

Worked example

Predict load at a balanced restriction

For \(a=10.0\) mm and \(b=20.0\) mm, effective area is \(\pi(0.0200^2-0.0100^2)/(2\ln2)=6.80\times10^{-4}\) m². If \(R_L=R_f\) at the operating clearance and \(p_s=0.800\) MPa, recess pressure is 0.400 MPa and supported load is 272 N. This load is present without any relative sliding of the two faces.

Second application

Infer a clearance change from pressure

A different bearing has \(p_r/p_s=0.600\) initially and 0.750 after a load change. Resistance ratios \(R_L/R_f=p_r/(p_s-p_r)\) therefore increase from 1.50 to 3.00. Since land resistance varies as \(h^{-3}\), the final gap is \(2^{-1/3}=0.794\) of its initial value. An initial 25.0 µm film becomes 19.8 µm, assuming unchanged oil viscosity and feed resistance.

Where lubricant pressure comes from

A shaft turning inside a plain bearing drags lubricant around with its surface. A load displaces the journal from the bore centre, creating a film whose gap narrows in one direction. Wall motion carries oil into this wedge, and a pressure gradient redistributes the flow so the same fluid rate passes successive sections. Pressure integrated over the bearing can then support a load. Surface motion alone in a uniform gap between surfaces with equal end pressures cannot produce this load in the ideal steady model.

Consider a broad slider bearing rather than the full journal geometry. Let a lower wall move at speed \(U\); a fixed upper wall forms a slowly varying small gap \(h(x)\). Assume incompressible Newtonian lubricant, constant viscosity, negligible inertia and negligible leakage through the sides. Pressure is nearly uniform across the thickness. The axial momentum equation reduces to \(\mu d^2u/dy^2=dp/dx\), and no slip gives \(u(0)=U,\ u(h)=0\).

\[u=U(1-y/h)-\frac{1}{2\mu}\frac{dp}{dx}\,y(h-y),\qquad q=\frac{Uh}{2}-\frac{h^3}{12\mu}\frac{dp}{dx}.\]

Mass conservation makes \(q\) per unit width constant along the bearing. Where the local dragged flow \(Uh/2\) exceeds \(q\), pressure rises downstream. Where it falls short, pressure falls and supplies a forward pressure-driven contribution. The transition locates a pressure maximum.

Solve a two-depth ideal bearing

Consider a stepped pad with two equal 10 mm long regions, gaps \(h_1=0.20\) and \(h_2=0.10\,\mathrm{mm}\) in the sliding direction. Both ends have ambient pressure, \(U=1.0\,\mathrm{m/s}\), and \(\mu=0.05\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}\). Ignore the local loss at the abrupt step and treat each region as fully developed. Integration of \(dp/dx=12\mu(Uh/2-q)/h^3\) across both regions gives zero net pressure change. With equal lengths this fixes

\[q=\frac U2\frac{h_1^{-2}+h_2^{-2}}{h_1^{-3}+h_2^{-3}}=5.56\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^2/s}.\]

In the first region \(Uh_1/2=1.00\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\). The positive pressure gradient is \(3.33\times10^6\,\mathrm{Pa/m}\), giving an interface gauge pressure of 33.3 kPa. The second region has an equal opposite gradient. The resulting triangular pressure distribution integrates to a force per unit width of \(0.5(0.020)(33333)=333\,\mathrm{N/m}\). A nominal 50 mm width would thus support approximately 16.7 N under the negligible-side-leakage assumption.

Understand failure at low speed

With zero sliding speed, equal end pressures imply zero steady gauge pressure everywhere in this model, so the load capacity vanishes. Start-up and shutdown therefore differ from established hydrodynamic running. A separately pressurised hydrostatic bearing can carry load at zero shaft speed. If a solution predicts large negative absolute pressures in a diverging region, actual oil may cavitate and the full-film boundary conditions must change. Viscous heating can also lower the local viscosity. Film thickness, thermal balance and supply conditions all matter when transferring the simple slider result to a journal in service.

Predict a viscosity change

For the same slider geometry and speed with equal prescribed end pressures, the calculated flux is independent of viscosity but all gauge pressure gradients are proportional to viscosity. Halving viscosity in the ideal stepped example therefore halves its load capacity at unchanged film thickness.

Derive load carried by a closing lubricant film

Sliding motion is not the only way to develop pressure in a thin film. Two parallel circular plates moving towards each other expel viscous oil radially, producing a squeeze-film pressure. Let plate radius be \(R\), uniform gap \(h(t)\), and approach speed \(v=-dh/dt>0\). Assume incompressible Newtonian oil, no slip, negligible inertia, rigid parallel plates, negligible edge resistance and ambient pressure at the rim. The gap is much smaller than the radius. Neither plate moves radially, so depth-integrated radial flux per unit circumference is \(q_r=-h^3(dp/dr)/(12\mu)\), the stationary-wall pressure-driven slit relation.

Inside radius \(r\), incompressibility requires outward flow through its circumference to equal the volume displaced by plate motion: \(2\pi r q_r=\pi r^2v\). Thus \(q_r=rv/2\) and \(dp/dr=-6\mu rv/h^3\). Integrating inward from ambient at the edge gives

\[p(r)-p_a=\frac{3\mu v}{h^3}(R^2-r^2),\qquad W=\int_0^R(p-p_a)2\pi r\,dr=\frac{3\pi\mu R^4v}{2h^3}.\]

The maximum pressure is at the centre, twice the area average because the pressure shape is parabolic. The load grows very steeply as the gap narrows at imposed speed. At imposed load, however, the approach slows. This distinction mirrors the need to separate displacement-controlled and force-controlled contact tests. A stationary gap with no relative normal motion produces no squeeze-generated pressure under the stated ambient boundary condition.

Estimate the time for a thrust pad to approach its stop

A circular pad of radius 25 mm settles under a constant additional normal force of 100 N on oil of dynamic viscosity \(0.15\,\mathrm{Pa\,s}\). Ignore inertia after the short initial transient. Rearranging the load law gives \(-dh/dt=2Wh^3/(3\pi\mu R^4)\). Between initial and final gaps, integration gives \(t=[3\pi\mu R^4/(4W)](h_f^{-2}-h_i^{-2})\). For \(h_i=150\,\mathrm{\mu m}\) and \(h_f=50\,\mathrm{\mu m}\) this yields 0.491 s.

At an intermediate 100 micrometre gap the instantaneous speed is \(2(100)(10^{-4})^3/[3\pi(0.15)(0.025)^4]=0.000362\,\mathrm{m/s}\). The centre gauge pressure is \(2W/(\pi R^2)=102\,\mathrm{kPa}\), independent of gap here because the load is prescribed and the approach speed adapts. Halving the gap at unchanged load reduces approach speed eightfold. The ideal solution takes infinite time to reach zero thickness, which should not be interpreted as assurance against surface contact: roughness, oil leakage paths, tilt and elasticity enter before an arbitrarily small gap is reached. On separation the sign of the calculated pressure reverses and cavitation or air ingress may prevent the predicted tensile liquid pressure from developing.

A load-cell test can independently discriminate squeeze action from simple dry sliding resistance. At fixed small gap and sufficiently slow normal motion the predicted force is proportional to approach speed, with a slope scaling as \(h^{-3}\). Closing the gap from 100 to 80 micrometres should multiply the force slope by \((100/80)^3=1.95\). The transient test must either measure instantaneous gap or be run with an imposed motion, since holding load constant causes speed and gap to evolve together. Comparing forces at the same clock time on two different tests would not isolate the gap dependence.

Problem sheet 5 · Friction work and fluid films

Relate an engagement event to thermal loading and use film-flow continuity to infer pressure gradients.

A stopped flywheel

A flywheel of inertia 0.80 kg m² initially turns at 60 rad/s and receives a constant braking torque of 12 N m. Find stopping time, heat and total revolutions during braking. If the same stop is repeated every minute, what is the cycle-average heat load?

Reveal the worked solution

Angular deceleration is \(12/0.80=15\,\mathrm{rad/s^2}\), so stopping takes 4.0 s. Heat equals the initial kinetic energy \(0.5(0.80)(60)^2=1440\,\mathrm J\). Mean speed is 30 rad/s and travel is 120 rad, or 19.1 revolutions. One stop every 60 s averages 24 W despite the much larger power during a stop.

Two faces, two pressure assumptions

A clutch has two active faces, 30 and 90 mm inner and outer radii, axial clamp 800 N, and friction coefficient 0.25. Find torque capacities for uniform wear and uniform pressure. A drive requires 25 N m. How does the choice of model affect the result?

Reveal the worked solution

Uniform wear gives \(r_e=60\,\mathrm{mm}\) and torque \(2(0.25)(800)(0.060)=24.0\,\mathrm{N\,m}\). Uniform pressure gives \(r_e=(2/3)(90^3-30^3)/(90^2-30^2)=65\,\mathrm{mm}\), yielding 26.0 N m. Only the pressure model nominally reaches the required torque. Wear distribution and tolerances matter for such a small margin.

Infer a pressure gradient from a flow

A slider locally has oil-film thickness 0.15 mm, viscosity 0.040 Pa s, lower-surface speed 2.0 m/s and measured flow per width \(1.20\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\). Find the pressure gradient and its sign. In a second region the gap is 0.10 mm at the same flow. Is the pressure still rising?

Reveal the worked solution

Use \(dp/dx=12\mu(Uh/2-q)/h^3\). The first dragged flow is \(1.50\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\), giving \(+4.27\times10^6\,\mathrm{Pa/m}\). At the smaller gap the dragged flow is \(1.00\times10^{-4}\), giving \(-9.60\times10^6\,\mathrm{Pa/m}\). The pressure maximum lies between the two regimes in a smooth converging film.

Chapter 11 · Eccentrically loaded weld groups

Resolve an eccentric in-plane force into direct shear and torsional shear and find the combined nominal throat demand.

From first principles

Distribute an eccentric force across a weld group's throat

Idealise each thin weld by its effective throat times its length. For constant throat \(t\) and total weld length \(L\), the load-carrying area is \(A=tL\). Suppose a planar force \(\mathbf F\) acts at offset \(\mathbf e\) from the throat-area centroid. Resolve it into the same force through the centroid and an additional torque \(M_z=(\mathbf e\times\mathbf F)_z\). In a nominal elastic weld-group model the direct force produces uniform shear.

\[\boldsymbol\tau_d=\frac{\mathbf F}{tL},\]
\[M_z=(\mathbf e\times\mathbf F)_z .\]

Small rigid rotation of the attached plate motivates a torsional shear distribution proportional to distance \(r\) from the centroid and tangential to the radius. Its scale follows from requiring the integral of moment to equal \(M_z\).

\[J_t=t\int r^2\,ds,\]
\[\boldsymbol\tau_M=\frac{M_z}{J_t}\,\mathbf e_z\times\mathbf r,\]
\[\boldsymbol\tau=\boldsymbol\tau_d+\boldsymbol\tau_M .\]

The direct and torsional contributions add as vectors. The largest nominal magnitude may therefore occur where their directions most nearly align. The calculation estimates throat demand from the overall load path; local notch stresses require a more detailed model.

Derivation and applications

Locate the elastic centre of an unequal weld group

An eccentric force produces a torsional shear flow proportional to distance from the weld-group centre. When throat thickness varies, the centre must account for the differing load-carrying areas. Consider narrow weld lines in one plane, with local effective throat \(t(s)\) measured normal to the nominal load-bearing section. Assume identical material properties and linear elastic shear, uniform through each throat. The area element is \(dA=t(s)ds\). Define centroid \(\mathbf c\) so the first area moment about it vanishes.

\[A_t=\int t\,ds,\]
\[\mathbf c=\frac{\int\mathbf r\,t\,ds}{A_t},\]
\[\int(\mathbf r-\mathbf c)t\,ds=0.\]

An in-plane force \(\mathbf F\) through the centroid gives uniform direct shear \(\mathbf F/A_t\). For torque \(M\) about the centroid take a shear field proportional to the local tangential displacement of a small rigid rotation. Its integral is zero by the centroid condition. Equating its moment to the applied torque fixes its scale.

\[J_t=\int|\mathbf r-\mathbf c|^2t\,ds,\]
\[\boldsymbol\tau_M=\frac{M}{J_t}\,\mathbf e_z\times(\mathbf r-\mathbf c).\]

For a straight weld of length \(\ell\) and constant throat \(t\), its contribution to the polar area moment contains a local term and a centroid-offset term. Here \(d\) is the distance from that weld's midpoint to the group centroid.

\[J_{t,j}=t\left(\frac{\ell^3}{12}+\ell d^2\right).\]

The assumptions describe nominal group demand. Local toe geometry and fabrication details can govern fatigue even when this nominal stress is low.

Worked example

Find the centre and torsional resistance of two seams

Two horizontal 100 mm welds lie at \(y=0\) and 60 mm with throats 4.00 and 2.00 mm and both centred at \(x=0\). Their areas are 400 and 200 mm². The centroid is at \(y=20.0\) mm. The local moment terms total \(6(100^3)/12=500{,}000\) mm⁴; the offset terms total \(400(20^2)+200(40^2)=480{,}000\) mm⁴. Thus \(J_t=980{,}000\) mm⁴. The thinner upper seam lies farther from the torsional centre.

Second application

Recover an unknown throat from a measured centroid

Two equal-length parallel seams are 50.0 mm apart. The lower throat is 3.00 mm, and the centroid is known to be 20.0 mm above it. Writing \(20=50t/(3+t)\) gives upper throat \(t=2.00\) mm. For lengths 80.0 mm each, total throat area is 400 mm². A 4.00 kN load whose line passes through the inferred centroid then gives uniform nominal direct shear of 10.0 MPa.

Reduce a weld to a carrying area

A fillet weld joins two plates along a line, but its specified leg length is not the effective area transmitting force. For an ideal equal-leg 45° fillet, the shortest distance from the root to the theoretical weld face is the throat \(t=s/\sqrt{2}\), where \(s\) is leg length. Effective throat area is \(t\) times effective weld length under the simplified model. End details and local weld defects require separate assessment.

Consider a bracket carrying an in-plane force whose line of action misses the weld group centroid. Move the force to that centroid and retain its moment \(M_z=Fe\). The direct force gives a uniform elastic shear flow vector \(q_d=F/\sum L\) under the line-weld approximation. The moment demands another shear flow, tangent about the centroid. For a rigid attachment and uniform throat and elastic response, assume its magnitude is proportional to radius \(r\), as expected from a small relative rotation.

\[J_L=\int_{\rm welds}r^2\,ds,\qquad q_m(r)=\frac{M_z r}{J_L},\qquad \boldsymbol{\tau}=(\mathbf q_d+\mathbf q_m)/t .\]

The line property \(J_L\) has units of length cubed, since weld length rather than throat area is integrated. For an unequal throat one must weight the centroid and polar property with the appropriate area or stiffness. Add direct and moment contributions as vectors at each point before locating maximum stress.

Calculate a corner stress

Take two vertical parallel welds of length 80 mm each, centred at \(x=\pm30\,\mathrm{mm}\). Their endpoints lie at \(y=\pm40\,\mathrm{mm}\). An upward force \(F=4.0\,\mathrm{kN}\) acts at \(x=+100\,\mathrm{mm}\). Its equivalent counterclockwise moment is 400,000 N mm. The total line length is 160 mm, so the direct flow is 25 N/mm upwards. The group line polar property is

\[J_L=2[80(30)^2+80^3/12]=229333\,\mathrm{mm^3}.\]

At the upper right corner the moment-induced flow vector is \(M_z(-y,x)/J_L=(-69.8,52.3)\,\mathrm{N/mm}\). Adding the direct contribution gives \((-69.8,77.3)\,\mathrm{N/mm}\), magnitude 104.2 N/mm. By symmetry the lower right corner has the same magnitude; the left endpoints experience partial cancellation of the vertical components. For an effective throat 4 mm, the maximum nominal combined shear is 26.0 MPa.

Decide what changes the joint demand

If the force passes through the centroid the moment vanishes and the throat stress is only \(25/4=6.25\,\mathrm{MPa}\). Increasing throat lowers nominal stress but leaves the line shear flow required by equilibrium unchanged. Moving the force closer to the attachment or spreading the welds farther apart can reduce the moment-induced flow. A transverse load normal to the plate introduces bending or prying and would require a different weld-group analysis. Fatigue at the weld toe depends on local geometry and the applicable welded-detail category, so the present nominal elastic calculation alone cannot predict a fatigue life.

Recover a leg dimension

A 4 mm ideal throat corresponds to a theoretical equal leg of \(4\sqrt2=5.66\,\mathrm{mm}\). A drawing that specifies a 4 mm leg instead leaves only a 2.83 mm theoretical throat, raising the calculated nominal maximum stress from 26.0 to about 36.8 MPa.

Turn a weld-group force history into a detail stress range

The static line-weld model produces a shear-flow vector for a given applied load. A fluctuating load introduces a separate fatigue question. In common nominal-stress assessment methods, experimental S–N curves for a specific welded detail can already incorporate local weld geometry and residual stresses. Use the exact nominal stress component, range convention, detail classification and life range associated with the test data. Multiplying such a detail stress again by an arbitrary notch factor can count the same local effect twice. Conversely, a smooth-base-metal fatigue curve generally does not represent a welded detail.

For an illustrative single-slope detail curve let \(\Delta s_{\rm ref}\) be the tested stress range at \(N_{\rm ref}\) cycles and exponent \(m\). Constant-amplitude life is \(N=N_{\rm ref}(\Delta s_{\rm ref}/\Delta s)^m\). Under an assumed linear accumulation of damage, a repeated block containing \(n_i\) cycles of each range consumes

\[D_{\rm block}=\sum_i\frac{n_i}{N_{\rm ref}}\left(\frac{\Delta s_i}{\Delta s_{\rm ref}}\right)^m.\]

The stress range is maximum minus minimum stress in the designated component, not its alternating half-amplitude. For a strictly proportional cycle, each local shear component in the preceding elastic model scales with the applied force. If force direction or line of action changes, the most highly stressed location can shift; recompute the vector histories before choosing the assessment component. A mesh-refined local notch stress and a nominal throat stress are different definitions and require corresponding fatigue data.

Compare a static throat check with a cyclic duty decision

Assume the applicable nominal-stress data for an illustrative connection give \(\Delta s_{\rm ref}=70\,\mathrm{MPa}\) at \(N_{\rm ref}=2.0\times10^6\) cycles with \(m=3\) throughout the ranges considered. These are supplied exercise data. A measured local nominal stress range is 35 MPa for 9000 ordinary cycles and 80 MPa for 300 heavy cycles per operating block. Their respective lives are \(2.0\times10^6(70/35)^3=16.0\times10^6\) and \(2.0\times10^6(70/80)^3=1.34\times10^6\) cycles.

Ordinary cycles consume \(9000/(16.0\times10^6)=0.0005625\) per block; heavy cycles consume \(300/(1.34\times10^6)=0.000224\). The sum predicts approximately \(1/0.0007865=1271\) blocks at unit damage. If an unchanged force path permits throat to grow by 20%, the nominal stress ranges scale by \(1/1.2\), and the calculated block life grows by \(1.2^3=1.728\) to about 2196 blocks. That improvement assumes the revised detail retains the same applicable S–N curve and geometry-independent loading distribution. If instead the hot spot shifts into the base-plate toe or a short stiff attachment raises local restraint, throat increase alone will not reproduce this scaling. Testing a representative weld or checking a matching detail category is more informative than increasing nominal strength without tracing the new failure location.

Under the supplied single-slope assumption the equivalent constant range for the same 9300 cycles is \([(9000\times35^3+300\times80^3)/9300]^{1/3}=38.7\,\mathrm{MPa}\) approximately. The arithmetic average range is only 36.45 MPa and underweights the heavy events. Quoting the equivalent range together with its associated cycle count makes the fatigue calculation reproducible.

Chapter 12 · Tolerance stacks and reliability of assemblies

Determine functional clearance by worst-case and statistical stacking, and connect component life probabilities to assembly reliability.

From first principles

Propagate dimensional variation into an assembly clearance

Start with the physical closing dimension of an assembly. Suppose the functional gap is \(g=A-B-C\) and the measured dimensions have stable means and variances. The signed relation comes from geometry, so taking expectations gives the mean gap regardless of independence. For a conservative interval check, the smallest gap pairs the smallest \(A\) with the largest \(B\) and \(C\).

\[\bar g=\bar A-\bar B-\bar C,\]
\[g_{\min}=A_{\min}-B_{\max}-C_{\max}.\]

For a statistical estimate, subtract the means and square the sum of deviations. When component deviations are independent, all mixed expectations vanish. The signs then disappear from the variance because each individual coefficient is squared.

\[\sigma_g^2=\sigma_A^2+\sigma_B^2+\sigma_C^2 .\]

If the independent dimensions are normal, \(g\) is normal and the interference probability is \(P(g<0)=\Phi(-\bar g/\sigma_g)\), with \(\Phi\) the standard normal cumulative distribution. A tolerance interval alone does not supply a standard deviation or distribution. Correlated machining errors leave mixed covariance terms in the squared sum and can increase or reduce the assembly spread.

Derivation and applications

Propagate a shared manufacturing drift through a clearance stack

Two dimensions made on the same fixture can vary together. A clearance depends on their difference, so ignoring covariance can substantially misstate the expected spread. Let \(H\) be an internal dimension and \(S\) the mating external dimension; assembled clearance is \(G=H-S\). Model a shared fixture drift by a zero-mean random variable \(Z\) with standard deviation \(\sigma_Z\). Let \(e_H,e_S\) be independent local deviations, also independent of \(Z\). The sensitivities \(a_H,a_S\) convert drift into dimensional change.

\[H=\bar H+a_H Z+e_H,\]
\[S=\bar S+a_S Z+e_S .\]

Subtract the dimensions before calculating variance. Only the difference between their sensitivities multiplies the shared term. Squaring and averaging eliminates products of independent zero-mean deviations.

\[\bar G=\bar H-\bar S,\]
\[\sigma_G^2=(a_H-a_S)^2\sigma_Z^2+\sigma_{e_H}^2+\sigma_{e_S}^2 .\]

Equivalently use the variance of each measured dimension and their covariance. Matched positive sensitivities cancel common fixture drift, while sensitivities of opposite sign amplify it. These relations require finite variances and the stated correlations; no particular distribution has yet been assumed.

\[\sigma_G^2=\sigma_H^2+\sigma_S^2-2\,\operatorname{Cov}(H,S),\]
\[\operatorname{Cov}(H,S)=a_Ha_S\sigma_Z^2 .\]

For normally distributed deviations the ratio of mean clearance to its standard deviation also gives the normal-tail estimate of interference probability. Pairing components from unrelated fixtures may remove the very covariance measured on matched components.

Worked example

Compare matched and randomly remixed parts

Take \(a_H=a_S=1\), shared drift spread 20.0 µm, and local independent spreads of 6.00 and 8.00 µm. A matched pair has clearance spread \(\sqrt{6^2+8^2}=10.0\) µm. If components are randomly paired from independent fixture drifts, the spread becomes \(\sqrt{6^2+8^2+2(20)^2}=30.0\) µm. For mean clearance 30.0 µm, normally distributed matched pairs have interference probability about 0.135 percent; independent remixed pairs give about 15.9 percent.

Second application

Infer manufacturing correlation from assembly records

Another process measures standard deviations of 12.0 µm for the bore, 10.0 µm for the shaft and 8.00 µm for their assembled clearance. Rearranging the variance identity gives covariance \((12^2+10^2-8^2)/2=90.0\) µm². The inferred correlation coefficient is \(90/(12\times10)=0.750\). A reconstruction using independent component measurements would give 15.6 µm clearance spread, nearly twice the measured value.

Begin with a physical closing dimension

A drawing is useful only if the manufactured pieces can meet in the intended order and provide the required clearance. Choose a positive direction along the assembly and write the closing dimension from a single datum. For a spacer fitted between two shoulders with a washer, suppose the available nominal length is \(H\), the spacer length is \(S\) and the washer thickness is \(W\). The clearance is \(g=H-S-W\). Housing expansion or coating thickness can be included as additional signed terms if they occur in the operating condition being assessed.

With specified independent admissible intervals, the worst-case minimum is \(H_{\min}-S_{\max}-W_{\max}\). Independence is unnecessary for this conservative bound, although correlated dimensions may prevent its most extreme combination from occurring. A statistical treatment asks a different question: given a supported production distribution, what fraction is likely to achieve a required clearance? Manufacturing limits alone do not determine such a distribution.

Propagate measured spreads

Write each dimension as its mean plus a deviation. The mean clearance is the same signed sum of means. For independent dimensions the variance is the sum of their variances because squaring the coefficients removes their signs. More generally, for a dimension vector with covariance matrix \(\Sigma\) and coefficient vector \(a\), \(\sigma_g^2=a^T\Sigma a\). A common machining datum can introduce correlations that either reduce or increase clearance variation.

Assume \(H=50.00\pm0.15,\ S=48.00\pm0.12,\ W=1.70\pm0.09\,\mathrm{mm}\). The nominal clearance is 0.30 mm and limiting clearance interval is \([0.30-0.36,0.30+0.36]=[-0.06,0.66]\,\mathrm{mm}\). Thus the stated tolerances do not guarantee a positive gap. Suppose separately that production measurements support independent, centred normal models with standard deviations 0.050, 0.040 and 0.030 mm, without further sorting. The resulting standard deviation is

\[\sigma_g=\sqrt{0.050^2+0.040^2+0.030^2}=0.0707\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Interference requires clearance below zero, a deviation of \(0.30/0.0707=4.24\) standard deviations. The normal model estimates about \(1.1\times10^{-5}\) of pairs would interfere, roughly eleven per million. This small estimated fraction and the possibility in a worst-case limit calculation address different production questions. An unmeasured mean shift or sorting parts to their tolerance limits can substantially change the probability.

Combine component survival probabilities

Define reliability at an explicit mission duration as the probability that a component performs its required function through that duration. If a machine requires both of two independent components to survive, its reliability is the product. Two bearings each assessed at 0.98 mission survival give \(0.98^2=0.9604\) for both. If two independent drives are genuinely redundant and either can carry the full demand, failure requires both to fail, giving \(1-(1-0.98)^2=0.9996\).

A shared lubricating supply or housing defect can defeat independence in either calculation. Similarly, a clearance that is satisfactory on the day of assembly need not remain satisfactory after thermal growth or wear. Carry the measured operating temperature range into the same dimension chain, and carry the relevant mission duration and shared causes into the reliability calculation. Both methods depend as strongly on the physical definition of success as on the arithmetic.

Estimate a life scale without discarding run-outs

An endurance test may end with some assemblies still functioning. Their exact failure times are unknown, yet they have demonstrated survival up to the stop time. Treating each as an observed failure at that time biases a life estimate downward, while discarding them throws away survival information. For illustration, assume independent, identically produced parts under a common load and a two-parameter Weibull survival law \(R(t)=\exp[-(t/\eta)^\beta]\). Its scale \(\eta\) has units of time and is the lifetime at which survival is \(e^{-1}\), while \(\beta\) sets the hazard shape. Assume \(\beta\) has been established independently for this exercise.

Differentiating survival gives the failure density \(f(t)=(\beta/\eta)(t/\eta)^{\beta-1}R(t)\). The likelihood contribution of a failed part is \(f(t_i)\); that of a right-censored surviving part is \(R(t_j)\). If \(d\) failures occur in the test, collecting all terms depending on \(\eta\) gives \(\ln L=\text{constant}-\beta d\ln\eta-\sum_{\rm all}(t_i/\eta)^\beta\). Differentiating with respect to \(\eta\) gives the maximum-likelihood scale

\[\hat\eta=\left(\frac{\sum_{\rm all}t_i^\beta}{d}\right)^{1/\beta}.\]

The numerator includes tested times of both failed and unfailed specimens, while the denominator counts only failures. With no failures this expression has no finite scale estimate; the data can support a lower bound under additional statistical assumptions. A Weibull model also presumes the tested population and failure mechanism remain relevant to the intended use.

Interpret a six-unit endurance test for a specified mission

Six identical drives are tested under the same continuous load. Three fail at 100, 150 and 200 h; the other three are stopped in working condition at 220 h. Use \(\beta=2\). The sum of exposure times squared is \(100^2+150^2+200^2+3(220^2)=217700\,\mathrm{h^2}\). Dividing by three failures and taking the square root gives \(\hat\eta=269.4\,\mathrm h\). The fitted single-drive survival at 150 h is \(\exp[-(150/269.4)^2]=0.733\). The estimated time at 90% survival is \(\hat\eta\sqrt{-\ln0.90}=87.4\,\mathrm h\). A proposed mission should therefore specify both duration and required probability.

If a machine needs two independently manufactured drives both functioning for the 150 h mission, fitted machine survival is \(0.733^2=0.538\); if either one can take the entire load and failure of one does not affect its companion, it becomes \(1-(1-0.733)^2=0.929\). Neither system calculation includes uncertainty in the fitted scale or the assumed shape, which is substantial in a six-unit sample. If all drives share a contaminant or one common failure mode, independence can also fail. The three run-outs increase the scale relative to using only observed failures, but they do not certify service beyond their observed duration at an arbitrary reliability target.

The hazard inferred under the assumed shape is \(h(t)=2t/\eta^2\), so its value doubles from 100 to 200 h. Replacing the fitted distribution by a constant failure rate calibrated at one time would alter the conditional survival of an already-aged drive. This matters when a maintenance interval concerns parts that have already completed one mission.

Separate a shifted process mean from random assembly scatter

An assembled dimension must fall within 0.15 mm of nominal. Model its error as normal with mean \(b\) and standard deviation \(s\). The yield is the difference of two normal cumulative probabilities at the tolerance limits.

Problem sheet 6 · Weld demand, assembly yield and reliability

Identify how eccentric loading, process variation and common causes change a machine assembly’s limiting condition.

One circular weld

A circular line weld of radius 25 mm and effective throat 3 mm carries an in-plane force of 2.0 kN whose line is offset 60 mm from its centre. Determine direct shear flow, moment-induced shear flow and largest resulting throat stress. Locate a point at which the components add.

Reveal the worked solution

Direct flow is \(2000/(2\pi25)=12.73\,\mathrm{N/mm}\). With \(J_L=2\pi r^3\), moment flow is \(M/(2\pi r^2)=2000(60)/(2\pi25^2)=30.56\,\mathrm{N/mm}\). At the point where the moment-induced tangent has the same direction as the applied force, the maximum is \((12.73+30.56)/3=14.43\,\mathrm{MPa}\); at the diametrically opposite point the flow components oppose.

Link process drift to yield

A clearance has mean 0.18 mm and independent contributing standard deviations 0.030 and 0.040 mm. Assuming a normal distribution, find its standard deviation and estimated fraction below zero. One process drifts so the mean clearance falls to 0.12 mm. What is the new fraction?

Reveal the worked solution

The combined standard deviation is \(\sqrt{0.03^2+0.04^2}=0.050\,\mathrm{mm}\). Initially zero lies 3.6 standard deviations below the mean, giving probability about 0.000159 or 0.0159%. After the shift it is only 2.4 standard deviations below the mean, giving about 0.00820 or 0.820%. An unchanged process spread can conceal a large change in assembly yield.

A shared supply

Two independent pumps each have mission survival probability 0.95 conditional on an operating shared power supply. Either pump alone handles the full demand. The supply survives the mission with probability 0.98. Find system survival with one and with two pumps. What upper bound holds even with many such pumps?

Reveal the worked solution

With one pump the survival probability is \(0.98(0.95)=0.931\). With both, survival conditional on the supply is \(1-(1-0.95)^2=0.9975\), giving total \(0.98(0.9975)=0.97755\). Increasing the number of independent pumps can push their conditional survival towards unity, but the system probability remains bounded by the single supply’s 0.98.

Follow power through a belt and its supporting shaft

A pulley of pitch radius 0.10 m transmits 4.00 kW at 1200 rpm. Its belt has 180° wrap and coefficient of friction 0.30. Treat the two spans as parallel and the pulley as overhung 60 mm outside a bearing. Find the minimum pretension, approximate root bending moment, and von Mises stress in a solid shaft of diameter 25 mm. Neglect keyway concentration and shaft weight.

Worked solution

The shaft speed is 125.66 rad/s. The torque is 31.83 N m, requiring T₁−T₂ = 318.3 N. At impending slip T₁/T₂ = exp(0.30π) = 2.566, so T₂ ≈ 203.3 N and T₁ ≈ 521.6 N. The mean span tension is about 362 N. This is the minimum common pretension in the elementary equal-extension belt model.

The net radial pull in this parallel-span geometry is their sum, approximately 725 N. Its 0.060 m lever arm gives 43.5 N m bending moment. Taking the difference of tensions here would confuse the torque-producing tangential resultant about the pulley axis with the transverse force on the shaft.

At the surface the nominal bending stress 32M/(πd³) is 28.4 MPa and the torsional shear 16T/(πd³) is 10.4 MPa. The combined equivalent stress is √(σ²+3τ²) ≈ 33.6 MPa. A shaft rotating under a stationary transverse load experiences alternating bending even when this static equivalent value is well below yield. A fatigue decision requires endurance, surface finish and notch information.

Chapter 13 · Locating a part with six contacts and predicting datum error

A dimensionally accurate part can still be assembled in the wrong place. Use small rigid-body motions to work out what six contacts actually locate, then predict how dirt or a tilted guide appears in a measurement.

One frictionless contact supplies one scalar condition

Take the infinitesimal rigid motion of a body to consist of a translation \(\mathbf t\) and a rotation vector \(\boldsymbol\varphi\) in radians. A point initially at \(\mathbf r_i\) moves by \(\mathbf t+\boldsymbol\varphi\times\mathbf r_i\). A rigid locating pad at that point controls the component along its inward normal \(\mathbf n_i\). If its normal height error is \(e_i\), the displacement condition is

\[\mathbf n_i\cdot\mathbf t+(\mathbf r_i\times\mathbf n_i)\cdot\boldsymbol\varphi=e_i .\]

Stack the contact rows into a six-column matrix. Six independent rows locate a rigid part in three dimensions for small changes while the contacts remain seated. Three contacts in a plane support translation normal to the plane and two tilts. Two contacts on a second face locate one further translation and the remaining rotation. One on a third face sets the last translation. A preload must keep the unilateral contacts engaged. Six pads clustered along a single line would leave rotations undetermined despite the pad count.

Compute a tilted datum from three measured pad heights

Place the first three pads at coordinates \((0,0),(a,0),(0,b)\) on the nominal support plane. Normal displacement of a point \((x,y)\) is \(w=t_z+\varphi_x y-\varphi_y x\). Evaluate at the pads and subtract the origin row. The inverse relation gives

\[t_z=e_1,\]
\[\varphi_x=(e_3-e_1)/b,\]
\[\varphi_y=-(e_2-e_1)/a .\]

The predicted height of any feature is therefore an affine interpolation of the three heights. Outside their triangle it is an extrapolation and can magnify a small discrepancy. An additional hard pad away from the plane defined by the first three requires elastic deformation, a gap or loss of another contact. Its load sharing needs a stiffness model.

A particle under a pad and an encoder offset

Use \(a=120\) mm and \(b=80\) mm. A 12 μm particle raises only pad 2. Then \(\varphi_y=-100\) μrad and \(\varphi_x=t_z=0\). A feature 180 mm from the origin in the x direction rises by 18 μm. Doubling the spacing a halves this sensitivity while leaving the particle height unchanged. The feature error varies with position and cannot be removed at every point by a single vertical zero adjustment.

Consider a horizontal translation stage whose encoder sits a vertical distance \(d=75\) mm from a tool point. A pitch change \(\varphi_y=80\) μrad produces a longitudinal difference \(d\varphi_y=6.0\) μm between the two lines of action. This is the first-order Abbe error derived from the same rigid-body rotation law. Encoder scale calibration alone cannot distinguish this error from translation at a single feature. Compare the extrapolated feature trace with the heights inside the support footprint.

Fix one pad-height error and move pad 2 farther from the origin. Predict the shift halfway between the original pad positions versus the shift halfway between the new positions. Label the point whose physical location changes between those comparisons.

Tutorial · reconstruct an entire datum plane

Pad heights at the three locations are 3, −3 and 11 μm. Find the two tilts and the vertical shift of a feature at (60,40) mm. What will a second probe at (120,80) mm read relative to nominal?

Show the plane reconstruction

The differences are −6 μm over 120 mm and +8 μm over 80 mm, giving \(\varphi_y=+50\) μrad and \(\varphi_x=+100\) μrad. At the first feature the height is \(3-3+4=4\) μm. At the second it is \(3-6+8=5\) μm. The first point lies on the line joining pads 2 and 3, so its height is also their arithmetic mean. That geometric check detects a reversed rotation sign.

Chapter 14 · Sizing the leaves of a compliant translation guide

A pair of thin leaves can guide a carriage with very little sliding friction. Their stiffness, stress and parasitic motion stem from the same bent shape. Derive those links before choosing a thickness or stroke.

Enforce the root and platform boundary conditions

Consider two identical rectangular leaves connecting a fixed base to a rigid guided platform. Each leaf has length L, width b, thickness t and bending stiffness \(EI=Ebt^3/12\). Let the platform translate sideways by a small amount \(\delta\) without rotating. Ignore shear deformation, axial tension and clamp compliance in the linear bending solution. Along each unloaded span \(EIw''''=0\), so the transverse shape is a cubic polynomial. At x=0 displacement and slope vanish. At x=L impose \(w=\delta\) and \(w'=0\). With \(s=x/L\) the resulting shape and curvature are

\[w=\delta(3s^2-2s^3),\]
\[w''={6\delta\over L^2}(1-2s).\]

The curvature changes sign at midspan. Each leaf has opposite end moments and maximal bending strain at both ends. A root modeled as a free pin would remove an essential slope condition and give the wrong stiffness.

Find guide force, surface stress and secondary motion

The end shear magnitude is \(EI|w'''|=12EI|\delta|/L^3\) per leaf. Because both leaves impose the same displacement their forces add. At the outer fibre the strain magnitude is \((t/2)|w''|\). Hooke's law gives

\[F={24EI\over L^3}\delta,\]
\[|\sigma|_{\max}={3Et|\delta|\over L^2}.\]

For inextensible leaves free to shorten their projected span, the arclength exceeds the projection by approximately \(\tfrac12\int_0^L (w')^2 dx\). Substituting the cubic shape gives \(3\delta^2/(5L)\). This parasitic travel is second order and has the same direction for positive or negative displacement. Constraining that axial motion instead introduces an axial force and nonlinearity. At large stroke use an appropriate geometric model for both stress and stiffness.

A stiffness target can conflict with a stress target

Choose \(L=40\) mm, \(b=10\) mm, \(t=0.30\) mm and \(E=200\) GPa. For each leaf \(I=2.25\times10^{-14}\) m⁴. The two-leaf stiffness is 1688 N/m. At 1 mm stroke the required force is 1.69 N, peak stress is 112.5 MPa and the free projected shortening is 15 μm. This small axial travel can matter when the stage is used for optical alignment.

Doubling the leaf thickness to 0.60 mm multiplies stiffness by eight, so 13.5 N is needed at the same stroke. Peak stress doubles to 225 MPa because surface fibres are farther from the neutral axis. In contrast, doubling width doubles stiffness without changing surface stress at a specified displacement. At a specified force the comparison changes because the stiffer guide travels less. Always identify which quantity is prescribed before drawing a design conclusion.

Hold the requested stroke fixed and double the leaf thickness. Check the ratios of force, peak stress and shortening against their different powers of thickness. Then reduce stroke until the original peak stress is recovered.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial · select stroke and compare an end moment measurement

Return to the original guide and restrict nominal peak bending stress to 90 MPa. Find the corresponding stroke, force and end-moment magnitude in one leaf. Estimate the projected shortening at that stroke.

Show the displacement-limited design

Solving the stress expression gives \(\delta=0.80\) mm. Force is \(1687.5(0.0008)=1.35\) N. The end moment magnitude is \(EI(6\delta/L^2)=0.0135\) N m. The leaf end shear is 0.675 N; multiplying it by half the span gives the same end-moment magnitude. The predicted projected shortening is \(3(0.0008)^2/[5(0.040)]=9.6\) μm. Local clamp stress concentrations require the actual corner geometry.

Chapter 15 · Deriving screw-drive torque, efficiency and backdrivability

A screw trades angular travel for axial force. Unwrap one turn into an inclined plane and determine why two nominally similar threads can behave differently when the load tries to drive backward.

Convert one revolution into an inclined-plane problem

Let d be the mean thread diameter and l the lead, namely the axial advance per complete revolution. For a multiple-start thread the lead equals pitch times the number of starts. One turn around the mean diameter travels horizontally \(\pi d\) in the unwrapped view and rises l. The helix angle satisfies \(\tan\lambda=l/(\pi d)\). Use a square thread, steady motion, Coulomb friction with coefficient \(\mu\) and negligible collar and bearing losses. The circumferential effort P corresponds to applied torque \(T=Pd/2\).

Resolve the normal reaction before inserting friction

Let the axial load W act downward. For raising the load with a horizontal circumferential force P, the contact normal magnitude is \(N=W\cos\lambda+P\sin\lambda\). Equilibrium along the helix gives \(P\cos\lambda-W\sin\lambda=\mu N\). Separating P terms and dividing by \(\cos\lambda\) yields

\[T_\uparrow={Wd\over2}{\tan\lambda+\mu\over1-\mu\tan\lambda}={Wd\over2}\tan(\lambda+\phi),\]
\[\phi=\arctan\mu.\]

For steady lowering the friction reverses. Define positive lowering torque as external effort in the direction of descent. The same balance then gives \(T_\downarrow=(Wd/2)\tan(\phi-\lambda)\). Positive \(T_\downarrow\) means the load needs external assistance to lower. Negative \(T_\downarrow\) means a retaining torque is required to prevent acceleration under the idealized sliding law. The transition is \(\tan\lambda=\mu\). Static and sliding friction may differ in a physical test.

Compare useful lifting work with input work over one turn

For one turn \(Wl\) is useful work and \(2\pi T_\uparrow\) is supplied work. Therefore \(\eta=\tan\lambda/\tan(\lambda+\phi)\). At small lead and fixed friction, torque can be modest but many turns are needed. This distinction matters when specifying useful output speed. An additional constant collar torque simply adds to the torque budget and reduces measured efficiency.

For \(W=800\) N, \(d=16\) mm, \(l=4\) mm and \(\mu=0.12\), \(\tan\lambda=0.0796\). Raising torque is 1.29 N m and efficiency is 39.5%. Lowering requires 0.256 N m of external effort. At \(l=10\) mm with the other values held fixed, raising torque becomes 2.09 N m and efficiency reaches 60.9%. Lowering torque is now −0.494 N m. The faster lead crossed the backdriving threshold near \(l=\pi d\mu=6.03\) mm. The model assumes a square flank; a V-thread adds normal loading through its flank angle.

Locate the lead where lowering torque crosses zero. Double the axial load while keeping friction fixed and predict which feature of the plot should remain stationary. Then raise the friction coefficient and repeat the sign test.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial · identify friction from a torque measurement

The 4 mm lead screw lifts 800 N at a measured 1.50 N m thread torque after independently subtracting bearing torque. Estimate sliding friction. If the same mechanism has two thread starts of pitch 4 mm, would the inferred friction predict self-locking?

Show the inverse force balance

Write \(y=2T/(Wd)=0.2344\) and \(t=\tan\lambda=0.07958\). From \(y=(t+\mu)/(1-\mu t)\), obtain \(\mu=(y-t)/(1+yt)=0.1520\). Two starts give 8 mm lead and \(t=0.1592\), just above this value. Under the fitted sliding model the torque for lowering changes sign. Temperature, lubrication and static friction affect such a near-threshold result and can be distinguished by separate raising and lowering measurements.

Further reading · MIT 2.72 · lecture 11 on screw drives

Chapter 16 · Matching a gear ratio to load inertia and move time

A larger reduction increases output torque and reduces speed. During acceleration the motor must also spin its own rotor faster. Refer the dynamics to a single shaft and find which reduction gives the shortest idealized move.

Reflect inertia using kinetic energy

Let \(N=\omega_m/\omega_L\) be the reduction, with motor inertia \(J_m\), load inertia \(J_L\), and negligible gear inertia and losses. Kinetic energy is the sum of \(\tfrac12J_m\omega_m^2\) and \(\tfrac12J_L\omega_L^2\). Substituting \(\omega_L=\omega_m/N\) gives the load inertia referred to the motor as \(J_L/N^2\). Alternatively, express the energy in load coordinates and obtain \(J_L+N^2J_m\). Both descriptions predict the same acceleration if the torque is referred to the same coordinate.

\[(J_L+N^2J_m)\alpha_L=NT_m-T_L .\]

Here \(T_L\) is a positive resisting load torque. The factor multiplying motor torque follows independently from equality of power \(T_m\omega_m=(NT_m)\omega_L\) for an ideal gear. Combining reflected inertia from one side with torque from the other introduces an erroneous factor N.

Find the ratio for maximum acceleration at rest

First let \(T_L=0\) and let the motor deliver constant torque \(T_m\). Differentiating the load acceleration gives

\[{d\alpha_L\over dN}=T_m\,{J_L-N^2J_m\over(J_L+N^2J_m)^2},\]
\[N_*=\sqrt{J_L/J_m},\]
\[\alpha_*={T_m\over2\sqrt{J_LJ_m}} .\]

At the optimum the reflected load inertia equals the motor inertia. Provided the motor can reverse torque instantly, an ideal rest-to-rest move with symmetric acceleration and braking switches at half distance. For load travel \(\Delta\theta\), \(t_{\rm move}=2\sqrt{\Delta\theta/\alpha_L}\) and the peak load speed is \(\sqrt{\alpha_L\Delta\theta}\). A motor speed limit, speed-dependent torque or external load can shift the useful choice away from the inertia match.

Test a candidate against a motor speed limit

Take \(J_m=0.0002\) kg m², \(J_L=0.020\) kg m² and \(T_m=0.40\) N m. The inertia match is \(N=10\). Load acceleration is 100 rad/s². A 1 rad move takes 0.20 s in the ideal triangular speed profile and reaches 10 rad/s at the load, hence 100 rad/s at the motor. At \(N=5\) acceleration is 80 rad/s²; at \(N=20\) it is also 80 rad/s². The time penalty is the same for these two reciprocal changes around the optimum, but the motor peak speeds differ by a factor of four.

With \(N=10\) and a hard motor speed limit of 60 rad/s, the load can reach only 6 rad/s. Acceleration and deceleration each require 0.06 s and cover 0.18 rad. Traversing the remaining 0.64 rad at constant speed adds 0.107 s. The total is 0.227 s. An actuator selected purely on peak torque would miss this change in move profile.

Locate the inertia-only acceleration maximum and compare ratios equally far below and above it on a multiplicative scale. Add a resisting torque and decide which direction a sensible ratio change must take to recover acceleration.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial · account for a constant resisting torque

Let \(T_L=1.0\) N m during a positive accelerating stroke with the same motor and inertias. Compare acceleration at N=10 and 12, and derive the new maximizing ratio assuming constant torque at the motor.

Show the shifted maximum

At N=10 the net load torque is \(4-1=3\) N m and acceleration is 75 rad/s². At N=12 it is \(3.8/0.0488=77.9\) rad/s². Differentiating the loaded acceleration and cancelling constants gives \(N^2-2(T_L/T_m)N-J_L/J_m=0\). The positive root is \(2.5+\sqrt{6.25+100}=12.81\). During braking the external load can assist deceleration, so the symmetric switching rule used in the unforced example needs to be rederived for that operating condition.

Chapter 17 · Force transmission and compliance near a toggle position

Two links approach alignment as a mechanical fixture closes. A small applied force can correspond to a large output reaction, but the ratio also makes the result sensitive to clearance and compliance. Derive it from displacement compatibility.

Choose a coordinate that remains measurable

Join two equal links of length L at an upper knee and place their outer ends on a common horizontal line. Pin the left end to the frame and allow the right end to slide horizontally. The two links form a symmetric tent with common angle \(\theta\) above the base. The knee coordinates are \((L\cos\theta,L\sin\theta)\), and the slider position is \(x=2L\cos\theta\). A vertical driver presses on the knee while allowing its small horizontal motion. These kinematic conditions matter because a guide fixing the knee's horizontal coordinate would impose a different linkage.

\[y=L\sin\theta,\]
\[x=2L\cos\theta,\]
\[{dx\over dy}={-2L\sin\theta\over L\cos\theta}=-2\tan\theta .\]

A descending knee extends the horizontal span. Near alignment the horizontal motion becomes small relative to the vertical stroke. An expansion in small y gives \(x=2\sqrt{L^2-y^2}\simeq2L-y^2/L\). The position deficit is quadratic in knee height.

Derive force amplification and check equilibrium

Assume rigid links, ideal pins and slow movement. Let a downward driver force have magnitude P and let a horizontal resisting load at the slider have magnitude Q. For any compatible infinitesimal motion, zero net work gives \(P|dy|=Q|dx|\). Hence

\[{Q\over P}={1\over2\tan\theta} .\]

An independent force diagram at the knee shows that each compressed link carries \(C=P/(2\sin\theta)\). Its horizontal component at the slider is \(C\cos\theta\), giving the same ratio. As alignment approaches, the linearized kinematic ratio diverges. Finite stiffness, clearances, buckling of slender links and elastic deformation at the joints then determine the actual force for a prescribed driver travel. A geometric ratio alone cannot predict the force at exact alignment.

Distinguish a force-controlled drive from a compliant load

With \(L=100\) mm and an applied \(P=60\) N, an angle of 15° gives \(Q=112\) N and link compression 116 N. At 5° the same input would balance \(Q=343\) N and link compression 344 N. Meanwhile the remaining horizontal travel to full extension shrinks from \(2L(1-\cos15^\circ)=6.81\) mm to 0.761 mm. The final part of the stroke therefore has high force ratio and little remaining travel.

Now replace the prescribed resisting force with a horizontal spring of stiffness 100 N/mm, first contacted at an angle of 10°. At 5° the additional extension is \(2L(\cos5^\circ-\cos10^\circ)=2.28\) mm. The spring force is 228 N and the required input is only \(2Q\tan5^\circ=39.9\) N. The ideal force-controlled estimate of 343 N answers a different question because the actual load follows the spring and the platform displacement. Compare the full angle range before deciding where maximum driver effort occurs.

Locate the angle of first spring contact. At 5 degrees read the two different horizontal reactions. Change only the input force and explain why the displacement-controlled spring result stays put even though the force-controlled result changes.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial · recover an angle from two force readings

For a slow frictionless stroke a driver transducer reads 50 N and the horizontal reaction is 250 N. Estimate \(\theta\), link compression and residual travel for 100 mm links. What measurement could reveal pin friction independently?

Show the equilibrium and geometry

The observed ratio gives \(\tan\theta=50/(2\cdot250)=0.10\). Thus \(\theta=5.71^\circ\), \(C=250/\cos\theta=251.2\) N and residual travel is \(200(1-\cos\theta)=0.993\) mm. Compare the force needed during slow closing and opening at the same geometry and spring deflection. Ideally the elastic load gives the same relation both ways. Dry friction adds a direction-dependent component that changes that pair of readings.

Chapter 18 · How an involute gear pair keeps contact from one tooth to the next

The tooth-count ratio gives speed reduction but says little about how many pairs carry load simultaneously. Derive the transverse contact ratio from the base circles and addendum circles, then check how centre-distance errors affect overlap.

Unwrap base pitch from a taut string

An involute is the curve traced by the end of a taut string as it unwinds from a base circle. At each point the string is normal to the involute and tangent to the base circle. The common normal of conjugate external involute teeth is therefore the straight line tangent to both base circles, the line of action. Successive teeth reach equivalent points on this line one base pitch apart. For module m, tooth count z and reference pressure angle \(\alpha\), the reference pitch radius is \(r=mz/2\) and the base radius is \(r_b=r\cos\alpha\). One tooth pitch on the base circle is

\[p_b={2\pi r_b\over z}=\pi m\cos\alpha.\]

The operating centre distance a can differ from the sum of the reference pitch radii. The new operating pressure angle satisfies \(\cos\alpha_w=(r_{b1}+r_{b2})/a\). The two base circles and base pitch are unchanged because tooth geometry was cut before assembly. This explains how involute gears accommodate a moderate centre-distance shift while retaining their velocity ratio.

Compute the interval on the line of action

At first and last contact one of the addendum circles intersects the common normal. In the right triangle from its centre to a tangent-foot point on the base circle and the addendum intersection, the distance along the tangent is \(\sqrt{r_a^2-r_b^2}\). The tangent feet of both base circles are separated along the normal by \(a\sin\alpha_w\). Combining the two triangle lengths gives

\[\ell=\sqrt{r_{a1}^2-r_{b1}^2}+\sqrt{r_{a2}^2-r_{b2}^2}-a\sin\alpha_w,\]
\[\epsilon_\alpha={\ell\over p_b}.\]

For \(1<\epsilon_\alpha<2\), the fraction of a mesh cycle in nominal two-pair contact is \(\epsilon_\alpha-1\), and the remainder has one pair. This is a geometric sharing opportunity. Actual pair loads depend on tooth compliance, profile modifications and errors. The formula assumes the usable involute extends across the predicted path without tip interference or undercut.

Count the overlap and change centre distance

Take a spur pair with 20 and 40 teeth, module 2 mm, reference pressure angle 20°, and addendum equal to one module on both gears. The reference radii are 20 and 40 mm and addendum radii are 22 and 42 mm. At the nominal 60 mm spacing the base pitch is 5.904 mm and path length is 9.655 mm. The contact ratio is 1.635, so about 63.5% of each cycle can have two tooth pairs in contact. A 600 rpm pinion produces \(20(600/60)=200\) tooth engagements per second and a mesh period of 5.0 ms.

Increase centre distance by 0.50 mm without recutting either gear. The operating angle rises to 21.26° and the new ratio becomes 1.395. The two-pair interval falls to about 1.97 ms per mesh period from 3.18 ms. Gear ratio stays 2:1. By contrast, at nominal centres shortening both addenda to \(0.8m\) changes the tangent intersection lengths and gives ratio 1.347. Measure centre distance and tooth height independently before attributing an apparent overlap reduction to a single manufacturing error.

Increase the centre distance until the ratio is about 1.50. Mark that point before decreasing tooth addendum. Read the fraction of time with a single contacting pair and explain why tooth-count speed ratio stays fixed.

Tutorial · estimate the centre-distance tolerance for overlap

For the original standard addenda, find the largest centre distance compatible with contact ratio at least 1.50, leaving interference checks and manufacturing allowances outside this geometric estimate. Compare with a first-order sensitivity estimate at nominal centres.

Isolate the operating tangent length

Let \(S=\sqrt{r_{a1}^2-r_{b1}^2}+\sqrt{r_{a2}^2-r_{b2}^2}\). Since \(a\sin\alpha_w=\sqrt{a^2-(r_{b1}+r_{b2})^2}\), the limiting distance is \(\sqrt{(S-1.50p_b)^2+(r_{b1}+r_{b2})^2}\), about 60.28 mm. Differentiating gives \(d\epsilon_\alpha/da=-1/(p_b\sin\alpha_w)\), about −0.495 per mm at nominal spacing. A 0.135 loss predicts about 0.27 mm additional spacing, close because the tested change is small.

Chapter 19 · Why increasing the length of a bonded overlap gives diminishing returns

Two slender straps transfer a tensile load through adhesive shear. Average stress decreases in inverse proportion to overlap length, but edge stress can approach a finite plateau. Derive the difference by coupling the axial extension of each strap to its neighbour.

Convert adhesive distortion into a distributed force

Consider identical adherends of thickness t, width b and Young's modulus E. A thin adhesive of thickness \(t_a\) and shear modulus G connects them over a length L. A tensile force P enters through strap 1 at the left and leaves through strap 2 on the right. Let \(n_1,n_2\) be axial force per unit width, with \(n_1+n_2=N=P/b\). Exclude bending and peel to isolate the symmetric extensional load-transfer mechanism. This idealization is more credible for an aligned or symmetrically balanced joint than for an eccentric single-lap joint.

Choose coordinates so the transmitted shear \(\tau=-n_1'=n_2'\) is positive. Axial strains are \(u_i'=n_i/(Et)\). Taking adhesive shear in the load-transfer direction as \(\tau=(G/t_a)(u_2-u_1)\) gives \(\tau'=G(n_2-n_1)/(t_aEt)\). One more derivative eliminates the member forces:

\[\tau''-\lambda^2\tau=0,\]
\[\lambda=\sqrt{{2G\over t_aEt}},\]
\[\int_{-L/2}^{L/2}\tau\,dx=N .\]

The inverse transfer length \(1/\lambda\) is a mechanical length scale. A stiff adhesive transfers load over a shorter distance. A stiffer or thicker adherend distributes the required relative extension over a larger region. Reversing coordinate labels reverses force derivatives consistently without changing the predicted stress magnitude.

Apply symmetry and recover the edge-to-mean ratio

Identical adherends loaded at opposite ends give a shear profile symmetric about the middle. Set \(\tau=C\cosh(\lambda x)\) and choose C so its integral equals N. At the centre and edge this yields

\[\tau(x)={N\lambda\cosh(\lambda x)\over2\sinh(\lambda L/2)},\]
\[{\tau_{\rm edge}\over N/L}={\lambda L\over2}\coth{\lambda L\over2}.\]

For a very short overlap the ratio approaches one because \(\sinh y\sim y\). For a long overlap the edge stress approaches \(N\lambda/2\) even while \(N/L\) continues to decrease. The central portion then carries little shear. The model computes a smooth nominal adhesive stress; it does not resolve singular edge detail, peel or a local failure criterion.

Compare an average-based estimate with the load-transfer profile

Take \(P=1000\) N, \(b=20\) mm, \(E=70000\) MPa, \(t=2\) mm, \(t_a=0.20\) mm and \(G=300\) MPa. The force per width is 50 N/mm and \(\lambda=0.1464\) mm⁻¹, giving a transfer length of 6.83 mm. With \(L=20\) mm the mean stress is 2.50 MPa, but the edge sees 4.07 MPa and the centre only 1.79 MPa.

At \(L=40\) mm mean stress halves to 1.25 MPa. The edge stress falls only to 3.68 MPa and the centre drops to 0.393 MPa. The long-overlap limiting edge stress is 3.66 MPa. Thus using mean stress alone would suggest substantial unused room at the ends. Doubling the width at fixed P, in contrast, halves N and every stress in this one-dimensional model. Manufacturing constraints and adhesion quality can still change which failure mechanism occurs first.

Double overlap length while keeping adhesive thickness fixed. Compare the percentage fall in centre and edge stress. Then increase adhesive thickness and predict where the newly transferred load is distributed along the overlap.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial · tune load-transfer length using adhesive thickness

At the original E, t, G and force, a preliminary model calls for a long-overlap edge stress no greater than 3.0 MPa. Find the minimum adhesive thickness implied by that asymptote. Can a finite overlap at that exact thickness satisfy the same bound?

Invert the long-overlap limit

The limit requires \(\lambda\le 2(3.0)/50=0.120\) mm⁻¹. Thus \(t_a\ge2G/(Et\lambda^2)=0.298\) mm. For a finite length, \(\coth(\lambda L/2)>1\), so the edge stress at exactly that thickness stays slightly above 3.0 MPa. One must allow additional thickness, adjust the width, or evaluate a different load path. An adhesive change also alters deformation and peel, so this result is one design condition rather than a full joint qualification.
