Designing a displacement bridge at its noise limit

Electromechanics, optics and measurement

A moving electrode changes two capacitances in opposite directions. Derive the displacement signal, reconstruct its noise in metres and select a carrier voltage consistent with measured mechanical stiffness and electrical losses.

Find the voltage of the moving electrode before calculating sensitivity

Consider three plane electrodes with common active area. At the centred position the middle conductor is separated by \(g\) from each outer electrode and each gap has capacitance \(C_0\). Displacement \(x\) toward the positive drive electrode reduces one gap and increases the other. Use \(s=x/g\), where \(|s|<1\). Drive the outer electrodes with coherent sinusoidal voltages \(+V_d\) and \(-V_d\), with \(V_d\) the rms amplitude of either electrode relative to ground. The middle conductor leads to a high-impedance amplifier. Its cable and input provide a fixed total capacitance \(C_p\) to ground. Leakage is initially negligible over one carrier period; the input draws negligible signal current. All formulas for carrier voltages in this project use rms amplitudes.

\[C_+=\frac{C_0}{1-s},\]
\[C_-=\frac{C_0}{1+s}.\]

The middle node has zero net charge at the carrier frequency. Its instantaneous ac charge is \(C_+(V_n-V_d)+C_-(V_n+V_d)+C_p V_n\). Set the sum to zero and solve the resulting linear equation for \(V_n\). After combining the gap capacitances the result simplifies considerably:

\[\frac{V_n}{V_d}=\frac{C_+-C_-}{C_++C_-+C_p}=\frac{2C_0s}{2C_0+C_p(1-s^2)}.\]

For zero parasitic capacitance the response is exactly \(V_d x/g\) throughout the ideal geometry. Each individual capacitance is strongly curved near an electrode. Charge conservation divides one nonlinear combination by another and removes that curvature from their voltage ratio. Additional capacitance reduces the small-signal gain and introduces an odd cubic term. Let \(\eta=2C_0/(2C_0+C_p)\). Expanding about the centre gives \(V_n/V_d=\eta s[1+(1-\eta)s^2+\cdots]\). The derivative at zero is \(K=\eta V_d/g\) in volts per metre. A small-signal calibration taken only at the origin implicitly accepts this cubic distortion at larger displacements.

For \(C_0=2.00\) pF, \(C_p=4.00\) pF, \(g=100\,\mu\mathrm m\) and \(V_d=1.00\) V, \(K=5000\) V/m. A displacement of 100 nm produces \(0.5000\) mV. At 20 μm, the exact signal is \(0.10204\) V against the linear prediction \(0.10000\) V. The 2.04% curvature would be apparent in a calibration over the wider range. A single proportional calibration cannot remove it. A fit to the ratio itself has a physical parameter, \(C_p/(2C_0)\), which can also be checked by measuring total input capacitance with the mechanical element held at centre.

Translate each readout fluctuation into equivalent displacement

Represent the amplifier near the carrier by an input series voltage noise density \(e_n\) and a shunt current noise density \(i_n\). With the drives treated as small-signal grounds for a noise perturbation, the central node presents \(C_T=2C_0+C_p\) at the centre. The impedance magnitude for current fluctuations is \(1/(\omega C_T)\). For mutually independent noise sources and a narrow demodulation band the corresponding input-referred displacement amplitude spectral density is

\[S_{x,e}^{1/2}=\frac{1}{K}\sqrt{e_n^2+\left(\frac{i_n}{\omega C_T}\right)^2}.\]

Use noise densities and gain referred to the same detection channel. An rms sine amplitude, a peak-amplitude display and a lock-in reading with an unreported normalisation can differ by factors involving \(\sqrt2\). Here noise figures denote the effective one-sided densities referred to the recovered rms carrier channel. For a white spectrum and an equivalent noise bandwidth \(B_e\) the displacement standard deviation equals the density times \(\sqrt{B_e}\). The displayed cut-off frequency of a multi-pole lock-in filter need not equal its equivalent noise bandwidth. A calibration using a known injected sideband measures both the gain convention and the actual band response.

At 100 kHz, \(e_n=10\,\mathrm{nV/\sqrt{Hz}}\), \(i_n=10\,\mathrm{fA/\sqrt{Hz}}\) and \(C_T=8.00\) pF, current noise produces \(1.99\,\mathrm{nV/\sqrt{Hz}}\). Adding variances gives \(10.20\,\mathrm{nV/\sqrt{Hz}}\), or \(2.04\,\mathrm{pm/\sqrt{Hz}}\) at the stated gain. A 100 Hz equivalent noise bandwidth gives 20.4 pm rms of electrical readout noise. Cable capacitance reduces the voltage made by current noise but also reduces signal gain. In fact \(C_T K=2C_0 V_d/g\), so their ratio for current noise is independent of \(C_p\) in this model. The voltage-noise contribution grows with cable capacitance. Higher carrier frequency initially reduces the current contribution but reaches a plateau set by voltage noise.

Derive backaction for a floating sensing plate

Electrical drive can change the mechanical stiffness of the sensor. For each gap take half the squared rms gap voltage times its capacitance derivative. This follows either by averaging the instantaneous plate pressure over a carrier cycle or by differentiating electrical coenergy with the middle node at its charge-balanced voltage. Mechanical motion must be slow compared with the carrier. Since \(C_-'\) is negative, the two signed forces cancel at the centre. For a small positive \(s\), write \(V_n\simeq\eta V_ds\), \(C_+'\simeq(C_0/g)(1+2s)\), \(C_-'\simeq-(C_0/g)(1-2s)\). The leading imbalance is

\[F=\tfrac12 C_+'(V_d-V_n)^2+\tfrac12 C_-'(-V_d-V_n)^2\simeq\frac{2C_0 V_d^2}{g^2}(1-\eta)x .\]

The electromagnetic stiffness \(K_e=2C_0 V_d^2(1-\eta)/g^2\) subtracts from the mechanical restoring stiffness \(k\). If \(C_p=0\), the equal field magnitudes on both sides cancel exactly for every displacement in ideal plane geometry. To check directly, the positive gap field is \([V_d(1-s)]/[g(1-s)]=V_d/g\). The negative gap field has the same magnitude. A charge amplifier holding the central electrode at virtual ground would change this condition and have a different backaction; its calibration must use its own circuit. For a finite \(C_p\), the measured change of resonance with carrier voltage can identify electrical coupling independently of the signal voltage.

With \(k=0.020\) N/m the nominal configuration has \(K_e=0.00020\,\mathrm{N/m}\) at 1 V and \(0.00080\,\mathrm{N/m}\) at 2 V. At 2 V the resonance ratio for the same effective mass is \(\sqrt{1-0.0008/0.020}=0.980\). A 2% frequency decrease measures roughly a 4% reduction of stiffness. If the displacement is imposed by a very stiff external positioner this effect changes the force needed to hold position. If the sample is itself a force sensor, the same effect changes the mechanical conversion between applied force and displacement. Quoting an electronic noise floor without specifying which mechanical situation is intended can hide the larger uncertainty.

Choose a feasible drive and include Brownian motion

Raising the carrier at fixed frequency increases displacement signal. It may also heat the dielectrics, exceed amplifier headroom or significantly soften the spring. Suppose each gap at centre has an effective loss tangent \(\tan\delta\), with a real conductance \(\omega C_0\tan\delta\) at the carrier. Its dissipated power is conductance times the squared rms voltage. Add both gaps to find \(P=2\omega C_0\tan\delta V_d^2\). Fixed parasitic capacitance at the stationary middle node has negligible carrier voltage at exact balance in this model. An actual shield or cable dielectric connected directly to one of the drive nodes will have its own loss. A calorimetric or current phase measurement should include that extra load.

With effective \(\tan\delta=0.010\), the two active gaps dissipate 25.13 nW per squared volt at 100 kHz. A 100 nW heat budget gives \(V_d\le1.995\) V. A driver that remains linear to 2.50 V and a softening allowance \(K_e\le0.1k\) permit 2.50 V and 3.16 V respectively. The thermal bound sets the largest practical drive under these assumptions. For the electrical readout alone, the best feasible setting is thus 1.995 V and the electrical floor is about \(1.02\,\mathrm{pm/\sqrt{Hz}}\). A separate limit on the electric field or measurement amplitude could be introduced as another experimentally justified constraint.

Now let the mechanical element have viscous damping \(b=1.00\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{N\,s/m}\) at \(T=300\) K. In thermal equilibrium the one-sided Brownian force density is \(\sqrt{4k_BT b}=1.29\times10^{-13}\,\mathrm{N/\sqrt{Hz}}\). Far below mechanical resonance its displacement density is this force divided by \(k-K_e\). At 1 V it is \(6.50\,\mathrm{pm/\sqrt{Hz}}\), already greater than the electrical term. At 1.995 V it is about \(6.70\,\mathrm{pm/\sqrt{Hz}}\). The combined density adds in quadrature, giving about 6.81 at 1 V and 6.78 at the heat limit. A minimum of the total can occur between them. For an imposed position of a rigid stage the Brownian motion of this compliant probe may be outside the measurement model; for a freely suspended sensing mass it is part of the signal uncertainty. The optimisation criterion follows the physical quantity the instrument measures.

Use the phase and polarity of a calibration to track false signals

Middle-node leakage to ground with conductance \(G\) changes the circuit equation to \([G+i\omega C_T]V_n=i\omega(C_+-C_-)V_d\) at small displacement. The ideal gain acquires a factor \(i\omega C_T/(G+i\omega C_T)\). For a 10 MΩ leakage path and 8 pF, the corner is 1.99 kHz. At 100 kHz the magnitude reduction is about 0.02% and the phase lead about \(1.14^\circ\). At 2 kHz both quadratures matter. A drift in humidity that lowers the resistance can look like a gain change if only the in-phase channel is recorded. Measure a known displacement at two carrier frequencies and fit both quadratures before interpreting it as a change in geometry.

Suppose the two actual drives are \(V_d(1+a)\) and \(-V_d(1-a)\). At centre the output becomes \(\eta V_da\), equivalent in the small-signal calibration to displacement \(ag\). An amplitude imbalance of one part in ten thousand gives a 10 nm offset at \(g=100\,\mu\mathrm m\), many orders above a short-band noise floor expressed in picometres. Reverse the mechanical calibration displacement to isolate odd signal, measure the voltages at the electrode terminals and compare the nulled bridge under exchange of the drive channels. Reversing both carrier phases simply reverses both desired signal and this coherently driven offset; by itself it cannot distinguish them. An offset stable over a short scan can be estimated from two symmetric calibration displacements, whereas a drifting imbalance requires a reference or simultaneous drive measurement.

Find the minimum for a compliant probe instead of guessing at it

It is possible to optimise the free probe analytically up to a scalar root. Write \(y=V_d^2\), \(K_e=hy\), and \(a\) for the readout displacement density at unit drive multiplied by one volt. Let \(d_F=\sqrt{4k_BT b}\) be the Brownian force density. At displacement frequencies well below the resonance, the total variance density has an electrical term falling as \(1/y\) and a mechanical term that grows as the spring softens:

\[S_x(y)=\frac{a^2}{y}+\frac{d_F^2}{(k-hy)^2},\]
\[{dS_x\over dy}=-{a^2\over y^2}+{2h d_F^2\over(k-hy)^3}.\]

On the stable interval the first derivative strictly increases when \(h>0\). The first term starts at negative infinity and the second diverges at the stiffness boundary. Hence there is one unconstrained minimum. Solve a bracketed scalar equation for \(y\), then compare with each experimental upper bound in volts squared. At zero parasitic capacitance the ideal floating plate has \(h=0\); total noise then falls monotonically toward an asymptotic Brownian floor. A numerical routine that divides by \(h\) would fail precisely at this informative physical limit. In either case, a force-noise specification referred to the external sample can produce a different optimum because force conversion multiplies displacement noise by stiffness.

With the baseline values \(a=2.039\times10^{-12}\ \mathrm{m\,V/\sqrt{Hz}}\), \(h=2.00\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{N/(m\,V^2)}\), \(d_F\simeq1.287\times10^{-13}\ \mathrm{N/\sqrt{Hz}}\). Substitution brackets the stationary point between 1.45 and 1.50 V. At roughly 1.47 V the density is about \(6.72\,\mathrm{pm/\sqrt{Hz}}\), a shallow minimum compared with the heat-limited result. A tiny uncertainty in amplifier noise or in the support damping could shift the exact optimum appreciably while having very little effect on achievable resolution. Reporting a numerically optimised voltage to six decimals would imply a knowledge of the physical system absent from these inputs.

A direct bench experiment can test whether the excess really comes from compliance. Compare the demodulated spectrum with the centre plate tightly held and with it free. The electrical floor should be similar if the clamping keeps cable capacitance and carrier coupling fixed. The free probe should acquire a mechanical resonance which moves with \(V_d^2\), and the low-frequency component should track the inferred static stiffness. A large peak locked to carrier-drive amplitude modulation in both experiments suggests coherent feedthrough. Also check that the noise estimate comes from a frequency band above drift yet below resonance, and that the inferred amplitude density times the square root of the bandwidth predicts repeated stationary readings. These practical checks distinguish a readout-limited displacement estimate from a calibrated measurement of actual thermal motion.

Explore the usable voltage range of the bridge

The horizontal axis ends at the first of the heat, driver and mechanical limits. With 4 pF fixed parasitic capacitance at 100 kHz the heat ceiling is 1.995 V rms, while a freely suspended probe reaches its minimum combined noise near 1.47 V rms. Reduce the added capacitance to zero and explain why a finite optimum arising from mechanical softening disappears. Then lower carrier frequency. The current-noise term grows and dielectric heating at a given voltage falls. Observe which effect changes the allowed voltage range and which changes the height of the electrical noise curve.

Open the live experiment

Tutorial · separate electronic resolution from mechanical response

Find the frequency at which current and voltage noise contribute equally

Retain the baseline input capacitances and noise sources. Calculate the crossover frequency and the effect of doubling cable capacitance from 4 pF to 8 pF. What happens to input-referred current-noise displacement at fixed frequency?

Work through the solution

Set \(i_n/(2\pi f C_T)=e_n\). With \(C_T=8\) pF the crossover is \(19.9\) kHz. The longer cable gives \(C_T=12\) pF and a crossover at 13.3 kHz. At 100 kHz, the current noise at the input falls to \(1.33\,\mathrm{nV/\sqrt{Hz}}\) but gain falls from 5000 to 3333 V/m. The current contribution remains \(0.398\,\mathrm{pm/\sqrt{Hz}}\). The voltage contribution increases from 2.00 to 3.00 pm/√Hz. Both results follow independently by substituting \(K=2C_0V_d/(g C_T)\) into the noise formula and cancelling \(C_T\) in only one of the two terms.

Diagnose a frequency shift and a noise claim

At 2.00 V the resonance changes from 500.0 Hz to 490.0 Hz. Determine the implied stiffness reduction. A second experiment quotes 10 pm rms in a 100 Hz noise bandwidth for the freely suspended probe at 300 K. Can thermal equilibrium and the measured spring and damping account for this number?

Work through the solution

At unchanged moving mass \(k_{\rm eff}/k=(490/500)^2=0.9604\). The measured electrical stiffness is \(0.000792\) N/m, consistent with the 0.00080 N/m prediction. At frequencies well below resonance the Brownian density is approximately 6.70 pm/√Hz, and a flat 100 Hz equivalent band would contain approximately 67 pm rms of thermal motion alone. Ten picometres rms would correspond to about 2.2 Hz equivalent noise bandwidth at this density, or require a changed mechanical condition, frequency band or calibration. To discriminate experimentally, measure the mechanical resonance spectrum with a known input-force calibration and integrate the noise with the actual filter transfer. Include mechanical susceptibility over the band if the filter extends close to resonance.

Further reading

  • Maxwell stress and electrical coenergy in mechanical force balances
  • MIT · Woodson and Melcher, lumped-parameter electromechanics, chapter 3

Eddy-current braking, magnetic penetration and the heat left behind

Electromechanics, optics and measurement

A conducting loop moves above a row of magnetic poles. The drag first increases with speed, but a rapid crossing need not generate proportionally greater braking. Trace the work done against the Lorentz force into circuit energy, heat and a measurable temperature change. Then decide which features of a solid plate the single-loop model can capture.

Generate a voltage from a spatially periodic field

Let a rectangular loop of height \(h\) along \(y\) and width \(w\) along \(x\) translate to the right above a stationary magnetic pattern. At the loop let the normal induction be \(B_z(x)=B_0\cos(kx)\), with \(k=2\pi/\lambda\). Its two active sides are centred symmetrically about the coordinate \(x_c\). Assume the field varies negligibly along \(y\) over this height. Integrating through the loop gives \(\Phi=h\int_{x_c-w/2}^{x_c+w/2}B_0\cos(kx)\,dx=[2hB_0\sin(kw/2)/k]\cos(kx_c)\). A uniform translation \(x_c=ut\) therefore produces an external emf with peak amplitude \(E_{\rm pk}=K u\), where \(K=2hB_0|\sin(kw/2)|\).

This spatial averaging explains an initially surprising design feature. A loop spanning precisely one full spatial period always links zero net flux from the sinusoidal field and therefore generates no emf in this ideal geometry. A loop of half a period has maximum coupling at a specified height. If \(w\) is much smaller than the period, \(\sin(kw/2)\simeq kw/2\) and \(K\simeq h w B_0 k\). This is exactly the small-loop result from using its area times the local gradient of induction. Reversing velocity reverses the emf at a fixed position. At a fixed positive speed the temporal frequency is \(f=u/\lambda\), so the same conductor experiences different electrical frequencies at different mechanical speeds.

Include finite inductance and derive the drag curve

Model the current path by a constant resistance \(R\) and self-inductance \(L\). Its steady periodic current solves \(L\dot i+Ri=\mathcal E(t)\). In phasor notation its magnitude is \(E_{\rm pk}/\sqrt{R^2+(\omega L)^2}\), lagging the emf by \(\arctan(\omega L/R)\), where \(\omega=ku\). The current develops field energy \(Li^2/2\) and dissipates \(Ri^2\). Multiplying the circuit equation by \(i\) and averaging over a period eliminates the derivative of stored energy. The average mechanical power delivered against the drag is therefore the mean Joule heating \(P=E_{\rm pk}^2R/[2(R^2+\omega^2L^2)]\). Dividing by the speed gives the drag magnitude

\[F(u)=\frac{K^2u}{2R[1+(u/u_c)^2]},\]
\[u_c=\frac{R}{kL}.\]

At low speed the drag is proportional to velocity. At the crossover \(u=u_c\) it reaches \(K^2u_c/(4R)\), and at much larger speeds it falls approximately as \(1/u\) within the assumed lumped model. The incoming mechanical power then tends to a constant rather than growing without bound. Why is there a maximum? Increasing speed raises the emf, but it also raises the reversal frequency. An inductive current increasingly lags the driving emf, and its component in phase with the emf does the net dissipative work. A measured turnover therefore tests both an electrical time scale and the spatial scale of the magnet pattern.

Use \(h=50\,{\rm mm}\), \(w=25\,{\rm mm}\), \(\lambda=0.100\,{\rm m}\), \(B_0=0.20\,{\rm T}\), \(R=0.050\,\Omega\) and \(L=0.150\,{\rm mH}\). Then \(K=0.01414\,{\rm T\,m}\), \(u_c=5.31\,{\rm m/s}\), and maximum mean drag is \(5.31\,{\rm mN}\). At \(u=2.0\,{\rm m/s}\) the field alternates at \(20\,{\rm Hz}\), giving an emf of \(28.3\,{\rm mV}\) peak. The current is \(0.529\,{\rm A}\) peak and its phase lag \(20.7^\circ\). The resistor receives approximately \(7.00\,{\rm mW}\), and the motor maintaining translation supplies a mean force of \(3.50\,{\rm mN}\). Using peak current in \(I^2R\) without its factor one half would overstate the mechanical force by a factor two.

Determine whether the current penetrates a real plate

A continuous metal plate contains many possible current paths, unlike a preformed loop. Their resistances, inductances and mutual couplings depend on the plate boundary, magnet geometry and frequency. For a good homogeneous conductor the magnetic diffusion equation gives a skin depth \(\delta=\sqrt{2/(\mu\sigma\omega)}\) under sinusoidal excitation. At a depth \(\delta\) a field entering a locally planar half-space has lost a factor \(e\) in amplitude and acquired a phase delay of one radian. The frequency to use is the frequency experienced by the moving material; the fact that the magnets are stationary in the laboratory does not make this frequency zero.

For copper with \(\sigma=5.8\times10^7\,{\rm S/m}\) and permeability close to \(\mu_0\), the skin depth is about \(14.8\,{\rm mm}\) at \(20\,{\rm Hz}\) and \(1.48\,{\rm mm}\) at \(2.0\,{\rm kHz}\). A \(2.0\,{\rm mm}\) plate therefore sees little through-thickness attenuation in the first experiment but may need a diffusion calculation in the second. The plate's lateral current pattern can still matter when the through-thickness current density is nearly uniform. Slots interrupt wide current loops and usually reduce drag even when the mass and the bulk conductivity change little. Comparing a slotted and an unslotted plate directly tests the importance of current closure.

If a plate experiences sharply separated poles, its applied field in the moving frame contains a fundamental and higher harmonics. Harmonic \(n\) has a skin depth smaller by \(\sqrt n\), so checking only the fundamental can miss surface concentration associated with narrow features. A Hall scan along the track supplies the actual spatial field spectrum. Multiplying each spatial wavenumber by the speed converts it into a temporal angular frequency for a constant-speed pass. Once the speed changes significantly over one pole spacing, a time-domain calculation using the measured position trace is preferable to this fixed-frequency conversion.

Close a mechanical and thermal energy budget

Take a rotating brake as a second experiment. A flywheel of known inertia \(J_{\rm rot}=0.020\,{\rm kg\,m^2}\) slows from \(120\) to \(80\,{\rm rad/s}\) while no drive supplies power. The released rotational kinetic energy is \(J_{\rm rot}(120^2-80^2)/2=80.0\,{\rm J}\). A separate run with the magnet removed measures bearing and air losses. Suppose the corresponding speed-dependent losses integrated over the magnetic braking trajectory total \(8.0\,{\rm J}\). Then the electromagnetic channel receives \(72.0\,{\rm J}\). Using the reference run's total energy over an arbitrary equal time could give a different answer, since the two speed histories affect the parasitic losses.

A calorimeter enclosing the conducting rotor has effective heat capacity \(C=60\,{\rm J/K}\) and a thermal link of conductance \(G=0.12\,{\rm W/K}\) to an environment at constant temperature \(T_a\). Its balance is \(C\,dT/dt=P_{\rm deposited}-G(T-T_a)\). During a \(20\,{\rm s}\) run, suppose the time-averaged rise above ambient is \(0.50\,{\rm K}\). Then \(1.20\,{\rm J}\) flowed through the thermal link. If all electromagnetic heat entered the calorimeter, its final rise from an ambient start is \((72.0-1.20)/60=1.18\,{\rm K}\). A measured rise near \(0.80\,{\rm K}\) would leave an energy discrepancy of approximately \(23\,{\rm J}\). Check conduction into a shaft, heat stored outside the instrumented volume, and the estimate of parasitic torque before adjusting the electromagnetic theory.

Find the spatial features that dominate a measured braking signal

A single Fourier component of field already produces an informative turnover. In a tracked experiment the field often has a measurable third harmonic. Let \(B(x)=B_1\cos kx+B_3\cos(3kx+\varphi)\), with constant translation speed. Flux integration across the finite loop multiplies each harmonic by its own width factor. The corresponding emf peak is \(2h|B_n|u|\sin(nkw/2)|\), and its temporal angular frequency is \(nku\). The linear \(RL\) equation treats the two drives independently. Orthogonality over a complete fundamental period makes the mean of their product vanish, so total mean heat can be found by adding the individual resistive powers. Within one period the interference terms do affect instantaneous torque and power.

Suppose \(B_3/B_1=0.20\) and keep the preceding \(w=\lambda/4\). Here \(\sin(kw/2)\) and \(\sin(3kw/2)\) both have magnitude \(1/\sqrt2\). At low speed the third harmonic therefore adds about four percent to mean drag. At \(u=u_c\), however, its angular frequency sees inductive impedance \(3R\) while the fundamental sees \(R\). The third-harmonic share relative to the fundamental becomes \(0.20^2(1+1)/(1+9)=0.008\), only \(0.8\%\). A peak-to-peak field reading can change visibly without a comparable change in mean drag. Conversely, if the loop width happens to null one spatial harmonic, another smaller harmonic can supply most of the signal. A field map and the loop geometry together determine what source drives the circuit.

In a solid plate, predicting force from field and speed needs a field solution that enforces current closure. In the slowly moving conductor, \(\mathbf J=\sigma[-\nabla\phi+\mathbf u\times\mathbf B]\) when the imposed magnetic pattern is effectively time independent in the laboratory and the induced magnetic field can be neglected. Charge conservation then gives \(\nabla\cdot[\sigma(-\nabla\phi+\mathbf u\times\mathbf B)]=0\). On an electrically insulated plate edge, enforce \(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf J=0\). Solving this potential problem accounts for charge accumulation that redirects currents at a boundary. Simply setting \(\mathbf J=\sigma\mathbf u\times\mathbf B\) can predict current flowing straight out of an insulating edge. The force follows from integrating \(\mathbf J\times\mathbf B\) through the conducting volume, and Joule heat follows from \(\int J^2/\sigma\,dV\).

A useful check on such a field calculation is a finite plate translating wholly inside an exactly uniform static magnetic field. Charges can rearrange until a uniform electrostatic field cancels \(\mathbf u\times\mathbf B\). An isolated plate in this steady limit then has zero transport current and zero drag. Motion alone does not guarantee braking. At an entry edge, the moving conductor spans a spatial change of induction, and one scalar potential generally cannot cancel the motional drive around every closed path. Slits, thickness variations, a conducting contact at a boundary, and the induced field at higher speeds each change this test problem in an identifiable way.

Interpret a thermal transient without assuming it is instantaneous

If the brake runs at a stable speed and equilibrium electrical conditions, a local single-temperature model sees a nearly constant power. Its step response from ambient is \(\Delta T=(P/G)[1-\exp(-Gt/C)]\). Removing the drive gives an exponential decay with the same time constant \(C/G\). Measuring that cooling tail fixes the ratio of heat capacity to conductance but does not set either one separately. A known heater power or an independent heat-capacity measurement supplies the missing absolute scale. A slope evaluated over a long interval without correcting leakage will underestimate the input power as the sample warms.

Take a small bench calorimeter enclosing the single test loop with \(C=1.20\,{\rm J/K}\) and \(G=0.020\,{\rm W/K}\). The \(7.00\,{\rm mW}\) low-speed run gives a \(0.350\,{\rm K}\) eventual rise and a \(60\,{\rm s}\) time constant. After \(30\,{\rm s}\) the rise is about \(0.138\,{\rm K}\), whereas a thermally isolated calculation would give \(0.175\,{\rm K}\). Its stored heat \(0.166\,{\rm J}\) is less than the \(0.210\,{\rm J}\) mechanical work over that interval; the thermal link accounts for the remainder. Loading the conductor thermally with an additional copper holder increases the response time and changes the initial slope without changing the final rise if conductance to the bath remains the same.

Compare predictions at a chosen travel speed

The live graph displays the force predicted by the finite-inductance loop alongside the resistive low-frequency extrapolation. Choose a speed, calculate the apparent excitation frequency, and compare the returned skin depth to the actual conductor thickness before interpreting the prediction as a plate measurement. Try choosing an inductance twice as large. The location of maximum force halves in speed, but the low-speed slope at fixed geometry and resistance is unchanged. A real plate whose high-speed drag does not follow the single-loop curve can still agree with the model on its low-speed slope. Plotting the measured residuals against speed can reveal the point where extra current paths or through-thickness diffusion become important.

Open the live experiment

A plate and holder cool from \(0.240\,{\rm K}\) above ambient to \(0.120\,{\rm K}\) in \(42\,{\rm s}\) after a run. Can these two readings establish the braking power?

Work through the solution

The half-life gives \(C/G=42/\ln2=60.6\,{\rm s}\), which agrees approximately with the small calorimeter example. It determines only a ratio. If an independent steady \(10.0\,{\rm mW}\) heater then produces a \(0.500\,{\rm K}\) temperature rise, \(G=0.0200\,{\rm W/K}\) and \(C=1.21\,{\rm J/K}\). A magnetic run that settles at \(0.350\,{\rm K}\) now corresponds to \(7.00\,{\rm mW}\), provided the thermal boundary and deposition pattern are sufficiently similar to the calibration.

Check the sign of instantaneous electromagnetic work

At a phase where the applied emf has just crossed zero, a finite-inductance loop may still carry considerable current. During part of a cycle the emf and current can have opposite signs. The product \(\mathcal E i\) is then negative even though \(Ri^2\) remains positive. The difference is a negative derivative of the magnetic store \(Li^2/2\). The loop briefly returns energy mechanically while its stored field decreases, and some of that release also becomes heat. A force transducer resolving separate pole passages can therefore see instantaneous variations that a mean braking-force estimate suppresses.

For the numerical \(2.0\,{\rm m/s}\) case the peak field energy is \(L I_{\rm pk}^2/2\approx21.0\,\mu{\rm J}\). The dissipated energy per full pole period is \(7.00\,{\rm mW}/20\,{\rm Hz}=350\,\mu{\rm J}\). These quantities serve different purposes. The first is a reversible instantaneous store; the second is net irreversible energy delivered each time the conductor moves one spatial period. Dividing the second by the spatial period of \(0.100\,{\rm m}\) recovers the mean drag of \(3.50\,{\rm mN}\) without using the translational speed explicitly.

Tutorial on braking measurements

At \(u=2.0\,{\rm m/s}\) the loop above experiences a mean drag of \(3.50\,{\rm mN}\). Predict what halving the induction and doubling the speed does, keeping its geometry and electrical properties fixed.

Work through the solution

Halving induction quarters \(K^2\). The speed factor contributes \(2[1+(2/5.31)^2]/[1+(4/5.31)^2]\approx1.46\). The combined change gives a drag about \(0.364\) times the original, or \(1.27\,{\rm mN}\). The mechanical power decreases less because speed has doubled. A linear-speed extrapolation would miss the inductive phase lag, already significant at \(4\,{\rm m/s}\).

In a repeated flywheel test the brake becomes warmer with every run. Which electrical parameter change can explain a change of drag even with the magnets held fixed?

Work through the solution

The resistance of an ordinary metal generally increases with temperature. At a fixed speed \(F\) is proportional to \(R/[R^2+(\omega L)^2]\). Raising resistance reduces drag on the low-frequency resistive side and can increase it on the high-frequency inductive side. The maximum occurs at \(R=\omega L\) if electrical geometry and inductance stay fixed. Measuring the temperature and resistance before each run helps distinguish this effect from a weakening field or a changed gap.

Compare a step-driven diffusion profile with sinusoidal skin penetration.

Applied study

Determine how load and curvature set the contact area, pressure and stresses between components.

How much area really carries the load?

A ball presses against a bearing race. The unloaded bodies touch at a point, which would imply infinite pressure for any finite force. Both bodies deform elastically under load, opening a contact patch whose size is part of the solution. A pin in a clevis carries a transverse load through a different combination of contact and shear. The relevant area depends on which failure mechanism is being examined.

Start with a sphere of radius \(R\) on a flat body. Assume a smooth nonadhesive interface, negligible tangential traction, isotropic linear elasticity and a small contact patch relative to curvature and body dimensions. Hertz’s local solution then treats each solid as an elastic half-space. Their normal compliances add through the effective modulus \(1/E^*=(1-\nu_1^2)/E_1+(1-\nu_2^2)/E_2\). A grooved race with appreciable curvature can require different principal radii and an elliptical patch.

From contact pressure to the normal force

In a circular contact of radius \(a\), the elastic solution gives \(p(r)=p_0\sqrt{1-r^2/a^2}\), with the greatest pressure at the centre. Equilibrium of a loaded body connects this local field to a measurable force. Integrating over rings of area \(2\pi r\,dr\) gives \(F=2\pi p_0a^2/3\), so the peak pressure is 1.5 times the patch mean.

Matching the curved gap to the elastic deflections gives \(a^2=R\delta\), with \(\delta\) the mutual approach of the remote bodies, and \(F=(4/3)E^*\sqrt{R}\,\delta^{3/2}\). Eliminating the approach yields

\[a=\left(\frac{3FR}{4E^*}\right)^{1/3},\qquad p_0=\frac{3F}{2\pi a^2},\qquad k_n=\frac{dF}{d\delta}=2E^*a.\]

The tangent stiffness \(k_n\) increases as load enlarges the patch. A single constant contact stiffness can describe small oscillations around a chosen preload. Following the surface during rolling subjects material below the track to a changing stress state. For Poisson ratio 0.30 the elastic solution puts the largest shear stress on the central axis near \(z=0.48a\), with magnitude about \(0.31p_0\).

Worked example · Steel ball on a steel seat

Let \(R=10.0\) mm, \(F=120\) N and \(E=210\) GPa, \(\nu=0.30\) for both bodies. Their combined modulus is \(210/[2(1-0.3^2)]=115.4\) GPa. The patch radius is 0.198 mm and the approach is \(a^2/R=3.93\,\mu\mathrm{m}\). The pressure averaged over the patch is 971 MPa, and \(p_0=1.46\) GPa. The maximum shear is about 452 MPa near a depth of 0.095 mm.

The 120 N force alone is a poor guide to stress at the contact. These are elastic predictions. Judging service life also requires material condition, lubrication, repeated loading and the rest of the bearing geometry. Compare how force and geometry determine nominal stress in a beam.

Transfer problem · A pin with two shear planes

A 12 kN load passes through a central 6 mm thick lug between two 4 mm thick clevis plates. Initially the pin diameter is 8 mm. Assume symmetric load sharing and negligible pin bending for this preliminary check. A cut through both shear planes gives mean pin stress \(\tau=F/[2(\pi d^2/4)]\). Nominal bearing pressure uses the projected pin area \(td\) in each plate, with the central lug carrying \(F\) and the outer plates \(F/2\) each.

For the 8 mm pin the mean shear is 119 MPa. Nominal bearing pressure is \(12000/(6\times8)=250\) MPa in the central lug, with dimensions here in millimetres, and \(6000/(4\times8)=188\) MPa in each outer plate. Suppose the exercise specifies limits of 125 MPa in mean shear and 200 MPa in nominal bearing. The central lug controls the diameter. Increasing \(d\) to 10 mm gives 76.4 MPa shear, 200 MPa in the central lug and 150 MPa outside. This meets the two stated nominal checks at the limiting diameter. Edge tear-out, net-section failure and pin bending would require additional dimensions and material data.

Exercises

1. Increase the contact preload

Increase the sphere load eightfold, maintaining geometry and elastic properties. Predict the factors of change in contact radius, peak pressure, approach and incremental stiffness. Calculate the new approach.

Show the load scalings

From \(a\propto F^{1/3}\), the radius doubles. The patch area quadruples, so \(p_0\propto F/a^2\) also doubles. The approach rises fourfold to about 15.7 µm, and the tangent stiffness doubles. A load path based on a geometrically fixed patch would overestimate the pressure increase.

2. Widen the contact or change the material?

At the original 120 N, compare doubling the sphere radius and halving the reduced modulus. Which choice reduces the peak pressure more, and which produces less movement?

Show the design comparison

Both changes double \(R/E^*\), increasing \(a\) by \(2^{1/3}=1.260\) and reducing \(p_0\) to \(2^{-2/3}=0.630\) times its former value. Since \(\delta=a^2/R\), doubling \(R\) gives \(2^{-1/3}\delta=3.12\) µm, whereas halving \(E^*\) gives \(2^{2/3}\delta=6.24\) µm. The larger sphere gives the same pressure reduction with a stiffer response at fixed load.

OneSimulation laboratory

Test the contact law under imposed approach

Compare recorded OneSimulation runs on this page. To test your own inputs, download the notebook and model files and run the model in an activated OneSimulation environment.

The example brings a 10 mm sphere towards a finite block. It prescribes the approach and measures the resulting force. Use the first mesh labelled 7 cells per reference radius for the comparisons below. Its baseline has youngs_modulus=210e9, poisson_ratio=0.30 and a final approach of 25 µm. The computed contact radius comes from a pressure-weighted moment of the contact patch. A force integral over the quarter model is multiplied by four in the supplied script.

  1. Multiply the array approaches by 0.5, keeping material properties and mesh unchanged. The final approach is now 12.5 µm. Relative to baseline the half-space prediction gives a radius factor \(\sqrt{0.5}=0.707\) and a force factor \(0.5^{3/2}=0.354\). Reuse the same mesh to see how closely this finite-body calculation follows these ratios.
  2. Restore the original approach and set youngs_modulus=105e9 Pa for both bodies. Under displacement control the elastic displacement pattern and patch should stay nearly fixed while the reaction and stress halve. At fixed force instead, the Hertz radius varies as \((E^*)^{-1/3}\) at fixed geometry. Which control condition describes the numerical run?

To read the first mesh directly, run the cells through “Contact patch and pressure” and evaluate the following line. For a shorter run you can set levels=(7,) in the mesh setup and stop at that cell.

print(forces[0][-1], 1000 * contact_radius[0])  # newtons and millimetres
